  findings: The study finds that in a spatial public goods game with tax-supported punishment, the prevalence of cooperation, defection, and punishment strategies depends sensitively on the synergy factor, tax level, punishment cost, and fine. When the tax exceeds the cost of punishment, punishing cooperators can dominate. At low tax and high punishment cost, defectors dominate. Intermediate regimes can produce coexistence of multiple strategies, including cyclic dominance. The spatial structure allows for richer dynamics than well-mixed models, including new three-strategy coexistence states. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about how the prevalence of cooperation and punishment strategies depends on game design dimensions such as player group size, number of strategies, spatial structure, tax level, punishment cost, and fine. However, because it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is useful for understanding which parameter regimes are likely to support cooperation or complex coexistence, which may indirectly inform efficiency predictions if one assumes that higher cooperation rates correspond to higher efficiency, but this link is not established or quantified in the paper.

- source: Hua, S. J., & Liu, L. J. (2023). Facilitating the evolution of cooperation through altruistic punishment with adaptive feedback. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing adaptive feedback in punishment intensity—where punishment increases as defection rises and decreases as cooperation rises—can facilitate the evolution and maintenance of cooperation in public goods games. The system can exhibit bistability (converging to either full cooperation with low punishment or full defection with high punishment), stable oscillations between cooperation and defection, or stable full cooperation/defection depending on the average per capita incentive and other parameters. Notably, full cooperation can be achieved and maintained with only low-intensity punishment under certain conditions. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and numerical simulations of the replicator dynamics with feedback, not on empirical or experimental data. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so implications for efficiency must be inferred indirectly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that adaptive, feedback-based punishment can promote and stabilize cooperation in public goods games, sometimes with only low punishment intensity. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the results suggest that under certain parameter regimes, adaptive punishment could lead to high efficiency (if cooperation is high and punishment costs are low). However, the absence of direct efficiency analysis means that predictions about efficiency effects must be made cautiously and indirectly, based on the reported cooperation dynamics. The model is most relevant for settings with institutional punishment, adaptive feedback, and infinite well-mixed populations, and less directly applicable to finite, experimental, or empirical settings.

- source: Xie, K., Liu, X. W., Wang, H. Z., & Jiang, Y. L. (2023). Multi-heterogeneity public goods evolutionary game on lattice. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a modified pool punishment mechanism and aspiration-driven updating in a multi-heterogeneity public goods game on a lattice can promote cooperation under certain conditions. Specifically, moderate punishment and low aspiration factor are most effective at increasing the fraction of cooperators. Too high punishment or aspiration factor leads to instability or loss of cooperation. The results are robust to different lattice sizes and initial conditions. While average payoffs for cooperators and defectors are reported, the main focus is on cooperation rates, not group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in public goods games with multiple heterogeneities, but only under moderate punishment and low aspiration factor. However, because the main outcome is cooperation rate and not group efficiency or total payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency. The results suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency may be non-monotonic: moderate punishment may improve outcomes, but excessive punishment can reduce both cooperation and payoffs. The lattice structure and aspiration-driven updating are important contextual factors.

- source: Han, D., Yan, S. T., & Li, D. D. (2019). The evolutionary public goods game model with punishment mechanism in an activity-driven network. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that increasing the maximal fine of punishment has a greater impact on defectors than on cooperators, but as the fine increases, the final density of cooperators decreases. The maximal cost of punishment mainly affects the prevalence of punishing strategies, with higher costs reducing the number of punishing cooperators. The initial density of cooperators has a counterintuitive effect: a higher initial density leads to a lower final density of cooperators, and vice versa. The results are reported in terms of the densities of strategy types, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. Its findings are limited to the prevalence of cooperation and punishment strategies under different parameter settings. While the model structure and parameterization (e.g., punishment cost, fine, network dynamics) are informative for understanding mechanisms, the lack of efficiency or payoff outcomes means this paper should not be used as a primary source for predicting efficiency effects of punishment. It may be useful for understanding how punishment parameters affect cooperation rates, but not for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Zhang, S. H., Zhang, Z. P., Wu, Y. E., Yan, M., & Xie, Y. Y. (2018). Tolerance-based punishment and cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that tolerance-based punishment, where cooperators only punish defectors after a threshold of tolerated defections, can significantly promote cooperation in spatial public goods games. The mechanism reduces the frequency of punishment and allows for higher levels of cooperation under adverse conditions. The effects of punishment fine (\alpha) and tolerance threshold (M) are systematically explored, showing that higher fines and lower tolerance thresholds promote cooperation. However, the main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment mechanisms with a tolerance threshold can increase cooperation rates in spatial public goods games. However, because the main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment. The results suggest that, under similar spatial and parameter conditions, enabling punishment (especially with moderate tolerance) is likely to increase cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency if the cost of punishment is not too high. However, the paper does not quantify efficiency or net group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency outcomes is not possible from this study alone.

- source: Quan, J., Cui, S. H., Chen, W. M., & Wang, X. J. (2023). Reputation-based probabilistic punishment on the evolution of cooperation in the spatial public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing reputation-based probabilistic punishment into the spatial public goods game increases the frequency of cooperation and average payoffs. Higher reputation thresholds (meaning more defectors are likely to be punished), higher punishment intensity (lower b), and higher punishment fines (beta) all lead to higher cooperation and higher average payoffs. The model shows that cooperative and punishing strategies form spatial clusters with higher reputations and higher payoffs, while defectors cluster in low-reputation, low-payoff regions. The effect on efficiency is inferred from the increase in average payoff and the reduction of defectors, but efficiency as a normalized group payoff is not directly reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong qualitative support that, in spatial public goods games, enabling punishment (especially when it is reputation-based and probabilistic) increases cooperation and average payoffs compared to control conditions without punishment. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of punishment (with higher intensity, lower cost, or higher fines) should be expected to increase efficiency, especially when the punishment mechanism targets low-reputation defectors. However, because the paper does not report efficiency as a normalized group payoff, quantitative predictions should be made with caution and may require mapping from average payoff or cooperation rate. The results are most applicable to spatial, agent-based settings with reputation dynamics and probabilistic punishment.

- source: Xiao, J. F., Liu, L. J., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2023). Evolution of cooperation driven by sampling punishment. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that sampling punishment can dramatically increase the average cooperation level in both linear (PGG) and nonlinear (CRD) social dilemmas, provided the punishment intensity is above a threshold. Low punishment thresholds and small sample sizes are optimal, as they maximize cooperation and minimize punishment cost. The effect of punishment intensity is nonlinear: below a threshold, it is ineffective, but above the threshold, further increases yield little additional benefit. These findings are robust across both PGG and CRD models. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so the direct effect on efficiency cannot be determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that institutional punishment mechanisms—specifically, sampling punishment—can increase cooperation rates in PGG-like environments, especially when punishment is sufficiently intense, the threshold for triggering punishment is low, and the sample size is small. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates, not payoffs. If the downstream prediction task assumes a strong link between cooperation and efficiency, these results may be indirectly informative, but caution is warranted.

- source: Jaffe, K., & Zaballa, L. (2010). Co-Operative Punishment Cements Social Cohesion. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that co-operative punishment (where the cost of punishing free-riders is shared among group members) is much more effective at maintaining a high proportion of contributors in a simulated public goods game than either no punishment or altruistic punishment (where punishers pay the cost individually). The effectiveness of punishment depends on the ratio of cost to punished versus cost to punisher, and on the efficiency of detecting free-riders. Co-operative punishment allows pro-social behavior to invade and persist in populations of free-riders, while altruistic punishment does not. However, the primary outcome is the proportion of contributors, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the structure of punishment (co-operative vs. individual) is a critical moderator of cooperation in public goods games. While it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes directly, the maintenance of high contribution rates under co-operative punishment suggests that efficiency would likely be higher in such settings, especially when the cost of punishment is shared and detection of free-riders is effective. For prediction tasks, the results imply that enabling co-operative punishment mechanisms (where costs are distributed) should be expected to yield higher efficiency than individual punishment, all else equal. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the precision of quantitative predictions.

- source: Adami, C., & Hintze, A. (2018). Thermodynamics of evolutionary games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a one-dimensional PGG with three players per group, cooperation only emerges when the synergy parameter r exceeds the group size (r > 3). Introducing punishment (with a fine for defectors) shifts the critical point, allowing cooperation to emerge at lower r. The effect of punishment depends on the fine imposed on defectors, not on the cost to punishers. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. The results are derived analytically and confirmed by agent-based simulations, but neither approach reports group efficiency or welfare directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that punishment increases the fraction of cooperators in a PGG-like environment and lowers the threshold for cooperation. However, it does not report or analyze group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare. The findings are useful for predicting when cooperation will emerge as a function of game parameters (e.g., punishment fine, group size, synergy), but do not directly inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes. The results suggest that, under the studied conditions, enabling punishment is likely to increase cooperation rates, which may be associated with higher efficiency in standard PGGs, but this link is not explicitly demonstrated or quantified in the paper.

  findings: The study shows that egoistic punishment can evolve and promote high levels of cooperation in both peer and pool punishment modes, outperforming altruistic punishment in a wider range of parameter settings (especially at higher punishment costs and lower fines). The mechanism is robust to different strategy updating rules and population structures. However, the paper does not directly report on group efficiency or total payoff; instead, it focuses on the prevalence of cooperation and the survival of different strategies.

However, because the main outcomes are cooperation rates and not group efficiency or total payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency outcomes. - source: Quan, J., Tang, C. X., Zhou, Y. W., Wang, X. J., & Yang, J. B. (2020). Reputation evaluation with tolerance and reputation-dependent imitation on cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that, in a spatial public goods game with both punishment and exclusion available, making exclusion the primary sanctioning mechanism (with punishers acting as pure cooperators when excluders are present) leads to a larger parameter region where cooperation dominates, compared to the reverse. The average frequency of cooperation is higher and the threshold for the emergence of cooperation is lower when exclusion leads. The results are robust across variations in the punishment fine, exclusion cost, and synergy factor. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the findings are based on behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than direct efficiency measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a spatial PGG with both punishment and exclusion, exclusion as the leading sanctioning mechanism is more effective at promoting cooperation than punishment as the leading mechanism, across a wide range of parameter values. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The results suggest that game designs enabling exclusion (rather than punishment) as the primary sanction may yield higher cooperation rates, which may correlate with higher efficiency, but this is not directly demonstrated. The findings are most relevant for settings with spatial structure, multiple sanctioning options, and evolutionary updating.

- source: Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2022). Effects of interconnections among corruption, institutional punishment, and economic factors on the evolution of cooperation. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that: (1) Corruption (bribery) reduces the level of cooperation when cooperators are more inclined to bribe, undermining the effectiveness of institutional punishment. (2) Both stronger leaders (higher punishment multiplier) and richer economic potential (higher pool multiplier) enhance cooperation, but their effects interact with the bribery tendencies of group members. (3) When defectors are more likely to bribe, strong leaders can sustain cooperation under poor economic potential, but in rich economic environments, weaker leaders may be more beneficial. The analysis is based on replicator dynamics and the stability of cooperation/defection equilibria, not on direct efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how punishment, corruption, and economic potential interact to affect cooperation in public goods games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it shows that punishment's effectiveness in promoting cooperation is reduced by corruption, especially when cooperators are more likely to bribe. Stronger punishment and higher economic potential generally expand the basin of attraction for cooperation, but the effect can reverse depending on who bribes more. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the presence of corruption and the relative strength of punishment and economic potential are key moderators of punishment's effect on cooperation, but direct translation to efficiency outcomes requires caution.

- source: Li, K., Cong, R., & Wang, L. (2016). Cooperation induced by random sequential exclusion. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the random sequential exclusion (R) mechanism for social exclusion in public goods games can sustain higher levels of cooperation (measured as the stationary frequency of cooperators and excluders) than the standard peer exclusion (P) mechanism, especially under adverse conditions such as high exclusion cost, low exclusion success probability, large group size, and low synergy factor. The advantage of the R mechanism is lost if the scheduling cost for coordination among excluders becomes too high. The results hold in both well-mixed and spatially structured populations. However, the paper does not report direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff, so the findings are about the prevalence of cooperative strategies rather than explicit welfare or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that more efficient or coordinated punishment mechanisms (like random sequential exclusion) can increase the prevalence of cooperative strategies in public goods games, especially when punishment costs are high or group size is large. However, because the paper does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. Instead, it suggests that under certain design dimensions (low scheduling cost, high group size, high exclusion cost), mechanisms that reduce redundant punishment may be more effective at sustaining cooperation. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that the structure and cost of punishment mechanisms are important moderators, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

  findings: The paper finds that in optional public goods games with punishment, the evolutionary process can converge to two types of equilibria depending on initial conditions and the magnitude of rationality: (1) an equilibrium with only punishers and cooperators, and (2) an equilibrium without punishers. The presence of punishers is vital for the evolution and stability of cooperation. The model also shows that bounded rationality leads to more stable evolutionary outcomes compared to previous models. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes.

However, it does not provide quantitative predictions or direct evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that punishment can sustain cooperation, which may imply higher efficiency, but does not quantify this effect or report efficiency outcomes. The model's parameters (e.g., group size, punishment cost, effectiveness) are specified, but efficiency is not analyzed as a dependent variable.

- source: Quan, J., Liu, W., Chu, Y. Q., & Wang, X. J. (2017). Stochastic evolutionary voluntary public goods game with punishment in a Quasi-birth-and-death process. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment and non-participation strategies into a stochastic evolutionary public goods game changes the evolutionary dynamics and the likelihood of the system reaching cooperative, defecting, or non-participation equilibria. Higher punishment probability (alpha) and lower punishment cost (gamma) both increase the parameter regions where the system is likely to reach cooperative/punishing (C+P) equilibria. However, the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff; instead, it reports the probability of the system selecting each type of equilibrium as a function of the parameters. The results suggest that punishment can promote cooperation under a wide range of conditions, but the effect is moderated by the cost and probability of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task: it shows that the likelihood of cooperative/punishing equilibria increases with higher punishment probability and lower punishment cost, and that these effects are sensitive to the marginal per-capita return (r/N) and the fixed income for non-participants. However, because the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, its findings must be mapped from the probability of cooperative equilibria to expected efficiency. The results suggest that, in parameter regions where the C+P equilibrium is likely, efficiency would be higher than in regions dominated by defection or non-participation. Thus, the paper supports the idea that punishment can increase efficiency, especially when punishment is effective and not too costly, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Cong, R., Zhao, Q. C., Li, K., & Wang, L. (2017). Individual mobility promotes punishment in evolutionary public goods games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the optimal mobility (migration tendency) of punishers depends on the cost of punishment. For high punishment costs, a medium tendency to migrate (rather than always punishing or always running) maximizes the prevalence of punishers. For low punishment costs, always punishing is optimal. The presence of cooperators (second-order free-riders) changes the dynamics: in harsh environments (low enhancement factor), punishers benefit from moderate migration, but in more favorable environments, cooperators can dominate, and the advantage of punishment diminishes. The model shows that mobility can help punishers survive, but only under certain cost and environmental conditions. The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but higher prevalence of punishers and cooperators is interpreted as beneficial for cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for how punishment, its cost, and the mobility of punishers affect the prevalence of cooperation in spatial public goods games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the results suggest that punishment can promote cooperation under certain conditions, especially when the cost is not too high and punishers have a moderate migration tendency. For prediction tasks, the findings imply that the effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to be positive when punishment is not too costly and when the game environment allows for spatial structure and mobility. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the precision of quantitative predictions.

- source: Wasieleski, D. M., & Hayibor, S. (2009). Evolutionary Psychology and Business Ethics Research. *Business Ethics Quarterly*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper argues that evolutionary psychology provides a useful framework for understanding cooperation, punishment, and norm enforcement in organizational and business ethics contexts. It reviews evidence from public goods games and related experiments (e.g., Fehr & Gächter, 2002) showing that the availability of punishment increases cooperation rates and reduces free-riding. The review also discusses the concept of strong reciprocity, where individuals are willing to punish norm violators at a cost to themselves, and suggests that such mechanisms are critical for sustaining cooperation in groups. However, the paper does not present new empirical data or directly analyze efficiency or group payoff; instead, it synthesizes existing theoretical and empirical work to propose directions for future research.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and contextual support for the idea that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation in public goods games and similar environments, referencing well-known experimental findings. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or payoff effects, nor does it systematically analyze how game design dimensions moderate these effects. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the general expectation that enabling punishment will increase cooperation and likely improve group outcomes, but it does not provide the detailed, dimension-specific evidence needed for precise prediction of efficiency changes.
