  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment, reward, exclusion, or adaptive combinations thereof can stabilize or increase cooperation in public goods games, but the implementation of these strategies is costly and can create second-order dilemmas. Theoretical models show that optimal (time-varying) incentive protocols can minimize the cumulative cost required to reach a desired cooperation level. Punishment is generally more cost-effective than reward for achieving high cooperation. Adaptive hybrid strategies (first carrot, then stick) can outperform pure punishment or reward. The paper also highlights that the structure of the population and the design of the incentive protocol (e.g., thresholds for switching between punishment and exclusion) are important moderators. However, the main outcomes are cooperation rates and incentive costs, not group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insights into how different incentive strategies (punishment, reward, exclusion, and hybrids) affect cooperation rates and the cost of achieving cooperation in public goods games. It is highly relevant for understanding the mechanisms by which punishment can promote cooperation, and for predicting how design features (such as the presence of punishment, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the use of hybrid strategies) might influence cooperation rates. However, because the primary outcomes are cooperation rates and incentive costs, not group efficiency or total payoff, the paper is only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The models suggest that punishment can increase cooperation and that cost-effective protocols exist, but do not provide direct quantitative predictions of efficiency changes.

- source: Strang, S., & Park, S. Q. (2017). Human Cooperation and Its Underlying Mechanisms. *Social Behavior from Rodents to Humans: Neural Foundations and Clinical Implications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper synthesizes evidence that the threat or presence of punishment in public goods games and similar economic games leads to higher and more stable cooperation rates. It highlights that when punishment is available, cooperation is maintained, and when it is removed, cooperation declines. The review also discusses the emotional and neural correlates of cooperation and punishment, such as the role of negative emotions in driving punishment and the involvement of brain regions like the insula, striatum, and DLPFC. However, the paper does not provide quantitative or direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it systematically analyze how game design dimensions moderate these effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong qualitative support for the idea that punishment increases cooperation in public goods games and related settings, and that this effect is robust across cultures and contexts. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates for the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it analyze how specific game design dimensions (such as player count, MPCR, or punishment cost) moderate these effects. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is best used as background for the general mechanism that punishment increases cooperation, but not for precise quantitative prediction or for understanding the impact of specific design features.

- source: Rosas, A. (2010). Evolutionary game theory meets social science: Is there a unifying rule for human cooperation?. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that cooperative strategies in public goods games and related social dilemmas are governed by a common conditional rule: cooperate with those of good reputation and enforce (by withholding benefits or costly punishment) against those of bad reputation. The choice between withholding benefits and costly punishment depends on the structure of the game—costly punishment is more relevant in public goods games where defectors cannot be targeted directly. The paper reviews experimental evidence showing that combining costly punishment with indirect reciprocity can increase cooperation at lower cost than punishment alone, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes. Theoretical and experimental evidence suggests that the same reputation rules (e.g., SCORING, SIMPLE STANDING, STERN JUDGING) operate across frameworks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing cooperation (and potentially efficiency) in public goods games depends on whether withholding benefits is a viable enforcement mechanism. It suggests that costly punishment is most effective and necessary in public goods games where direct targeting of defectors is not possible. However, the paper does not provide quantitative data on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency. It is most useful for understanding the conditions under which punishment is likely to be effective as an enforcement mechanism, and for mapping the conceptual relationships between different enforcement strategies.

- source: Price, M. E. (2008). The resurrection of group selection as a theory of human cooperation. *Social Justice Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The review highlights that experimental public good games and related economic games (including with punishment) show that people often contribute more than predicted by self-interest and sometimes punish non-cooperators at a cost to themselves, even when reciprocity and reputation are ruled out. This pattern is observed across diverse societies. However, the review argues that these behavioral outcomes do not necessarily indicate group selection or strong reciprocity as an evolved adaptation, and that the experimental settings may not reflect ancestral environments. The review does not report or analyze efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on the theoretical interpretation of observed contribution and punishment behaviors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public good games. It reviews behavioral outcomes (contribution, punishment) from experimental games, but does not report or synthesize efficiency, group payoff, or welfare data. The main value for prediction is contextual: it affirms that punishment is present in many public good game experiments and that it affects contribution behavior, but it does not quantify or analyze efficiency effects. The review also cautions that experimental game results may not generalize to real-world or evolutionary contexts.

- source: Frey, U. J., & Burgess, J. (2023). Why do climate change negotiations stall? Scientific evidence and solutions for some structural problems. *Global Discourse*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat
  findings: The paper finds that climate change negotiations are structurally analogous to public goods games, with similar challenges of free-riding, group formation, and the need for institutional mechanisms to foster cooperation. It reviews experimental evidence showing that the possibility of sanctioning (punishment) increases cooperation and, over time, can improve group welfare, though some studies note that punishment can be inefficient in the short term. The authors argue that the absence of enforceable sanctions in climate agreements is a key reason for stalled progress and that introducing sanctions, improving transparency, and building trust and fairness are critical for better outcomes. However, the paper does not report new empirical data or direct efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and literature-based support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public-goods-game-like environments increases cooperation and, in the long run, can improve group welfare and efficiency, though it acknowledges that punishment can sometimes be inefficient in the short term. It highlights the importance of institutional design features such as sanctioning, transparency, and trust. However, because it does not report new empirical data or direct efficiency outcomes, it should be used as contextual and mechanistic support rather than as a source of quantitative effect sizes for efficiency prediction.

- source: Wang, X. F., & Perc, M. (2022). Replicator dynamics of public goods games with global exclusion. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a global exclusion mechanism (a centralized institution that can exclude defectors from the public good) into the public goods game can lead to higher efficiency and average group payoff, provided the cost of exclusion is not too high and the probability of successful exclusion is sufficiently large. The model predicts that, in these parameter regions, cooperation can emerge and be stabilized, sometimes through cyclic dominance among defectors, cooperators, and excluders. The results are robust across a wide range of functional forms for the exclusion probability. When exclusion is too costly or too unlikely, the system reverts to defection and low efficiency. The paper does not analyze empirical data but provides theoretical and numerical phase diagrams supporting these conclusions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public goods games, the addition of a sufficiently effective and not overly costly global exclusion (institutional punishment) mechanism can substantially increase group efficiency compared to the baseline game. The effect is robust to the details of how exclusion is implemented, as long as the exclusion probability is high enough and the cost is not prohibitive. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, when the game design includes a global exclusion mechanism with moderate or low cost and high effectiveness, one should expect a significant increase in efficiency relative to the control. The model also clarifies that if exclusion is too costly or ineffective, efficiency gains will not materialize. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of institutional punishment/exclusion in PGG-like environments.

- source: Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2020). Evolutionary game dynamics in multiagent systems with prosocial and antisocial exclusion strategies. *Knowledge-based Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper shows that in finite well-mixed populations playing a public goods game, prosocial pool exclusion (where cooperators can pay to exclude defectors from benefits) is more effective at promoting cooperation and group efficiency than costly punishment. Even when antisocial exclusion (where defectors exclude cooperators) is introduced, prosocial pool excluders maintain their evolutionary advantage and can dominate the population, especially when second-order sanctioning is allowed. When both exclusion and punishment (prosocial and antisocial) are available, prosocial pool excluders prevail and maximize group payoff/efficiency. Antisocial exclusion alone leads to defection and low efficiency, but does not undermine the effectiveness of prosocial exclusion when both are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public goods games with the option for prosocial exclusion, enabling such exclusion mechanisms leads to higher group efficiency and welfare than enabling costly punishment, even in the presence of antisocial sanctioning. The results are robust to the inclusion of second-order sanctioning and to moderate changes in the marginal per-capita return (mpcr). For prediction tasks, the presence of prosocial exclusion as a punishment-like mechanism should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, and to outperform standard costly punishment. The findings are most directly applicable to games with continuous contributions, no communication, and fixed group size and rounds, as modeled here.

- source: Xie, K., & Liu, T. J. (2024). The regulation of good and evi promotes cooperation in public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that allowing players to both punish defectors and reward cooperators (PEPG strategy) in a spatial public goods game, especially when combined with heterogeneous payoff allocation based on reputation, leads to higher cooperation rates and higher average payoffs for cooperators. The effect is sensitive to the enhancement factor (r), the importance of reputation in payoff allocation (a), and the strength of the bonus-penalty (gamma). Defectors' average payoffs are always lower than those of cooperators in the steady state. The model demonstrates that the union of PEPG and reputation-based heterogeneity is more effective than either alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides simulation evidence that combining punishment and reward (PEPG) with reputation-based heterogeneous payoffs increases cooperation and average payoffs in public goods games. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that enabling punishment (especially when combined with reward and reputation mechanisms) will increase efficiency, particularly when the marginal per-capita return (mpcr, via r), the effectiveness of punishment/reward (gamma), and the importance of reputation (a) are high. However, the model is not a direct empirical test and does not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative optimum, so quantitative predictions should be made with caution.

- source: Nakamaru, M., & Yokoyama, A. (2014). The Effect of Ostracism and Optional Participation on the Evolution of Cooperation in the Voluntary Public Goods Game. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in voluntary public goods games, allowing group members to exclude (ostracize) candidates with low reputation (defectors) leads to the evolution and maintenance of high cooperation rates. Optional participation (where individuals can choose whether to join a group based on group reputation) does not sustain cooperation. The strictest exclusion rule (requiring unanimous acceptance for membership) is most effective at promoting cooperation. The model is evolutionary and simulation-based, and outcomes are reported as the proportion of cooperators, not as group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that costless ostracism (exclusion of defectors) is a highly effective mechanism for sustaining cooperation in public goods games, more so than optional participation alone. While the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the implication is that group efficiency would be higher when ostracism is enabled, since the share of cooperators is much higher. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is costless and takes the form of exclusion, rather than costly point-based punishment. The results are less directly applicable to predicting the quantitative effect of standard costly punishment on efficiency, but they support the qualitative prediction that effective exclusion or ostracism can greatly increase group efficiency if measured.

- source: Quan, J., Zheng, J. J., Wang, X. J., & Yang, X. K. (2019). The effect of increasing returns to scale in public goods investment on threshold values of cooperation under social exclusion mechanism. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of an exclusion-type punishment mechanism (social exclusion) in a PGG with increasing returns to scale can promote the emergence of cooperation, depending on the cost and effectiveness of exclusion, the returns to scale, and the public goods multiplier. Increasing the effectiveness of exclusion, lowering its cost, or increasing the public goods multiplier can shift the system from all-defection to coexistence of cooperation and exclusion. However, simply increasing returns to scale cannot eliminate defection entirely in the presence of exclusion; it only expands the parameter region where cooperation and exclusion coexist. In structured populations, increasing returns to scale can more directly promote cooperation. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the findings are about the prevalence of cooperative strategies rather than direct payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It shows that the presence and effectiveness of an exclusion-based punishment mechanism can substantially increase the prevalence of cooperation in PGG-like environments, especially when the cost of exclusion is low and its effectiveness is high. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results suggest that, under certain parameter regimes, enabling punishment (via exclusion) is likely to increase efficiency, but this is inferred from the prevalence of cooperation rather than direct payoff data. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is implemented as social exclusion rather than direct fines.

- source: Reuben, E., & Tyran, J. R. (2010). Everyone is a winner: Promoting cooperation through all-can-win intergroup competition. *European Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing tolerance-based expulsion in a spatial public goods game can substantially increase the level of cooperation (measured as the fraction of cooperators and expellers). The effect is strongest at intermediate tolerance thresholds and when expulsion costs are high. The mechanism allows expellers to balance the cost of expulsion with the benefit of removing defectors, leading to higher and more stable cooperation levels. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes, so the direct effect on efficiency cannot be determined from the reported results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that tolerance-based expulsion mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial public goods games, especially when the cost of expulsion is high and the tolerance threshold is optimally set. However, because the reported outcomes are cooperation levels rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The results suggest that enabling punishment (in the form of expulsion) is likely to increase cooperation, but the impact on efficiency is not directly quantified and may depend on the costliness of expulsion and the structure of the population. The model is theoretical and simulation-based, not empirical.

- source: Sun, X. P., Li, M. Y., Kang, H. W., Shen, Y., & Chen, Q. Y. (2023). Combined effect of pure punishment and reward in the public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that social exclusion is a more effective strategy than punishment for promoting cooperation in spatial public goods games. Introducing antisocial punishment (defective punishers) helps eliminate second-order free-riding by cooperators who do not bear exclusion costs. The level of cooperation is sensitive to the exclusion probability, antisocial punishment fine, and exclusion/punishment costs. High exclusion probability and low antisocial punishment fine promote cooperation. The model demonstrates that cooperative excluders can protect cooperators from invasion by defectors and defective punishers, and that the combination of social exclusion and antisocial punishment can sustain high cooperation levels. However, the study does not report on group efficiency or total payoff, so its direct relevance to efficiency prediction is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms like social exclusion and antisocial punishment can increase cooperation rates in spatial public goods games, which may be associated with higher efficiency in practice. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most useful for understanding how the structure and parameters of punishment and exclusion mechanisms affect cooperation dynamics, which may inform priors about efficiency effects in similar game designs. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper's results should be treated as informative about behavioral mechanisms but not as direct evidence.

- source: Liu, L. J., Xiao, Z. L., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2022). Early exclusion leads to cyclical cooperation in repeated group interactions. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated public goods games, the ability to exclude defectors early (after monitoring) leads to cyclical dominance among cooperators, defectors, and excluders, and maintains high average cooperation rates. If exclusion is delayed, defectors can dominate. The model shows that the timing of exclusion, monitoring cost, and discount factor are key moderators. While the model defines payoffs and uses them in evolutionary dynamics, the main reported outcomes are behavioral (strategy frequencies), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task: it shows that enabling a form of punishment (social exclusion) can sustain high cooperation rates if implemented early, and that the timing and cost of exclusion are critical moderators. However, because the main outcomes are behavioral (not efficiency or group payoff), the findings should be used as informative-indirect evidence. The model's structure and parameterization (group size, rounds, exclusion cost, monitoring cost) can inform how similar design dimensions might affect the efficiency impact of punishment in repeated PGGs, but direct quantitative predictions about efficiency are not available.

- source: Orr, S. W. (2001). The economics of shame in work groups: How mutual monitoring can decrease cooperation in teams. *Kyklos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds, through theoretical argument and reference to motivation crowding theory, that mutual monitoring and social sanctions (including social disapproval) in public-goods-like group incentive schemes can decrease cooperation among normatively motivated individuals due to crowding out of intrinsic motivation. The effect is non-linear: some monitoring may initially increase cooperation, but excessive or controlling monitoring leads to suspicion and a shift from normative to gain frames, reducing contributions. For individualistic types, monitoring may crowd in normative motivation and increase cooperation. The overall effect of mutual monitoring or social sanctions on group performance is thus frequency-dependent on the motivational composition of the group. The paper does not provide direct predictions or data on efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that in groups with many normatively motivated members, mutual monitoring may reduce cooperation and thus likely reduce efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical caution that enabling mutual monitoring or social sanctions (punishment) in public-goods-like environments may not always increase efficiency or cooperation, especially in groups with many normatively motivated members. The effect of punishment or monitoring is predicted to be non-linear and frequency-dependent: it may increase cooperation among individualists but decrease it among cooperators if perceived as controlling. For the prediction task, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not universally positive and may depend on group composition and the perceived nature of monitoring. However, the paper does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence on efficiency, so its value is mainly in highlighting possible negative or mixed effects of punishment mechanisms, especially social (rather than material) sanctions.

- source: Forsyth, P. A. I., & Hauert, C. (2011). Public goods games with reward in finite populations. *Journal of Mathematical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in finite populations, allowing individuals to reward contributors to the public good can initiate and maintain a significant minority of cooperators, even though the majority of the population is typically composed of non-cooperating shirkers. Reward is effective at jump-starting cooperation but is unable to stabilize it in the long run due to exploitation by second-order free-riders. The average group payoff (efficiency) is increased relative to pure defection, but full efficiency is not achieved. The effectiveness of reward is largely independent of group size when key parameters are scaled appropriately. The paper does not provide new results on punishment, but notes that punishment is more effective at stabilizing cooperation once established.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence on the effects of reward (not punishment) in public goods games. It shows that positive incentives can increase efficiency above the baseline of mutual defection, but do not achieve full efficiency due to the persistence of second-order free-riders. The findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, reward alone is insufficient to fully stabilize cooperation and maximize efficiency. The results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with reward enabled, but only indirectly inform predictions about punishment. The analysis of group size, MPCR, and reward parameters is informative for understanding how these dimensions affect efficiency with reward. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is most useful for cases where the treatment is reward (not punishment), and less directly informative for punishment-enabled games.

- source: Kurokawa, S., & Ihara, Y. (2017). Evolution of group-wise cooperation: Is direct reciprocity insufficient?. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper shows that in n-player repeated prisoners' dilemma games with non-rivalrous public goods, direct reciprocity alone can support the evolution of cooperation (and thus high group efficiency), especially as group size increases. This holds even when individuals sometimes make mistakes or have imperfect information, provided they use sufficiently optimistic conditional strategies. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The main outcome is the condition under which cooperative strategies are selectively favored over defection, which is directly tied to group payoff and efficiency. The paper does not provide empirical or simulated efficiency values, but gives analytic thresholds for when cooperation is favored.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public goods games with non-rivalrous benefits, increasing group size can make cooperation (and thus high efficiency) more likely, even in the presence of mistakes or imperfect information, as long as players use optimistic conditional strategies. However, the paper does not model punishment, so it does not provide direct evidence for the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. Its results are most relevant for predicting efficiency in control (no-punishment) conditions as a function of group size, information, and error, but not for the effect of punishment. The paper is useful for understanding baseline efficiency in repeated public goods games with direct reciprocity, but not for predicting the incremental effect of punishment.

- source: Murase, Y., & Baek, S. K. (2018). Seven rules to avoid the tragedy of the commons. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated public goods games with three players and implementation error, it is possible to construct deterministic memory-3 strategies that achieve mutual cooperation (efficiency) and defensibility (no player can be exploited), as well as distinguishability (outperforming unconditional cooperators). For n-person games, memory length at least n is required for such strategies. The strategies do not involve explicit punishment actions or costs, but use conditional cooperation and defection rules that can serve similar functions. The findings are theoretical and do not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling or disabling punishment mechanisms on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated public goods games, it is possible to achieve high efficiency through carefully designed deterministic strategies with sufficient memory, even in the presence of errors. However, it does not study the effect of explicit punishment mechanisms (such as costly punishment or sanctions) on efficiency, nor does it manipulate or compare games with and without punishment. Therefore, while the results are highly relevant for understanding the potential for cooperation and efficiency in repeated PGGs, they do not directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in experimental or empirical settings. The paper is most useful for understanding the theoretical limits of strategy design rather than the causal effect of punishment interventions.

- source: Smaldino, P. E., & Lubell, M. (2014). Institutions and Cooperation in an Ecology of Games. *Artificial Life*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model demonstrates that reputational exclusion, when information about behavior is reliable and flows quickly, allows cooperators to achieve much higher payoffs relative to defectors than capacity constraints. However, if reputation is slow to converge or cooperators are rare, capacity constraints are more effective. The ability of cooperators to join many games under reputation is key to their advantage. The model also finds that increasing the productivity of the public goods game (higher multiplier) increases the relative payoff to cooperators under reputation, but not necessarily under capacity constraints. Limiting the number of games an agent can play reduces the effectiveness of reputation-based exclusion. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning (costly punishment), so direct evidence on punishment is not provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it models only exclusion-based institutions (capacity constraints and reputation-based entry) rather than costly punishment. However, it is highly informative about the effects of group entry rules and reputation mechanisms on efficiency and cooperation. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is only adjacent, but it provides strong evidence that exclusion and reputation can be powerful alternatives to punishment for promoting efficiency, especially when information is reliable and cooperators are common. The findings suggest that the design of group entry and information flow can be as important as punishment in determining efficiency outcomes.

- source: Stewart, A. J., Parsons, T. L., & Plotkin, J. B. (2016). Evolutionary consequences of behavioral diversity. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in iterated public goods games, increasing the number of available contribution levels (behavioral diversity) can either increase or decrease group efficiency, depending on the ratio of public benefit to individual cost (mpcr). With low mpcr, more choices allow for more robust cooperative strategies and higher mean payoffs. With high mpcr, the abundance of suboptimal robust strategies causes populations to get stuck at lower efficiency. The ability to punish (by reducing one's own contribution in response to defection) is necessary for two-choice strategies to be robust, but explicit costly punishment is not modeled. The paper provides analytic conditions for when simple strategies can resist invasion by more complex ones, and uses evolutionary simulations to show the impact of choice set size on mean payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games as a function of the number of contribution options (all_or_nothing, player_count, mpcr, etc.), but it does not model explicit costly punishment as a separate mechanism. Instead, 'punishment' is operationalized as the ability to reduce one's own contribution in response to others' defection. The findings suggest that increasing the number of contribution options (behavioral diversity) can either help or hurt efficiency, depending on mpcr. For prediction, the paper supports the idea that more behavioral options do not always increase efficiency, and that the effect depends on the underlying game parameters. However, because explicit costly punishment is not present, the results are only adjacent to the core prediction task regarding punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled games.

- source: Gavrilets, S. (2021). Coevolution of actions, personal norms and beliefs about others in social dilemmas. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model predicts that, in repeated public goods and related games, the long-term average group action and payoff converge to the Nash equilibrium based on material payoffs when there is no external authority. Social and cognitive factors (such as conformity, cognitive dissonance, and beliefs about others) affect the distribution and variance of actions and beliefs, but do not shift the group average away from the material-payoff optimum in the absence of authority. When an external authority promotes a particular action, group behavior can shift toward the promoted standard, sometimes increasing or decreasing efficiency depending on the alignment of the promoted action with the material optimum. The model does not include explicit peer punishment or sanctioning as a treatment variable, so it does not directly inform the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a theoretical baseline for predicting efficiency in public goods and related games without explicit punishment mechanisms. It shows that, absent external authority or explicit sanctions, efficiency is determined by material payoffs, and social/cognitive factors mainly affect variance and convergence speed. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that, unless punishment is modeled as a material cost for non-conformity (as in the 'disapproval by peers' term), enabling punishment would require additional modeling or empirical evidence. The model is most useful for predicting control (no-punishment) efficiency and for understanding the role of soft normative pressures, but does not provide direct evidence for the effect of enabling explicit punishment on efficiency.

- source: Kurokawa, S., Wakano, J. Y., & Ihara, Y. (2010). Generous cooperators can outperform non-generous cooperators when replacing a population of defectors. *Theoretical Population Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated n-player public goods games, reciprocators who are 'generous' (i.e., tolerate some defection before retaliating) can outperform less generous reciprocators and defectors in terms of average payoff and fixation probability, especially when group size is at least 4. The optimal generosity level depends on the benefit-to-cost ratio and group size. Generosity facilitates the emergence of cooperation (replacement of defectors by reciprocators) but does not help maintain cooperation once established. The model provides explicit conditions for when generosity is advantageous and quantifies the optimal generosity for maximizing the likelihood of cooperation's evolutionary success.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated public goods games with n ≥ 4, the design of reciprocation strategies (specifically, the degree of generosity/tolerance for defection) can significantly affect the efficiency outcome when cooperation is replacing defection. While the model does not include explicit punishment mechanisms (such as costly punishment), it analyzes a form of contingent cooperation that functions as a sanctioning mechanism (withholding cooperation from defectors). The findings suggest that, for predicting efficiency in games with repeated interaction and contingent cooperation, allowing some tolerance for defection (generosity) can increase the likelihood of efficient cooperative outcomes, especially in the emergence phase. However, the model does not address explicit costly punishment or its direct efficiency effects, so its relevance to games with explicit punishment is adjacent rather than exact.

- source: Zhang, J. L., Zhang, C. Y., & Cao, M. (2015). How insurance affects altruistic provision in threshold public goods games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing insurance for contributors in threshold public goods games increases the provision of the public good and can improve the efficiency (group payoff) of the system. The effect is strongest when the insurance compensation is high and the required threshold for provision is high. The model shows that insurance can shift the evolutionary dynamics to favor contributors over defectors, depending on parameter values. The allocation rule for surplus contributions (parameter omega) also moderates the effect. The paper does not analyze punishment or sanctions, but provides a theoretical basis for how risk-mitigation mechanisms (like insurance) can promote cooperation and efficiency in threshold public goods settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that risk-mitigation mechanisms (insurance) for contributors can increase efficiency in threshold public goods games, especially when the risk of loss is high and insurance compensation is generous. While it does not study punishment, it is relevant for predicting efficiency in games where contributors face risk and can be protected from loss. The findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, adding insurance or similar mechanisms can substitute for punishment in promoting efficiency. The model is most applicable to threshold public goods games with all-or-nothing contributions, no communication, and no explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. The results are parameter-dependent and highlight the importance of the threshold, insurance compensation, and allocation rules for surplus contributions.

- source: Chen, Q., Chen, T., & Wang, Y. J. (2019). Cleverly handling the donation information can promote cooperation in public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: Publishing the donation list partially (i.e., only for those who contribute above a threshold) increases total contributions to the public good compared to publishing all or none of the list. The optimal threshold for publication can be set dynamically as the mean of recent contributions, which is both effective and convenient. The model shows that this approach increases the sum of contributions and reduces the variance in contributions, indicating more uniform and higher cooperation. The effect is robust to changes in network connectivity and the window of historical data used. Gossip and self-assessment of face act as indirect social sanctions, motivating higher contributions. There is no explicit punishment or reward mechanism with costs and impact parameters as in standard PGG punishment treatments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that indirect social sanctions (via partial publication of contribution information and gossip) can increase total group contributions in a PGG-like environment. While it does not study explicit costly punishment, it suggests that reputational mechanisms can substitute for or complement formal punishment in promoting cooperation and group welfare. For prediction tasks, the findings imply that introducing a reputational/gossip-based sanction (by publicizing contributions above a threshold) can increase group payoff, especially if the threshold is set adaptively. However, the absence of explicit punishment cost/impact parameters means the results are not directly transferable to standard punishment treatments. The sum of contributions is a close but not exact proxy for efficiency.

- source: Smaldino, P. E., & Lubell, M. (2011). An Institutional Mechanism for Assortment in an Ecology of Games. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that capacity constraints (limits on the number of players per game) are a simple and effective institutional mechanism for promoting positive assortment of cooperators in an ecology of public goods games. When group size is limited, cooperators can cluster together and achieve higher payoffs relative to defectors, even without punishment or reputation. Budget constraints (limits on the number of games per player) are less effective. The model does not include punishment, but discusses it as a more complex and potentially costly alternative. The main outcome is the relative cooperator payoff, not group efficiency or total welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not model punishment or sanctions. However, it is relevant for understanding how institutional design (e.g., group size limits) can promote cooperation and higher payoffs without the need for punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that capacity constraints can increase the relative payoff of cooperators, but does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is not directly useful for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency in the context of punishment.

- source: Moreira, J. A., Pacheco, J. M., & Santos, F. C. (2013). Evolution of collective action in adaptive social structures. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals can adapt their social ties—specifically, break links with defectors more quickly than with cooperators—cooperation can be sustained at higher levels than in well-mixed or static networks. The evolutionary dynamics shift from defection dominance to a scenario with stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors, depending on the group size, enhancement factor, and network adaptation rates. Stochastic effects (e.g., mutation, exploration) further help populations escape defection traps and maintain cooperation. However, the paper does not analyze the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it report group efficiency or total payoff as a primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for understanding how endogenous partner selection and adaptive network structure can promote cooperation in PGGs, which may indirectly inform predictions about efficiency in environments where such mechanisms are present. However, it does not address punishment or sanctions, nor does it provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that adaptive partner selection can increase cooperation rates, but it does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency changes due to punishment or other interventions.

- source: Du, C. P., Jia, D. Y., Jin, L. B., & Shi, L. (2018). The impact of neutral reward on cooperation in public good game. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing a neutral reward mechanism, where the majority strategy in a group is rewarded by the minority, promotes the evolution of cooperation in a spatial public goods game. As the reward factor increases, cooperation becomes more prevalent, and for sufficiently high reward, the system can reach full cooperation. The main outcomes are the frequency of cooperators and the spatial distribution of strategies. The paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reward mechanisms (even when not perfectly targeted) can promote cooperation in spatial public goods games. However, because the outcomes are limited to cooperation rates and do not include efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The game design dimensions are described in the model, but the absence of payoff-based outcomes limits the paper's direct utility for predicting efficiency effects of punishment or reward. The results may suggest that reward mechanisms can increase efficiency if cooperation translates to higher group payoff, but this is not demonstrated in the paper.

- source: Zhang, H. R., & Perc, M. (2016). Evolution of conditional cooperation under multilevel selection. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in public goods games with continuous contribution and multilevel selection, the presence of conditional strategies (modeled as piecewise linear response functions) enables the emergence and stability of high cooperation levels. The dominant strategy is robust to initial conditions and parameter changes. The study does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on average contribution and strategy prevalence.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that conditional cooperation strategies can sustain high contribution levels in public goods games under multilevel selection, even without punishment or reward. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff, nor does it manipulate or analyze punishment-related dimensions. Its findings are most relevant for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in the absence of explicit sanctioning mechanisms, and for interpreting the role of conditional strategies as an alternative to punishment. It is not directly informative for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment.

- source: Sutter, M., & Untertrifaller, A. (2020). Children's heterogeneity in cooperation and parental background: An experimental study. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that allocating taxes and rewards based on memory reputation generally increases the cooperation rate in spatial public goods games, especially in environments with high taxes or high synergy factors. In low-tax, low-synergy environments (favorable to defectors), equal allocation of rewards and taxes is more effective at sustaining cooperation. The model shows that the effectiveness of reward mechanisms depends on the balance between tax and reward costs, memory length, and the synergy factor. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, and does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or efficiency outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant: it suggests that the structure of incentive mechanisms (tax-funded rewards, reputation-based allocation) can affect cooperation rates, and that the effectiveness of such mechanisms depends on environmental parameters. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, but it may provide contextual insight into how non-punitive incentive mechanisms interact with game design dimensions.

- source: Tomassini, M., & Antonioni, A. (2020). Public Goods Games on Coevolving Social Network Models. *Frontiers in Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that allowing agents to rewire their social connections when unsatisfied (i.e., when their payoff is less than their contribution) leads to higher average contributions in public goods games on networks, compared to static networks. The transition from low to high contribution occurs at lower enhancement factors when rewiring is allowed, and higher rewiring probabilities further increase average contributions. These effects are robust across both synthetic and real social network structures. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or reward options, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that enabling a form of costless partner switching (exit) can increase average contributions in networked public goods games, which may be relevant for predicting the effects of punishment-like mechanisms that allow players to avoid or sanction defectors. However, because the model does not include explicit costly punishment, and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment is limited. The findings are most relevant for understanding the impact of dynamic network structure (partner choice) rather than punishment per se.

- source: Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2022). Indirect exclusion can promote cooperation in repeated group interactions. *Proceedings of the Royal Society A-mathematical Physical and Engineering Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that indirect exclusion—where a game organizer probabilistically selects cooperators for future participation—can promote the stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors or even lead to the dominance of cooperation in repeated public goods games. The effectiveness of indirect exclusion increases with higher identification probability and more repeated rounds. The model avoids the second-order free-rider problem associated with costly direct exclusion. However, the main reported outcomes are the equilibrium fraction of cooperators and the stationary distribution of strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that indirect exclusion mechanisms can increase the equilibrium level of cooperation in repeated public goods games, especially as the number of rounds and the probability of correctly identifying cooperators increase. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment (or exclusion) on efficiency. The results are most relevant for predicting changes in cooperation rates rather than efficiency, and the exclusion mechanism studied is costless and indirect, differing from standard costly punishment treatments.

- source: Zhou, T. W., Ding, S., Fan, W. J., & Wang, H. (2016). An improved public goods game model with reputation effect on the spatial lattices. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that embedding a reputation effect into the utility function of agents in a spatial PGG model leads to a substantial increase in the stationary fraction of cooperators, especially as the reputation factor increases. The critical synergy factor required for cooperation to emerge is reduced, and the region of parameter space supporting full cooperation is expanded. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are based on Monte Carlo simulations and are relevant for understanding how reputation mechanisms can promote cooperation, but do not directly inform predictions about efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation mechanisms can promote cooperation in spatial PGGs, but does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. For the downstream prediction task—predicting efficiency effects of punishment—this paper is only weakly informative. It suggests that non-monetary social mechanisms (like reputation) can increase cooperation, but does not provide data or analysis on how punishment affects efficiency, nor does it report group payoff or welfare. The game design dimensions are described in detail for the reputation mechanism, but not for punishment or efficiency.

- source: Zheng, J. J., Ren, T. Y., Ma, G., & Dong, J. H. (2021). The emergence and implementation of pool exclusion in spatial public goods game with heterogeneous ability-to-pay. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing pool exclusion, where excluders pay a cost to exclude defectors from the public good, can significantly increase the level of cooperation in a spatial public goods game. The effectiveness of exclusion depends on parameters such as the enhancement factor (r), the probability and effectiveness of exclusion, and the contribution rate to the exclusion pool. Cooperation is more likely to prevail when exclusion is effective and not overinvested. However, the main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators and excluders, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that exclusionary sanctions (pool exclusion) can increase cooperation in spatial public goods games, especially under certain parameter regimes. However, because the main outcome is cooperation level (not efficiency or group payoff), and because the sanction is exclusion rather than classic costly punishment, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs. The results suggest that effective, not overly costly exclusion can help sustain cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency, but this is not directly demonstrated.

- source: Chen, Q., Chen, T., & Wang, Y. J. (2017). Publishing the donation list incompletely promotes the emergence of cooperation in public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: Publishing the donation list incompletely (only for those who contribute above a threshold) leads to higher total contributions and more equal contributions among players in a public goods game on a network. There is an optimal threshold for publication that maximizes these effects. The per capita number of neighbors and the amount of historical data players consider also moderate the effect, but the main result is robust: partial transparency outperforms full transparency or secrecy. The model is agent-based and does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; instead, it relies on social reputation and face loss as motivators.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that social information mechanisms (such as partial publication of contribution lists) can increase cooperation in public goods games, but it does not study monetary punishment or its effect on efficiency. The findings are relevant for predicting the effect of information disclosure or reputation-based interventions, not for predicting the effect of adding costly punishment. The sum of contributions is a proxy for group welfare, but efficiency (as defined by payoff relative to full cooperation) is not directly reported. Use this paper as informative for the effect of social/reputational incentives, not for monetary punishment treatments.

- source: Yang, Z. H., Li, Z., Wu, T., & Wang, L. (2013). Effects of adaptive dynamical linking in networked games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that adaptive dynamical linking—where agents lengthen or shorten social ties based on whether their partners cooperate or defect—can dramatically increase the prevalence of cooperation in both the prisoner's dilemma and public goods game. The effect is stronger when agents adjust their links more rapidly (higher adjustment rate, Δ), and under weak selection. The mechanism does not involve any explicit punishment, reward, or reputation system. The outcome measured is the frequency of full cooperation (all-cooperator absorbing state) in repeated simulations. There is no direct measurement or discussion of group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the prediction task of how punishment affects efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant as a demonstration that network adaptation (partner switching) can promote cooperation, but it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be contextually useful for understanding alternative mechanisms (partner choice) that can promote cooperation, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Wang, X. J., Chen, W. M., & Zhao, J. H. (2020). The evolution of cooperation within the multigame environment based on the Particle Swarm Optimization algorithm. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that increasing the threshold required to initiate a public goods game (i.e., requiring more cooperators for the game to proceed) leads to higher stable levels of cooperation in a spatially structured population. Introducing a reputation adjustment factor, where defectors lose reputation and cooperators gain reputation, further increases cooperation rates. The model shows that even small reputation effects can shift the population toward cooperation. The effect of noise in strategy updating is complex: higher noise can promote cooperation under high thresholds. However, the paper does not analyze or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare; all main results are in terms of the fraction of cooperators.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms which increase the cost of defection (here, via reputation loss) or require a threshold of cooperation to initiate public goods production can increase cooperation rates in spatial PGGs. However, because the model does not include explicit punishment (costly sanctions) and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The findings may be useful for understanding how threshold and reputation mechanisms affect cooperation, but should not be used as direct evidence for the efficiency effects of punishment interventions.

  findings: The study finds that introducing conditional participation (interpreted as a form of punishment) in a spatial public goods game can promote cooperation, but only within an optimal range of aspiration levels. If aspiration is too low, the effect is minimal; if too high, cooperation is suppressed. The transition from defection to cooperation can be abrupt or gradual depending on aspiration and synergy factor. The main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or payoff. The model does not include standard costly punishment or direct payoff reduction, and does not report efficiency or welfare outcomes.

The conditional participation mechanism is adjacent to punishment, but differs in implementation and effect. The main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency. The findings may be useful for understanding how conditional participation or abstention can affect cooperation, but should not be directly used to predict efficiency effects of standard punishment interventions.

- source: Li, Y. X., Jin, X. G., Su, X. C., Kong, F. S., & Peng, C. B. (2010). Cooperation and charity in spatial public goods game under different strategy update rules. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that charity (modeled as payoff transfer from egalitarians to the lowest-payoff neighbor) does not enhance cooperation under random selection update rules, but does promote cooperation under threshold selection rules (where only the lowest-payoff individuals update their strategies). The effect of charity is measured by the frequency of cooperation, not by group payoff or efficiency. The model shows that the presence of charity can lower the threshold for the multiplication factor (r) needed to sustain cooperation under threshold selection. The frequency of charity is positively correlated with the variance of payoffs in the population. The paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it provides indirect evidence about the effect of a redistribution mechanism (charity) on cooperation rates under different update rules. The findings suggest that the effect of a second-stage redistribution mechanism (distinct from punishment) on cooperation depends on the selection dynamics, but the lack of efficiency or payoff reporting limits its direct utility for the prediction task. The game structure and update rules are described in detail, which may be useful for mapping to similar simulation studies.

  findings: The study finds that introducing reputation-dependent imitation and a strict reputation evaluation mechanism (low tolerance for defection) in a spatial public goods game leads to higher and more stable levels of cooperation (as measured by the frequency of cooperators). The effect is stronger when the enhancement factor (analogous to MPCR) is higher. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The results are based on agent-based simulations, not empirical or experimental data.

Its findings about reputation-dependent imitation and tolerance in reputation evaluation may be contextually informative for understanding mechanisms that promote cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The absence of payoff-based outcomes and the lack of a punishment mechanism limit its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Benchekroun, H., & Van Long, N. (2008). The build-up of cooperative behavior among non-cooperative selfish agents. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated public goods game with no punishment or sanctions, but where agents can condition their behavior on a public 'stock of cooperation' (social history), a continuum of Markovian Nash equilibria exist. These equilibria can support steady-state contribution and welfare levels above the static Nash equilibrium but below the social optimum. The social optimum cannot be stably achieved without punishment. The model provides explicit analytical characterizations of these equilibria and shows that the more patient the agents (lower discount rate), the closer the best equilibrium can approach the social optimum. The paper emphasizes that, unlike models with punishment, the highest sustainable efficiency is strictly less than the social optimum.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games without punishment or sanctions, especially when social history or trust mechanisms are present. It provides strong theoretical evidence that, in the absence of punishment, efficiency can be improved above the Nash baseline if agents coordinate on social-history-based strategies, but the social optimum is not achievable. For the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the expectation that enabling punishment (which is absent here) would allow efficiency to reach or exceed the best equilibrium described in this model, and potentially reach the social optimum. The paper is not informative about the effect of punishment per se, but it provides a theoretical upper bound for efficiency in the absence of punishment, given social history mechanisms.
