  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the evolutionary competition between 'alone' (pairwise) and 'together' (group) attitudes leads to complex phase behavior. At low public goods multiplication factors, pairwise interactions dominate; at intermediate values, both attitudes coexist but this can paradoxically lower average income; at high multiplication factors, group interactions dominate and cooperation can be sustained. The presence of the 'together' attitude at low multiplication factors can actually reduce average income, contrary to intuition. However, the paper does not study punishment or sanctions, so it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It is useful for understanding how the structure of interaction (pairwise vs. group) and the public goods multiplication factor affect efficiency, but it does not address punishment, sanctions, or related mechanisms. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects.

- source: Takezawa, M., & Price, M. E. (2010). Revisiting The Evolution of Reciprocity in Sizable Groups: Continuous reciprocity in the repeated n-person prisoner's dilemma. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper shows that in repeated n-person public goods games, continuous reciprocity (where players match the average contribution of others) is much more robust than discrete reciprocity (all-or-nothing) in supporting the evolution of cooperation, especially as the marginal per capita return (mpcr) increases. The basin of attraction for continuous reciprocity grows almost linearly with efficiency, allowing cooperation to invade from low initial frequencies when mpcr is high. Assortative interaction (preferential grouping of reciprocators) further enhances the viability of continuous reciprocity, even at lower efficiency. However, both forms of reciprocity struggle to invade when efficiency is low or group size is large, unless assortativity is present. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the efficiency of cooperation (mpcr) is a critical moderator of the success of reciprocity-based cooperation in public goods games. In the absence of punishment, high mpcr (approaching 1) is necessary for cooperation to be stable or to invade. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the mpcr dimension should be weighted heavily when estimating efficiency outcomes in control (no-punishment) games. However, because the paper does not model punishment or sanctions, it does not directly inform the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It does, however, highlight that in high-mpcr environments, cooperation can be stable even without punishment, while in low-mpcr environments, cooperation is unlikely to persist without additional mechanisms.

- source: Quan, J., Liu, W., Chu, Y. Q., & Wang, X. J. (2018). Stochastic dynamics and stable equilibrium of evolutionary optional public goods game in finite populations. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in an optional public goods game (with strategies: cooperate, defect, or opt out), the probability of the group reaching full cooperation, full defection, or universal non-participation depends on the investment return multiplier (r), the fixed income for non-participants (sigma), and the group size (N). Optional participation can prevent the spread of defection and, for certain parameter ranges (large r and sigma), can make full cooperation the most likely outcome. However, for small sigma or r, non-participation or defection may dominate. The model provides quantitative relationships between parameters and the likelihood of each stable state, which directly map to group efficiency. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly informative for predicting efficiency in optional public goods games without punishment or reward. It provides quantitative relationships between group size, number of rounds (implicitly infinite in the Markov process), MPCR (via r and N), and the probability of full cooperation (i.e., efficiency). However, it does not address the effect of punishment, so it cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment. It is most useful as a control or baseline for optional PGGs without sanctions.

- source: Meloni, S., Xia, C. Y., & Moreno, Y. (2017). Heterogeneous resource allocation can change social hierarchy in public goods games. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that when players in a networked PGG can allocate their contributions unevenly—favoring games where they previously earned higher payoffs—this leads to increased cooperation, a more heterogeneous distribution of investments, and a shift in the structure of wealth production. Specifically, small clusters of cooperators become highly productive, and the overall wealth distribution approaches the Pareto law (80% of wealth produced by 20% of nodes) for sufficiently strong payoff-driven allocation. The study also shows that the classical PGG (equal allocation) does not reproduce these features. The model is robust to different evolutionary update rules and network structures. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are present or analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctions in PGGs or their impact on efficiency. It is highly relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in resource allocation affects efficiency and wealth distribution in PGGs, but it cannot inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task—estimating the efficiency impact of punishment given game design dimensions—this paper is not directly informative, as punishment is not present in the model.

- source: Fan, R. G., Zhang, Y. Q., Luo, M., & Zhang, H. J. (2017). Promotion of cooperation induced by heterogeneity of both investment and payoff allocation in spatial public goods game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that heterogeneity in both investment and payoff allocation, especially under the mixed-preference mechanism (where both previous payoff and social status influence decisions), promotes higher average payoff and cooperation rates compared to mechanisms based solely on wealth or social status. Increasing the enhancement factor r or reducing the heterogeneity parameter alpha further increases cooperation and average payoff. The structure of the interaction network (BA scale-free) also enhances cooperation and payoff compared to other network types. However, the paper does not include or analyze any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to the prediction of punishment effects on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are only relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in investment and payoff allocation, and network structure, affect efficiency and cooperation in PGGs without punishment. It should not be used to inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Zhang, Y. L., Wu, T., Chen, X. J., Xie, G. M., & Wang, L. (2013). Mixed strategy under generalized public goods games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the evolutionary outcome in generalized public goods games with mixed strategies depends critically on the shape of the production function, the group size, the population size, and the cost-to-benefit ratio. For linear production functions, the outcome is determined by the cost/benefit ratio. For nonlinear functions, concave shapes favor stable coexistence (CESS), convex shapes favor bistability (repeller), and sigmoidal shapes can produce more complex dynamics with multiple equilibria. When the group size equals the population size, cooperation is always lost. The paper provides explicit conditions for the emergence of full cooperation, full defection, or intermediate equilibria, but does not address the effects of punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides detailed theoretical predictions about how efficiency (group payoff relative to the cooperative optimum) in public goods games depends on the production function, group size, population size, and cost/benefit ratio, but it does not address punishment or sanctioning. Therefore, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. However, it is highly informative for understanding baseline efficiency in control games without punishment, especially as a function of game design parameters such as group size, population size, and the nonlinearity of the public goods production function.

- source: Huang, Z. G., Wu, Z. X., Wu, A. C., Yang, L., & Wang, Y. H. (2008). Role of collective influence in promoting cooperation. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in public goods games on heterogeneous networks, both the structure of the network and the way individuals distribute their contributions (collective influence parameter alpha) strongly affect cooperation and payoffs. When individuals prefer to contribute more to smaller groups (alpha < 0, social loafing), cooperators receive higher net payoffs, and cooperation is more likely to persist, especially in scale-free networks. The paper provides detailed analysis of individual net payoffs (P_ii) as a function of network degree and alpha, showing that social loafing can make cooperation individually rational for a larger fraction of players. However, the paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it provides useful context for how network structure and contribution allocation rules (collective influence) affect cooperation and payoffs in PGG-like environments. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be informative for understanding baseline efficiency and cooperation in the absence of punishment, especially in networked or heterogeneous group settings.

- source: Quan, J., Yang, X. K., & Wang, X. J. (2018). Spatial public goods game with continuous contributions based on Particle Swarm Optimization learning and the evolution of cooperation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent

  findings: The study finds that the Particle Swarm Optimization (PSO) learning rule can promote higher average cooperation levels in spatial public goods games with continuous contributions, compared to the Fermi rule and Genetic Algorithm learning. The network structure matters: a nearest-neighbor coupled network (1-D) is more conducive to cooperation than a square lattice (2-D). The memory/imitation parameter (c9) has a complex, non-linear effect on cooperation, depending on the investment enhancement factor (r). However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcomes, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, as they show how network structure and learning rules affect average cooperation levels in spatial continuous PGGs. It may inform expectations about baseline cooperation in the absence of punishment, but not about the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Zhang, L., Zhang, L. M., & Huang, C. W. (2022). Defectors in bad circumstances possessing higher reputation can promote cooperation. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a reputation rule—where reputation is based on matching one's strategy to one's neighbors—combined with heterogeneous investment allocation (parameterized by α), strongly promotes cooperation in spatial PGGs. Higher heterogeneity (larger α) lowers the critical synergy factor required for cooperation to emerge and increases the region of parameter space where full cooperation is stable. The results are robust to changes in network structure (square lattice, Moore neighborhood, scale-free network), total investment, noise, and initial cooperation proportion. However, the study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in PGG-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how reputation and heterogeneous investment can promote cooperation, but not for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects. It may be contextually useful for understanding alternative cooperation-promoting mechanisms.

- source: Li, A. M., Wu, T., Cong, R., & Wang, L. (2013). One step memory of group reputation is optimal to promote cooperation in public goods games. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game with dynamic group formation based on group reputation (which is determined by individual memory of past cooperation), cooperation is maximized when individuals have short memory (one-step). Moderate levels of expectation about group reputation and sufficient opportunities to change groups (network dynamics) also promote cooperation. Too high or too low expectations, or too long memory, reduce cooperation. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and the main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings about memory, reputation, and group switching may be relevant for understanding other mechanisms that promote cooperation, but it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Kang, H. W., Zhou, X. J., Shen, Y., Sun, X. P., & Chen, Q. Y. (2021). Influencer propagation model promotes cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that introducing influencers with extra gain (parameter alpha) and varying their proportion (theta) can significantly promote the emergence and stability of cooperation in spatial public goods games, especially when the synergy factor (r) is low. The model shows that cooperative influencer groups can resist invasion by defectors and spread cooperation through the network. However, as r increases, the effect of influencers on promoting cooperation diminishes, and high values of alpha and theta can eventually stabilize both cooperative and betrayal groups. The main outcome is the proportion of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. It is relevant for understanding how leadership, influencer effects, and heterogeneity can promote cooperation, but does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. The findings may be useful for adjacent questions about mechanisms that promote cooperation, but not for the specific prediction task of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency.

- source: Chen, Y. S., Yang, H. X., Guo, W. Z., & Liu, G. G. (2018). Promotion of cooperation based on swarm intelligence in spatial public goods games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The introduction of swarm intelligence (where players update their strategies based on both their own best past strategy and the best current strategy in their neighborhood) into a continuous spatial public goods game leads to a strong promotion of cooperation. The level of cooperation depends on the multiplication factor (r) and the weight given to personal vs. neighborhood best strategies (c1). For low c1 (neighborhood-focused), cooperation can dominate at high r, but is vulnerable at low r. For high c1 (personal best-focused), cooperation is more stable even at low r, but the system is more diverse in strategies. The main reported outcome is the average cooperation level, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are only relevant for understanding how alternative strategy updating rules (swarm intelligence) affect cooperation rates, not payoffs or efficiency. It does, however, confirm that cooperation can be promoted by non-punitive mechanisms, but this is not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency under punishment treatments.

- source: Xu, K. Y., Li, K., Cong, R., & Wang, L. (2017). Cooperation guided by the coexistence of imitation dynamics and aspiration dynamics in structured populations. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the coexistence of imitation and aspiration dynamics in structured populations can have nontrivial effects on cooperation. For fixed update rules, introducing a moderate fraction of aspiration-driven individuals into an imitation-driven population can cause a 'plunge of cooperation' at intermediate synergy factors. When both strategies and update rules coevolve, only one update rule eventually dominates, and the stationary cooperation level depends on which rule prevails. Aspiration dynamics can promote cooperation when the synergy factor is low (poor synergy), but undermine it when the synergy factor is high. However, the study does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings about the effects of update rules (imitation vs aspiration) on cooperation rates may be of indirect interest for understanding behavioral dynamics, but are not directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in games with or without punishment. It should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

  findings: The model shows that cooperation (measured as the fraction of conditional cooperators) only survives in multicultural states with high cultural diversity and very low or zero noise. In monocultural states or with any significant noise, cooperation dies out. The number of participants in public goods games is inversely related to cultural diversity. The paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the model.

For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be useful for understanding the role of cultural diversity and conditional cooperation in the absence of punishment.

  findings: The paper finds that introducing heterogeneity in the scale return coefficient between groups (i.e., making the returns to investment nonlinear and group-specific) generally increases the average cooperation level compared to a homogeneous (linear) setting. Uniform heterogeneity is most conducive to high and stable cooperation in most parameter ranges, but exponential heterogeneity can outperform in some cases. Stronger heterogeneity (higher amplitude) generally promotes higher cooperation. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcome, so the findings are about cooperation rates, not efficiency.

It is not relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be contextually useful for understanding how nonlinearities and heterogeneity in returns affect cooperation rates.

  findings: The study finds that the presence and strength of 'cooperators with independent thought' (CIs) can strongly promote and stabilize cooperation in a spatial PGG. When the heuristic capability of CIs exceeds that of 'defectors with independent thought' (DIs), cooperation dominates; when the reverse is true, defection dominates. If both have equal heuristic capability, coexistence occurs. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is not informative for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the model. The findings are only indirectly relevant, as they pertain to the role of heuristic-driven strategy updating and independent thought in promoting cooperation, not to institutional design features such as punishment.

However, the paper does not consider punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency. Its findings are only relevant for understanding how population density and mobility affect cooperation rates in optional participation settings without sanctions. It should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Huang, C. W., Han, W. C., Li, H. H., Cheng, H. Y., Dai, Q. L., & Yang, J. Z. (2019). Public cooperation in two-layer networks with asymmetric interaction and learning environments. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the structure of the interaction and learning networks (specifically, their overlap) can significantly affect the steady-state level of cooperation in a spatial PGG. For low synergy factor r, higher overlap between interaction and learning networks increases the fraction of cooperators; for high r, lower overlap increases cooperation. The results are robust to changes in network topology and node degree. However, the paper does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism and does not report efficiency or group payoff. Its findings are only relevant for understanding how network structure (specifically, the overlap between interaction and learning networks) affects cooperation rates in PGGs without punishment. It does not inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency in the context of punishment.

- source: Kim, J., Chae, H., Yook, S. H., & Kim, Y. (2015). Spatial evolutionary public goods game on complete graph and dense complex networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The SEPGG with voluntary participation exhibits distinct stationary states depending on the multiplication factor (r), group size (N), and network density (mean degree). On complete graphs (all-to-all), either defectors or loners dominate depending on r, with no stable cooperator-dominant state. On sparse networks, cooperation can dominate for sufficiently low mean degree. The paper provides scaling laws for the transitions between these states. However, the study does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or group payoff; it focuses on the prevalence of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how group size and network structure affect cooperation and opting out in voluntary participation PGGs, but not for the effect of punishment. The results may be useful as a baseline for the behavior of voluntary participation PGGs without punishment, but cannot inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Li, J., Wu, T., Zeng, G., & Wang, L. (2012). Selective investment promotes cooperation in public goods game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that selective investment (where cooperators invest in only a fraction of their neighbors) robustly promotes cooperation in networked public goods games. Lower selectivity parameter (alpha) accelerates the emergence and maintenance of cooperation compared to the classical model where cooperators invest in all groups. Social-preference and wealth-preference patterns (where investment is biased toward high-degree or high-payoff neighbors) hamper the speed and stability of cooperation compared to random selection. Anti-preference patterns (investing in less connected or lower-payoff neighbors) can further accelerate cooperation. Theoretical analysis on double-star networks supports these simulation results. However, the study does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how network structure and selective investment affect cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The results may be useful for contextualizing the baseline cooperation dynamics in the absence of punishment, but do not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment-enabled efficiency.

- source: Huang, K. K., Zheng, X. P., Yang, Y. Q., & Wang, T. (2015). Behavioral evolution in evacuation crowd based on heterogeneous rationality of small groups. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that increasing the degree of rationality (parameter alpha) in a subset of players leads to a higher fraction of cooperators in the population. When more players use rational allocation strategies, cooperation is promoted, and the phase transition points for cooperation occur at lower enhancement factors. The ratio of rational to conventional players also modulates cooperation: more rational players lead to more cooperation. However, the study does not include punishment, reward, or direct efficiency/payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in rationality and investment strategies can promote cooperation, but it does not inform predictions about punishment-enabled versus punishment-disabled efficiency. The absence of punishment and the focus on cooperation rates rather than payoffs limit its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Broom, M., Lafaye, C., Pattni, K., & Rychtár, J. (2015). A study of the dynamics of multi-player games on small networks using territorial interactions. *Journal of Mathematical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the fixation probability of cooperators in the public goods game is generally less than 1/N (where N is the number of players), indicating that cooperation is disfavored under the birth-death process in these small networked populations. The fixation probability is strongly influenced by the mean temperature (a measure of how often individuals are replaced), with higher temperatures amplifying selection and lower temperatures suppressing it. The specific graph structure (network topology) has some effect, but mean temperature is a more important determinant. The results are derived for small graphs (3-4 players), and the framework is generalizable to larger graphs in principle. No punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms are included in the models.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It does, however, provide theoretical insights into how network structure and group size affect the evolutionary success of cooperation versus defection in public goods games. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment, but it may provide contextual background on how structural features (such as player count and group formation) influence cooperation in the absence of punishment.

- source: Figuières, C., Masclet, D., & Willinger, M. (2013). Weak Moral Motivation Leads to the Decline of Voluntary Contributions. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals' moral motivation is weak (i.e., sensitive to others' contributions), average contributions in repeated public goods games decline over time. The model predicts that this decay is robust under both myopic and forward-looking behavior, and can explain several empirical regularities observed in experiments, such as the restart effect and slower decay in longer games. The model does not include punishment or sanctions, nor does it analyze efficiency or group payoff directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not model or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. Its main relevance is in providing a theoretical account of contribution decay in standard linear public goods games without punishment. It may be useful for understanding baseline dynamics in the absence of punishment, but does not inform the prediction of treatment efficiency when punishment is enabled.

- source: Wang, X. J., & Lv, S. J. (2019). The roles of particle swarm intelligence in the prisoner's dilemma based on continuous and mixed strategy systems on scale-free networks. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The authors propose a method to predict the strategies of players whose current actions are hidden, based on the similarity of their past behavior to that of observed players. The method is tested on both the prisoner's dilemma and public goods games on a spatial lattice. The prediction method achieves high accuracy (success rate up to 0.9) in identifying hidden strategies, with performance depending on the length of historical data and game parameters (temptation to defect in PDG, enhancement factor in PGG). The paper does not study punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or related environments. It does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare. Its findings are not informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Acemoglu, D., & Wolitzky, A. (2020). SUSTAINING COOPERATION: COMMUNITY ENFORCEMENT VERSUS SPECIALIZED ENFORCEMENT. *Journal of the European Economic Association*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the optimal way to sustain cooperation in repeated public-goods-like games depends on the effectiveness of the punishment technology. If specialized enforcers can punish at low cost and high impact, then pure enforcer punishment (with immediate return to cooperation) maximizes efficiency. If specialized enforcement is less effective, then combining enforcer punishment with community enforcement (e.g., temporary withdrawal of cooperation or ostracism) is optimal. The paper provides explicit characterizations of the maximum sustainable cooperation (efficiency) in each regime, and shows that the presence of effective specialized enforcement can increase group welfare compared to pure community enforcement. However, if the punishment technology is very weak, community enforcement alone is nearly optimal. The results generalize to both small and large groups, and to settings with imperfect monitoring.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency impact of introducing punishment in public-goods-like games, conditional on the effectiveness of the punishment technology and other game design parameters. It shows that, when punishment is highly effective (i.e., the cost to punishers is low relative to the harm inflicted), enabling punishment can substantially increase efficiency by supporting higher cooperation. However, if punishment is costly or ineffective, the efficiency gain is limited, and community enforcement may be nearly as good. The explicit formulas and comparative statics in the paper can be used to inform predictions about treatment efficiency as a function of punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, group size, and discount factor. The results also highlight that the structure of punishment (one-time vs. repeated, enforcer vs. peer) and the possibility of ostracism or repentance are important moderators.

- source: Tarui, N., Mason, C. F., Polasky, S., & Ellis, G. (2008). Cooperation in the commons with unobservable actions. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a dynamic common property resource game with unobservable actions, a two-part symmetric group punishment strategy (harsh group punishment after a defection, then forgiveness and return to cooperation) can support full cooperation (first-best efficiency) as a subgame perfect equilibrium, provided certain conditions on the discount factor, resource growth rate, and group size are met. The analysis shows that the threat of the harshest possible group punishment (worst perfect equilibrium) is sufficient to deter defection, even when individual actions are not observable. The paper provides explicit mathematical conditions for when this is possible and uses numerical simulations to illustrate how the sustainability of cooperation depends on group size and other parameters. Notably, the effect of group size is non-monotonic: increasing group size can initially make cooperation easier to sustain (by making punishment more credible), but for larger groups, the incentive to defect eventually dominates, making cooperation harder to sustain.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that, in public-goods-like environments with unobservable actions, the availability of credible, symmetric group punishment can enable full efficiency (relative to the first-best cooperative outcome), provided the punishment is sufficiently harsh and the parameter conditions (discount factor, resource growth, group size) are favorable. The analysis is directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in games where only aggregate outcomes are observable. The findings suggest that punishment can have a strongly positive effect on efficiency, but only if the punishment is credible and harsh enough to deter defection. The effect of group size is nuanced: increasing group size can initially make punishment more credible (and thus support efficiency), but for larger groups, the incentive to defect increases and efficiency becomes harder to sustain. The paper does not address other design dimensions such as chat, reward, or information feedback, so its predictive value is strongest for settings matching the model's assumptions (no chat, no reward, symmetric group punishment, unobservable individual actions, continuous contribution/harvest choices).

- source: Noailly, J., van den Bergh, J. C. J. M., & Withagen, C. A. (2009). Local and Global Interactions in an Evolutionary Resource Game. *Computational Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that local punishment mechanisms in a spatial CPR game allow for the emergence and persistence of cooperative and mixed-strategy equilibria, which are associated with higher aggregate payoffs and efficiency than the all-defector equilibrium. The frequency of efficient equilibria increases with higher sanction levels and larger population sizes, and spatial clustering of cooperators and enforcers is crucial for sustaining cooperation. The model demonstrates that spatial structure and local enforcement can overcome the limitations of non-spatial models, leading to more diverse and efficient outcomes. Sensitivity analysis shows that increasing the cost or effectiveness of sanctions, or increasing population size, generally increases the prevalence of efficient (cooperative or mixed) equilibria.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in spatially structured public-goods-like games increases group efficiency and aggregate payoff, especially when punishment is local and sufficiently strong. The findings suggest that, for a given control efficiency, the introduction of local punishment will often lead to higher efficiency, particularly in larger groups and with higher sanction levels. The results also highlight the importance of spatial structure and local interactions, which may moderate the effect of punishment compared to non-spatial models. The paper's parameter sensitivity analysis can inform predictions about how changes in punishment cost, effectiveness, and group size affect the efficiency gains from punishment.

- source: Okada, D., & Bingham, P. M. (2008). Human uniqueness-self-interest and social cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper develops a two-stage game-theoretic model in which players can cooperate or not, and, if there are non-cooperators, cooperators can choose to fight (coerce) or not. The key result is that the cost structure of coercion (punishment) is decisive: if coercion is cheap and effective (as in synchronous/remote attack, e.g., human projectile weapons), then robust equilibria with high cooperation and high efficiency are possible. If coercion is costly and ineffective (as in asynchronous attack, e.g., most non-human animals), only non-cooperation is robust, and group efficiency is low. The model thus predicts that enabling effective, low-cost punishment can transform a low-efficiency public goods game into a high-efficiency one, provided certain thresholds are met.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling effective, low-cost punishment (i.e., high punishment effectiveness per unit cost) in a public-goods-like environment can shift the equilibrium from low to high efficiency, especially as group size increases. The model is directly informative for the effect of punishment cost and punishment technology (effectiveness), and indirectly for player count and the structure of the contribution decision. It suggests that, all else equal, lowering punishment cost or increasing punishment effectiveness should make punishment more likely to increase efficiency. The findings are most relevant for one-shot or short games with simultaneous moves and no communication, and may be less directly applicable to repeated or highly structured lab experiments with additional features.

- source: Noailly, J., Withagen, C. A., & van den Bergh, J. C. J. M. (2007). Spatial evolution of social norms in a common-pool resource game. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in a spatial CPR game, the introduction of enforcers (who punish defectors) leads to the emergence and persistence of cooperative equilibria, which are associated with higher aggregate payoffs and more efficient resource use. The frequency of such efficient equilibria increases with the sanction rate (punishment effectiveness) and is further enhanced when resource dynamics (feedback from resource depletion) are included. Simulations show that, for a wide range of parameters, punishment increases the likelihood of equilibria with cooperation and higher payoffs, compared to scenarios without punishment. The spatial structure (local punishment) is crucial for these results, as it allows cooperators and enforcers to protect each other and sustain cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment in a spatial CPR (public goods-like) game increases the frequency of efficient, high-payoff equilibria, especially when the punishment is sufficiently strong and resource feedbacks are present. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism (with nontrivial cost and effectiveness), local interaction, and resource dynamics should be expected to increase treatment efficiency relative to control. The results are most directly applicable to settings with local punishment, spatial structure, and evolutionary imitation dynamics, but the qualitative direction (punishment increases efficiency) is robust across parameter sweeps. The paper does not provide precise quantitative predictions for efficiency, but shows that the effect is substantial and positive under a wide range of conditions.

- source: Lee, J. H., Sigmund, K., Dieckmann, U., & Iwasa, Y. (2015). Games of corruption: How to suppress illegal logging. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that punishment (via hired enforcers) can sustain high efficiency (cooperation and group welfare) only if a sufficient fraction of enforcers are honest. If corruption is prevalent among enforcers, punishment fails and efficiency collapses. The system is bistable: both high- and low-efficiency equilibria are possible. Small rates of random exploration (mutation) among enforcers can tip the system toward either high or low efficiency, depending on whether honest or corrupt exploration is more common. Providing information about enforcer honesty (i.e., making corrupt enforcers identifiable) greatly increases the likelihood of high-efficiency outcomes. The model shows that interventions targeting enforcer honesty (education, incentives, transparency) are more effective for improving efficiency than interventions targeting harvesters directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that the effectiveness of punishment in improving efficiency in public-goods-like games depends critically on the integrity of the punishment institution. If enforcers are honest and/or can be identified as such, punishment can lead to high efficiency. If enforcers are corrupt and cannot be identified, punishment may fail and efficiency may be low. The model suggests that game design dimensions that increase the likelihood of honest enforcement (e.g., transparency, information sharing, enforcer education) will increase the efficiency gain from punishment. The presence of bistability means that initial conditions and small biases (e.g., in enforcer behavior) can have large effects. For prediction, this paper implies that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on the effectiveness and honesty of the punishment mechanism, and on whether players can identify corrupt enforcers.

- source: Gavrilets, S., & Shrestha, M. D. (2021). Evolving institutions for collective action by selective imitation and self-interested design. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in agent-based models of collective action with institutionalized punishment by leaders, both selective imitation and self-interested design (foresight) in leaders can lead to the emergence of effective institutions that increase cooperation, group production, and payoffs (efficiency). The presence of leaders who monitor and punish free-riders increases group effort and payoffs compared to groups without leaders or punishment. The effect is strongest when the benefit-to-cost ratio is high, punishment is not too costly, and leaders value future payoffs. The model also shows that the details of the updating mechanism (imitation vs. foresight) matter most when the benefit-to-cost ratio is intermediate and leaders heavily discount the future. In 'us vs. nature' games, increased monitoring and production lead to higher payoffs for all; in 'us vs. them' games, overproduction can reduce payoffs for subordinates. The model provides direct evidence that institutionalized punishment can increase efficiency in public-goods-like settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling institutionalized punishment by leaders in public-goods-like games increases group efficiency and payoffs, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is high and punishment is not too costly. The effect is robust to variations in group size, punishment cost, and the leader's share of rewards. For prediction tasks, the presence of leader-administered punishment should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control (no punishment), with the magnitude depending on the benefit-to-cost ratio, punishment cost, and whether leaders value future payoffs. The model also suggests that the effect of punishment is less positive or even negative if punishment is very costly or if leaders do not value future payoffs. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in games with institutionalized punishment, especially when the control efficiency is low due to free-riding.

- source: Okada, I., Yamamoto, H., Toriumi, F., & Sasaki, T. (2015). The Effect of Incentives and Meta-incentives on the Evolution of Cooperation. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a theoretical evolutionary game framework, cooperation and high efficiency (group payoff) can only be stably achieved if the incentive system includes second-order incentives for first-order rewards. Systems with only first-order punishment or reward, or with second-order incentives for punishment but not for reward, cannot maintain cooperation or efficiency in the long run due to the second-order free-rider problem. The cost-effect ratio of incentives (how much impact per unit cost) is a critical parameter: higher ratios make it easier for cooperation to be sustained. The model predicts that systems with second-order rewards for first-order incentives are more robust and can achieve high efficiency with fewer incentive-providers than systems with second-order punishments. The analysis is entirely theoretical, using replicator dynamics, and does not provide empirical estimates of efficiency but gives qualitative and parametric conditions for when efficiency is high or low under different incentive structures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of punishment and reward systems (including meta-incentives) on efficiency in public-goods-like games. It suggests that simply enabling punishment is not sufficient for high efficiency; the structure of incentives, especially the presence of second-order incentives for rewarding, is crucial. If a game design includes only first-order punishment, efficiency will not improve in the long run. If second-order incentives for first-order rewards are present and the cost-effect ratio is high, efficiency can be high. The model also clarifies that the effect of punishment on efficiency is conditional on the broader incentive structure. For prediction tasks, this paper implies that the presence or absence of second-order incentives, and the cost-effectiveness of punishment/reward, are key moderators of the efficiency effect of punishment.

- source: De Silva, H., Hauert, C., Traulsen, A., & Sigmund, K. (2010). Freedom, enforcement, and the social dilemma of strong altruism. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Theoretical analysis and simulations demonstrate that costly punishment enables the emergence and maintenance of cooperation in joint effort games, but only when participation is voluntary. In the voluntary regime, the stationary distribution is dominated by punishers, leading to higher group payoffs and efficiency. In the compulsory regime, defectors dominate and group efficiency collapses. This result is robust for both 'weak' and 'strong' altruism (i.e., whether or not contributors benefit from their own contributions). The findings imply that the combination of voluntary participation and punishment is critical for sustaining cooperation and efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on group efficiency is highly contingent on whether participation in the public goods game is voluntary or compulsory. If participation is voluntary, introducing punishment leads to a regime dominated by punishers and high group efficiency. If participation is compulsory, punishment does not prevent the dominance of defectors and group efficiency remains low. For prediction tasks, the presence or absence of voluntary participation (i.e., whether players can opt out) is a key moderator of the efficiency impact of punishment. The model also suggests that these results are robust to variations in group size, population size, and the strength of selection, as long as the basic structure (voluntary participation, costly punishment) is maintained.

- source: Lee, J. H., Jusup, M., & Iwasa, Y. (2017). Games of corruption in preventing the overuse of common-pool resources. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that delegated punishment (enforcement) can support efficient, fully cooperative outcomes in common-pool resource games, but only if a sufficient fraction of enforcers are honest. If corruption is prevalent among enforcers, punishment loses its effectiveness, and the system converges to defection and resource overuse (low efficiency). The model exhibits bistability: depending on initial conditions (notably the initial fraction of honest enforcers), the system ends up either in full cooperation (high efficiency) or full defection (low efficiency). Key parameters moderating the effect of punishment include group size (larger groups make cooperation harder), resource growth rate (higher growth rates favor defection), and the speed of resource dynamics (slower resource response masks the benefits of cooperation and impedes efficiency). The cost of harvesting has a mixed effect. The model provides direct predictions about how these dimensions affect the likelihood of efficient outcomes under punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like resource games depends critically on the honesty of enforcers and several game design dimensions. If punishment is implemented via delegated enforcement and enforcers are honest, punishment can enable high efficiency (full cooperation). However, if enforcers are corrupt, punishment fails and efficiency collapses. The model shows that increasing group size, increasing resource growth rate, and slowing resource dynamics all reduce the effectiveness of punishment in achieving efficiency. These findings should inform predictions about treatment efficiency: punishment will only improve efficiency if the enforcement mechanism is robust to corruption and the group size and ecological parameters are favorable. The model is most directly relevant to settings with delegated (not peer) punishment and where corruption is possible.

- source: Wang, M., Kang, H. W., Shen, Y., Sun, X. P., & Chen, Q. Y. (2021). The role of alliance cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a dynamic common-pool resource game with probabilistic punishment of defectors, increasing the monitoring probability and punishment severity generally increases the proportion of cooperators and can help maintain higher resource levels (i.e., higher efficiency/welfare). However, if the initial resource level is too low or the resource's maximum growth rate is insufficient, the system will experience resource depletion (tragedy of the commons) regardless of punishment strength. The model demonstrates bistability: depending on initial conditions, the system may converge to full cooperation with resource depletion, or to a mixed/cooperative state with sustainable resources. Thus, punishment can improve efficiency, but only when ecological conditions allow for resource regeneration.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like resource environments is conditional: punishment (higher probability and severity) can increase efficiency and resource sustainability, but only if the resource's regenerative capacity and initial abundance are sufficient. In settings where the resource cannot recover (low growth rate or low initial stock), punishment does not prevent collapse. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by ecological parameters (resource growth, initial abundance) in addition to standard game design dimensions. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related, and the efficiency outcome is directly modeled.

- source: Weibull, J. W., & Salomonsson, M. (2006). Natural selection and social preferences. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the evolutionary stability and efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-like games depend on the structure of reproductive externalities. When group members' fitness is positively affected by others' payoffs (cooperative breeding), punishment and cooperation can become stable and lead to higher group welfare, especially if the punishment is sufficiently harsh and the cost is not too high. In contrast, under strong reproductive competition, punishment can be stable but may not increase group efficiency. The model shows that the presence and effectiveness of punishment as a mechanism for promoting cooperation and efficiency is highly sensitive to the underlying fitness function and game parameters. The analysis provides explicit conditions for when punishment leads to more efficient, cooperative outcomes and when it does not.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on when punishment is likely to increase efficiency in public-goods-like games, depending on the game design and the nature of social/reproductive externalities. It suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not universal: it can be positive, nil, or even negative, depending on whether the environment favors cooperative or competitive externalities. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that game design dimensions such as the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the structure of payoffs, and the degree of group interdependence (e.g., MPCR analogs) are critical moderators. The findings support the idea that punishment is more likely to improve efficiency when group welfare is a significant component of individual fitness, and less likely (or even counterproductive) when individual competition dominates.

- source: POLLOCK, G. B. (1988). POPULATION-STRUCTURE, SPITE, AND THE ITERATED PRISONERS-DILEMMA. *American Journal of Physical Anthropology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that punishment-based strategies (TFT/NTFT) can maintain high group efficiency and suppress defection when the population is structured into multiple groups or dyads, allowing intergroup competition. The effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation and high payoffs is reduced as the scope of spite (the number of groups a defector can affect) increases or as population structure becomes less partitioned. In the limit of a single group (no structure), punishment cannot prevent defection and efficiency collapses. The analysis generalizes to n-person games, showing that the same logic applies: partitioned populations with group-level competition allow punishment to sustain cooperation and high efficiency, while unstructured populations do not.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public-goods-game-like environments will increase efficiency (group payoff) relative to control, but only when the game design includes sufficient population structure (multiple groups, limited overlap in group membership, or mechanisms for intergroup competition). If the game is a single group or highly overlapping, punishment will not improve efficiency. The findings are directly relevant for settings where the marginal per-capita return, group size, and population structure can be specified, and they highlight the importance of these dimensions as moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency.

- source: Kendal, J., Feldman, M. W., & Aoki, K. (2006). Cultural coevolution of norm adoption and enforcement when punishers are rewarded or non-punishers are punished. *Theoretical Population Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that both reward-based and punishment-based metanorms can enable the stable coexistence and fixation of norm-adopting punishers, leading to high group efficiency (mean fitness) under a wide range of parameter values. The presence of a metanorm (either rewarding punishers or punishing non-punishers) is necessary for this outcome; without it, the only stable equilibrium is full defection (no norm adoption, no punishment). The Reward Model is generally more effective than the Punishment Model in enabling the population to reach the efficient equilibrium, especially when starting from low frequencies of norm-adopters and punishers. The probability of fixation of the efficient equilibrium is higher under the Reward Model, and the region of attraction is larger. The models also show that cultural drift can facilitate the transition from the inefficient to the efficient equilibrium, especially in small populations and when costs of norm observation and punishment are low. The results are robust across a range of parameter values and are relevant to public goods game-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment (especially when reinforced by a metanorm, such as rewarding punishers or punishing non-punishers) can shift a population from an inefficient equilibrium (low efficiency, no cooperation) to an efficient one (high efficiency, full cooperation and sanctioning), provided the costs and benefits are favorable. The results suggest that, in public goods game-like environments, the introduction of punishment (with or without additional metanorms) can substantially increase group efficiency, especially if the cost of punishing is not too high and the benefit to punishers is sufficient. The Reward Model (rewarding punishers) is particularly effective, suggesting that institutional or social mechanisms that reward sanctioning may be more robust in promoting efficiency than those that only punish non-punishers. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, given knowledge of the cost/benefit structure and initial conditions. However, the results are theoretical and assume well-mixed populations and specific forms of cultural transmission.

- source: Powers, S. T., & Lehmann, L. (2013). The co-evolution of social institutions, demography, and large-scale human cooperation. *Ecology Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that the co-evolution of institutions with sanctioning (punishment) and demography enables the stable emergence and maintenance of large-scale cooperation, resulting in a dramatic increase in group carrying capacity and efficiency. In the absence of institutionalized punishment, cooperation cannot be stably maintained, and group size/efficiency remains low. The model demonstrates that the optimal institution invests most, but not all, of the public good into technological improvement (increasing carrying capacity), while retaining enough for sanctioning to deter defectors. Sensitivity analyses show that the positive effect of punishment on efficiency is robust to changes in key parameters, though it is diminished by high migration rates or low benefits of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling institutionalized punishment in public-goods-like environments will increase group efficiency, especially in structured populations. The model directly links the presence and effectiveness of punishment to higher group payoffs and larger sustainable group sizes. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, all else equal, introducing or strengthening punishment mechanisms should increase efficiency, provided the cost of punishment is not prohibitive and the benefit from cooperation is sufficient. The model also highlights the importance of population structure (limited migration) and the allocation of resources between sanctioning and productive investment. While the model is not an empirical experiment, its detailed mapping to public goods game structure and explicit focus on efficiency make it highly informative for predicting the impact of punishment on efficiency outcomes.

- source: Iwasa, Y., & Lee, J. H. (2013). Graduated punishment is efficient in resource management if people are heterogeneous. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that graduated punishment (punishment increasing with the harm caused by selfish actions) is only efficient—i.e., maximizes group welfare—when two conditions are met: (1) there is heterogeneity among players in their sensitivity to utility differences, and (2) there is a nonzero probability of false accusation (punishing prosocials by mistake). In standard rational or homogeneous quantal response models, optimal punishment is not graduated but rather jumps from zero to a high level. With heterogeneity and error, the optimal punishment increases smoothly with harm, matching the graduated punishment observed in successful resource management. The paper provides explicit formulas for optimal punishment and shows how efficiency-maximizing punishment depends on model parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that the efficiency impact of punishment in public-goods-like games depends critically on the presence of player heterogeneity and error in monitoring. In standard homogeneous or error-free settings, punishment is either absent or severe, with little efficiency gain from graduation. When heterogeneity and error are present, graduated punishment is optimal and can improve efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency will be more positive in environments with player heterogeneity and monitoring errors, and that the optimal punishment level should increase with the harm caused by defection. The paper's analytic results can inform parameterization of efficiency predictions as a function of harm, benefit, error rates, and heterogeneity.

- source: Gao, L., Wang, Z., Pansini, R., Li, Y. T., & Wang, R. W. (2015). Collective punishment is more effective than collective reward for promoting cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that collective punishment is more effective than collective reward in promoting and stabilizing cooperation in multiplayer snowdrift games, especially for small groups or high initial cooperation. When the punishment-to-benefit ratio exceeds a threshold, a stable state of full cooperation emerges for both small and large groups, while such a state does not appear for large groups with only reward. The main outcome is the frequency of cooperation, which is closely related to group payoff but not a direct measure of efficiency. The results suggest that, in environments where individual-level discrimination is not possible, collective punishment can maintain high cooperation and thus higher group fitness or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in multiplayer public-goods-like environments (specifically, snowdrift games), enabling collective punishment (even when it cannot be targeted at individuals) can substantially increase the frequency and stability of cooperation, especially in small groups or when the punishment intensity is high. This implies that, for prediction tasks, the presence and strength of collective punishment should be expected to increase efficiency or group payoff, particularly in small groups. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions of efficiency as defined by total group payoff relative to full cooperation, so the findings should be interpreted as indicating a strong positive effect on efficiency-related outcomes via increased cooperation rates and stable cooperative equilibria.

- source: Perry, L., Shrestha, M. D., Vose, M. D., & Gavrilets, S. (2018). Collective Action Problem in Heterogeneous Groups with Punishment and Foresight. *Journal of Statistical Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that allowing punishment in heterogeneous groups increases cooperation and group effort, especially when individuals have foresight and can anticipate the effects of their actions on others' future behavior. Foresight enables punishment to become utility-increasing, overcoming the second-order free-rider problem. However, the costs of punishment can be substantial, so higher cooperation does not always translate into higher group payoffs or efficiency. The model predicts a division of labor, with dominant individuals specializing in punishment and subordinates in contribution. The effect of punishment on group efficiency is context-dependent: it can be positive, negative, or neutral depending on parameter values (e.g., group size, benefit/cost ratio, hierarchy steepness, precision, and foresight weight).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends on the interplay between group heterogeneity, foresight, and the costs of punishment. If punishment is cheap and foresight is present, enabling punishment is more likely to increase efficiency by raising cooperation. If punishment is costly, the net effect on efficiency may be negative, as the costs of punishment can outweigh the gains from increased contributions. The model suggests that the effect of punishment is sensitive to group size (larger groups make cooperation harder), the benefit/cost ratio, and the degree of hierarchy. For prediction, the presence of foresight and the relative costs of punishment are key moderators. The model does not provide direct empirical estimates but offers qualitative guidance on when punishment will or will not improve efficiency.

- source: Nakamaru, M., & Dieckmann, U. (2009). Runaway selection for cooperation and strict-and-severe punishment. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in spatially structured populations, the co-evolution of cooperation and punishment (with continuous strategies for both) can lead to runaway selection for both high cooperation and strict-and-severe punishment. This dynamic is most pronounced when punishment is not too costly and cooperation is neither too cheap nor too expensive. The model shows that strict punishment norms (high strictness and severity) evolve naturally and reinforce high cooperation, resulting in much higher group-level cooperation than in the absence of punishment. In contrast, in well-mixed populations, punishment and cooperation do not evolve. The findings suggest that punishment can greatly enhance group cooperation and, by implication, group efficiency, but only under certain cost structures and spatial conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in spatially structured public-goods-like environments can substantially increase group cooperation and, by extension, efficiency—especially when punishment is strict, severe, and not too costly. The model suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on the cost structure of cooperation and punishment, and on the population structure (spatial vs. well-mixed). For prediction tasks, the findings imply that punishment will have a more positive effect on efficiency when: (1) the game is spatially structured (analogous to local interactions), (2) punishment is strict and severe, (3) costs of punishment and cooperation are not too high, and (4) baseline cooperation is low without punishment. However, the model is theoretical and does not provide direct empirical efficiency ratios, so its quantitative predictions should be interpreted with caution.

- source: Lee, J. H., Iwasa, Y., Dieckmann, U., & Sigmund, K. (2019). Social evolution leads to persistent corruption. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
