  findings: This theoretical study shows that while institutional punishment can, in principle, sustain cooperation and high payoffs in public-goods-like settings, the possibility of corruption (i.e., punishment institutions accepting bribes) leads to cycles or stable states of reduced cooperation and lower average payoffs. The model demonstrates that transparency and monitoring are necessary to maintain honest punishment, but these are costly and thus not always sustained. As a result, the efficiency gains from punishment are not robust: corruption can cause efficiency to fall well below the cooperative optimum, and the system may oscillate between periods of high and low efficiency depending on the prevalence of corruption and monitoring.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a cautionary theoretical perspective: enabling punishment in a public-goods-like game does not guarantee increased efficiency if the punishment institution can be corrupted. The efficiency benefit of punishment depends critically on the integrity of the institution and the cost and prevalence of monitoring. In prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on whether corruption is possible and how transparency is maintained. If the game design allows for institutional corruption or lacks mechanisms for transparency and monitoring, enabling punishment may not improve—and may even reduce—efficiency relative to the control. The model also highlights the importance of considering dynamic and cyclical effects, not just static outcomes.

- source: Currie, T. E., Campenni, M., Flitton, A., Njagi, T., Ontiri, E., Perret, C., & Walker, L. (2021). The cultural evolution and ecology of institutions. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper argues that institutions, including punishment mechanisms, can alter the payoffs in collective action problems such as public goods games, making cooperation more stable and frequent. The agent-based model demonstrates that when agents can collectively set punishment levels, cooperation can emerge and be maintained, with higher payoffs for cooperators and a reduction in free-riders. The model is illustrative and not based on empirical data. Macro-scale analyses show that institutional complexity and inclusivity are associated with higher economic development, but these are not tested in controlled PGG-like settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that introducing punishment mechanisms in public goods games can increase cooperation and, in model terms, improve payoffs for cooperators. However, it does not provide empirical estimates of efficiency or group payoff changes due to punishment in controlled experimental settings. The agent-based model suggests that the ability to set and adjust punishment can help overcome collective action problems, but the findings are illustrative and not directly transferable to quantitative prediction tasks. The paper is useful for understanding mechanisms and for contextualizing the role of punishment, but not for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions.

- source: Couto, M. C., Pacheco, J. M., & Santos, F. C. (2020). Governance of risky public goods under graduated punishment. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Graduated institutional punishment, where the severity and cost of punishment increase with the number of defectors, is more effective than strict (fixed) punishment in promoting cooperation and public good achievement in collective-risk dilemmas. This effect is especially pronounced at low risk levels and when both fines and costs are graduated. The model shows that graduated punishment reduces the time populations spend in all-defector states and increases the prevalence of cooperation and sustainable institutions. The approach also helps solve the second-order free-rider problem associated with institutional punishment. The findings are robust across a range of parameters and extend to linear public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in threshold public goods games with risk and institutional punishment, making punishment and its costs graduated (i.e., increasing with the number of defectors) leads to higher group achievement and overall welfare than strict, fixed punishment. While the main outcomes are not reported as efficiency ratios, the close relationship between group achievement, average fitness, and efficiency means the results are highly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that, for games with similar structure (threshold, risk, institutional punishment), enabling graduated punishment will likely increase efficiency relative to strict punishment, especially at low risk and when punishment costs are also graduated. The results are robust to changes in group size, threshold, and other parameters.

- source: Janssen, M. A. (2015). A behavioral perspective on the governance of common resources. *Current Opinion in Environmental Sustainability*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: chat
  findings: The paper finds that, contrary to the predictions of standard economic theory, groups can achieve higher efficiency and avoid the tragedy of the commons when allowed to communicate or implement costly punishment. Both lab and field experiments show that these mechanisms increase group earnings and reduce overuse of resources. The review also notes that centralized sanctioning mechanisms may be more effective and sustainable than peer punishment. However, the effectiveness of punishment and other interventions is context-specific, depending on factors such as group size, communication, trust, and cultural background. The paper also discusses the phenomenon of 'crowding out,' where externally imposed rules can sometimes reduce performance compared to voluntary agreements.
  prediction_guidance: This paper supports the prediction that enabling punishment in public-goods-game-like environments generally increases group efficiency or earnings, especially when compared to baseline games without punishment or communication. It also highlights that the effect of punishment is moderated by contextual factors such as group size, communication, and the nature of the sanctioning institution (peer vs. centralized). While the review does not provide quantitative estimates, it offers strong qualitative evidence that punishment mechanisms, when available, tend to improve efficiency outcomes, but the magnitude and sustainability of these effects depend on the specific game design and social context.

- source: Zhang, B. Y., & Hofbauer, J. (2016). Quantal response methods for equilibrium selection in 2 x 2 coordination games. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a mini public goods game with punishment, the QRE method selects a cooperative equilibrium if the punishment is sufficiently strong (i.e., if the fine-to-fee ratio is high enough). The selection of cooperation depends on the effectiveness and cost of punishment. For intermediate punishment, cooperation can dominate for some parameter values even if the limiting QRE is defection. The results are derived analytically and illustrated with theoretical examples, but no empirical or simulated efficiency or payoff data are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical conditions for when punishment will lead to the selection of a cooperative equilibrium in a 2x2 mini public goods game. While it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it implies that strong punishment can shift equilibrium selection from defection to cooperation, which would be expected to increase efficiency in a public goods context. The results are most directly relevant for games with two players, binary choices, and explicit punishment options, and can inform predictions about the qualitative effect of punishment strength and cost on equilibrium selection, but not quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Quan, J., Yang, X. K., & Wang, X. J. (2018). Continuous spatial public goods game with self and peer punishment based on particle swarm optimization. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that both self-punishment (modeled as a fitness penalty for contributing less than peers) and peer punishment (costly punishment of low contributors by others) substantially increase the average cooperation level in a continuous spatial PGG. Lower tolerance for low contributions and higher punishment intensity both promote higher cooperation. The effect is robust across a range of memory and imitation parameters. However, the paper does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, so it is unclear whether the increased cooperation translates into higher group efficiency, especially given the costs of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that both self-punishment and peer punishment mechanisms can increase average cooperation rates in continuous spatial PGGs, especially when tolerance is low and punishment intensity is high. However, because the main outcome is cooperation rate rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The cost of punishment is modeled and subtracted from individual fitness, but the aggregate effect on group payoff or efficiency is not reported. Thus, while the paper supports the idea that punishment increases cooperation, it does not directly inform whether punishment increases or decreases efficiency in these settings.

- source: Sugiarto, H. S., Chung, N. N., Lai, C. H., & Chew, L. Y. (2015). Socioecological regime shifts in the setting of complex social interactions. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that social ostracism (punishment) can maintain cooperation in a common-pool resource setting, but the resilience of cooperation and the occurrence of regime shifts depend strongly on the structure of the social network. Higher network degree (more social ties) increases the width of the hysteresis curve, making the system more resilient to regime shifts (sudden collapse of cooperation and resources). In sparse networks, regime shifts occur more easily and hysteresis is reduced or absent. The model shows that both social and ecological parameters (e.g., ostracism strength, opportunity cost, resource inflow) can drive regime shifts. However, the main outcomes are the fraction of cooperators and resource levels, not direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that punishment (ostracism) can stabilize cooperation in a public-goods-like environment, and that the effect of punishment is moderated by network structure (player connectivity). However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment is more effective at maintaining cooperation (and thus, by implication, higher group welfare) in denser networks, but the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits its predictive value for the target outcome.

- source: Xu, M., Zheng, D. F., Xu, C., Zhong, L. X., & Hui, P. M. (2015). Cooperative behavior in N-person evolutionary snowdrift games with punishment. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing costly punishment into an N-person snowdrift game (a close variant of the public goods game) can suppress non-cooperators and promote a cooperative population, but only under certain conditions: low cost-to-benefit ratio, large group size, and strong punishment effectiveness. In these cases, the system reaches a 'frozen' phase with only cooperators and punishers. If the punishment is weak, the cost-to-benefit ratio is high, or the group size is small, punishers are eliminated and the system remains a mix of cooperators and non-cooperators. The model is analyzed using replicator dynamics and confirmed by simulation. The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but the elimination of non-cooperators implies higher group performance in those parameter regimes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in N-person public-goods-like games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the steady-state elimination of non-cooperators in the presence of strong punishment, large group size, and low cost-to-benefit ratio suggests that efficiency would be higher in these regimes when punishment is enabled. Conversely, when punishment is weak, costly, or the group is small, punishment does not improve cooperation and likely does not improve efficiency. The findings are most relevant for settings where the game structure is similar to the N-person snowdrift game (cost shared among cooperators, task completed if at least one cooperator exists), which is close but not identical to the standard public goods game.

- source: Nhim, T., Richter, A., & Zhu, X. Q. (2019). The resilience of social norms of cooperation under resource scarcity and inequality - An agent-based model on sharing water over two harvesting seasons. *Ecological Complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that strong punishment (high punishment strength) and high social capital (frequent encounters) support the emergence and resilience of cooperation in a common-pool resource dilemma. However, resource scarcity, high variability in resource inflow, and increased inequality (in land or skill) undermine cooperation, making collapse of social norms more likely. The system tends toward either full cooperation or full defection, with little evidence for stable mixed equilibria. The model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but the collapse of cooperation is interpreted as a breakdown of socially optimal resource use.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that punishment can sustain cooperation in public-goods-like resource dilemmas, but its effectiveness is strongly moderated by resource scarcity, variability, and inequality. The model is not a direct PGG but is structurally similar, with a focus on seasonal water allocation and peer punishment. The main outcome is the presence or absence of cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff, so the paper is most useful for predicting the conditions under which punishment will be effective at sustaining cooperation, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency gains. The findings suggest that in environments with high scarcity, variability, or inequality, the positive effect of punishment on efficiency (if any) is likely to be weaker or absent.

- source: Bshary, R. (2010). Cooperation between unrelated individuals - a game theoretic approach. *Animal Behaviour: Evolution and Mechanisms*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The chapter argues that a wide variety of game-theoretic mechanisms—including punishment, sanctions, partner choice, and reciprocity—are necessary to explain the diversity of cooperative behaviors observed in nature. In n-player (public goods) games, the chapter discusses the tragedy of the commons and the theoretical role of punishment in stabilizing cooperation, especially in humans. It reviews empirical examples and theoretical models but does not provide quantitative data on efficiency or group payoff. The discussion of punishment is detailed, especially in the context of human experiments (citing Fehr & Gächter 2002 and others), but the chapter itself does not present new empirical results or efficiency measurements.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for understanding the conceptual and mechanistic role of punishment in public goods and related games, and for mapping the diversity of game structures and partner control mechanisms. However, it does not provide direct quantitative evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report experimental manipulations of game design dimensions. For prediction tasks, this chapter can inform the expected direction and mechanisms by which punishment may affect cooperation, but not the magnitude or efficiency outcomes under specific game parameterizations.

- source: Clavien, C., & Klein, R. A. (2010). EAGER FOR FAIRNESS OR FOR REVENGE? PSYCHOLOGICAL ALTRUISM IN ECONOMICS. *Economics and Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that while experimental economics and neuroeconomics have revealed robust patterns of altruistic punishment and cooperation in public goods and related games, these studies do not provide decisive evidence about the underlying psychological motives (altruism vs. egoism). Behavioral outcomes such as costly punishment of free-riders are well established, but the interpretation of these actions (e.g., as motivated by fairness, revenge, or self-interest) remains open. Neuroimaging studies (e.g., de Quervain et al. 2004) show reward-related brain activation during punishment, but this does not conclusively distinguish between altruistic and egoistic motives. The paper argues that more fine-grained experimental designs and controls are needed to advance the debate.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or variants. It is useful for understanding the psychological and motivational underpinnings of punishment behavior, but does not report or synthesize data on group efficiency, welfare, or total earnings. For the downstream prediction task, this paper offers conceptual context but no quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency outcomes under punishment. It highlights the need for careful experimental design and the limitations of current evidence, but does not inform the mapping from game design dimensions to efficiency.

- source: Wu, J. H., Luan, S. H., & Raihani, N. (2022). Reward, punishment, and prosocial behavior: Recent developments and implications. *Current Opinion in Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The review finds that both reward and punishment can promote prosocial behavior in public goods games and related social dilemmas, but their effectiveness depends on context. Punishment can sometimes reduce group welfare, especially when it leads to retaliation or is applied under noisy conditions, where it may mistakenly target cooperators and harm group payoffs. Reward is often found to be at least as effective as punishment in promoting cooperation and may be more efficient, particularly under noise. The legitimacy and source of punishment (e.g., third-party or democratic punishment) can moderate its effectiveness. Theoretical models suggest that reward is important for establishing prosociality, while punishment helps maintain it once established, but this dynamic has not been directly tested in experiments. The review also notes that indirect strategies like gossip may be more efficient than punishment in some real-world settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides useful qualitative guidance for predicting the effects of punishment and reward on prosocial behavior and, to a lesser extent, on efficiency in public goods games. It suggests that punishment does not always increase efficiency and can sometimes reduce it, especially under noise or when perceived as illegitimate. Reward may be more efficient in some contexts, particularly when monitoring is imperfect. The review also highlights the importance of the structure and legitimacy of punishment mechanisms. However, because the review does not report quantitative efficiency outcomes or systematically analyze game design dimensions, its value for direct prediction of treatment efficiency is limited to qualitative boundary conditions and theoretical expectations.

- source: Redhead, D., Dhaliwal, N., & Cheng, J. T. (2021). Taking charge and stepping in: Individuals who punish are rewarded with prestige and dominance. *Social and Personality Psychology Compass*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper proposes that high-status individuals (prestige or dominance) are more likely to engage in costly punishment in collective action settings, such as public goods games, because their status lowers the net cost and risk of punishment. Punishment can further increase their prestige or dominance through reputational rewards. The review highlights that punishment is not always altruistic and may be motivated by self-interest, especially when reputational gains are possible. The paper also notes that the net payoff of punishment depends on the balance of reputational gains and costs (including risk of retaliation), and that these dynamics are context-dependent. However, the paper does not provide new empirical data or direct analysis of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and contextual background on the social and reputational mechanisms that may moderate the effects of punishment in public goods and collective action games. It suggests that the effectiveness and cost-benefit of punishment may depend on the status of the punisher, the risk of retaliation, and the cultural or institutional context. However, it does not provide quantitative or direct evidence on how punishment affects efficiency or group payoff, nor does it systematically analyze the impact of specific game design dimensions. Thus, while it is useful for understanding potential moderators and mechanisms, it offers limited direct support for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design parameters.

- source: Griffin, C., & Belmonte, A. (2017). Cyclic public goods games: Compensated coexistence among mutual cheaters stabilized by optimized penalty taxation. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a cyclic public goods game with three species, the system without penalty taxation is unstable and leads to the dominance of a single species, with the interior coexistence point being a point of minimal total population growth (i.e., lowest efficiency). Imposing a penalty tax (punishment) on population growth can stabilize the interior fixed point and allow for coexistence of all species, but this tax is not redistributed and thus reduces overall efficiency further. The optimal tax is the minimal one that achieves stability, but any positive tax reduces total population growth below the already minimal level at the coexistence point. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not include empirical or simulation data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in a cyclic public goods game with three types of mutual cheaters, introducing a penalty tax (punishment) can stabilize coexistence but always reduces total population growth (efficiency) compared to the no-punishment case. The penalty is not redistributed, so it is a pure cost. Thus, in similar game designs, enabling punishment (as a non-redistributive tax) is predicted to decrease efficiency relative to the control. The findings are most relevant for settings with cyclic/multispecies structure and non-redistributive punishment, and less so for standard two-strategy PGGs or for punishment that is redistributed. The results are directly informative for predicting the direction of efficiency change (less positive) when enabling punishment in such models.

- source: Kosfeld, M. (2020). The role of leaders in inducing and maintaining cooperation: The CC strategy. *Leadership Quarterly*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: Drawing on a range of lab and field experiments, the paper finds that: (1) Groups with a higher share of conditional cooperators achieve better efficiency and group outcomes; (2) Punishment of non-cooperation (especially when administered by leaders with prosocial motives) increases group efficiency and cooperation, as shown in field data from Ethiopian forest user groups and lab public goods games; (3) Antisocial punishment by leaders is associated with significantly worse group outcomes; (4) Trust by leaders (as opposed to control) can increase efficiency in principal-agent settings; (5) Self-selection mechanisms can help attract motivated types, further improving group efficiency. The paper provides strong evidence that punishment, when properly targeted and motivated, is a key mechanism for sustaining high efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public-goods-like games will increase group efficiency, especially when punishment is prosocially motivated and not antisocial. The evidence is drawn from both lab and field settings, including real-world resource management and controlled experiments. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by the motives of the punisher (leader or group member), the presence of motivated types, and the structure of the group. For prediction tasks, the paper supports a positive effect of punishment on efficiency, particularly when the punishment technology is effective and antisocial punishment is minimized. The paper also highlights the importance of leader behavior and group composition, which may interact with the game design dimensions.

- source: Bhui, R., Chudek, M., & Henrich, J. (2019). How exploitation launched human cooperation. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that negative indirect reciprocity (NIR)—whereby individuals are allowed to exploit those with bad reputations—can suppress exploitation and support the emergence and maintenance of cooperation and public goods provision. The model shows that cooperation is most stable when exploitation is inefficient (harm to victim > gain to exploiter), and that as the frequency of opportunities for reputation improvement increases, the minimum benefit required for stable cooperation also increases. The model provides explicit conditions under which cooperation is stable, and shows that NIR can serve as a foundation for more complex forms of cooperation. The overall effect of NIR is to increase group efficiency and welfare by reducing exploitation and supporting norm adherence, especially in early or cognitively simple societies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms (here, tolerated exploitation of ill-reputed individuals) can increase group efficiency and public goods provision, especially when exploitation is inefficient and when opportunities for norm adherence are not too frequent. The model gives explicit payoff-based conditions for when cooperation is stable, which can inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. The findings suggest that punishment is more likely to improve efficiency when the cost to the victim of exploitation is high relative to the benefit to the exploiter, and when errors in reputation assignment are low. The model also highlights that as norm adherence becomes more mandatory and frequent, the requirements for stable cooperation become stricter, potentially reducing the positive effect of punishment on efficiency in some parameter regimes.

- source: Powers, S. T., & Lehmann, L. (2017). When is bigger better? The effects of group size on the evolution of helping behaviours. *Biological Reviews*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that as group size increases, the marginal benefits of helping, the effectiveness of reciprocity, and relatedness all tend to decrease, making cooperation less likely to evolve or be stable in large groups. Punishment can stabilize cooperation, but its effectiveness also declines with group size unless institutional mechanisms are in place. The authors argue that human societies have overcome these limitations through the evolution of institutions that coordinate sanctioning and information sharing, thereby enabling large-scale cooperation. The paper provides a conceptual framework for understanding how game design features (such as group size, punishment, and information structure) interact to affect the evolution and maintenance of cooperation, but does not provide quantitative predictions or new data on efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding the qualitative effects of group size and institutional punishment on cooperation and efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that, all else equal, increasing group size will tend to reduce the effectiveness of both reciprocity and punishment in sustaining cooperation, unless institutional mechanisms are present to coordinate sanctioning and information. The paper highlights the importance of considering diminishing marginal returns, monitoring costs, and second-order free-riding in large groups. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the expectation that punishment will be less effective at increasing efficiency in larger groups unless institutional features (e.g., centralized punishment, coordinated monitoring) are present. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates or experimental data for efficiency outcomes, so its support is primarily conceptual and mechanistic.

- source: Zhou, K., & Ren, T. Y. (2021). Low-carbon technology collaborative innovation in industrial cluster with social exclusion: An evolutionary game theory perspective. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a social exclusion mechanism (where excluders pay a cost to expel defectors from the public goods game) can significantly increase the level of cooperation in a networked public goods environment. The effect is stronger when the cost of exclusion is low and the probability of successful exclusion is high. Sparse network structures (low average degree) are more conducive to sustaining cooperation. Voluntary participation (allowing loners) also helps prevent defection from dominating. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, focusing instead on the prevalence of cooperative strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms (here, social exclusion) can increase cooperation rates in public-goods-like environments, especially when the cost of exclusion is low and the network is sparse. However, because the model and results focus on behavioral outcomes (cooperation level) rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The model's structure and parameter explorations may inform qualitative expectations about when punishment is more likely to be effective, but do not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes for the prediction task.

- source: Song, Q., Cao, Z. H., Tao, R., Jiang, W., Liu, C., & Liu, J. Z. (2020). Conditional neutral punishment promotes cooperation in the spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that language and symbolic communication fundamentally alter the possibilities for collective action by simplifying coordination, reducing the costs of monitoring and punishing free riders, and amplifying reputation and signaling effects. It reviews theoretical models and empirical studies (from both ethnography and experiments) showing that communication (including chat, gossip, and feedback) increases cooperation rates and the effectiveness of punishment in social dilemmas. However, it does not report direct measures of efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with and without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that enabling communication (chat, language, gossip) in public goods games and collective action settings increases cooperation and the effectiveness of punishment, which should, in principle, improve group outcomes and efficiency. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates or direct empirical evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it is not directly usable for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of communication and norm establishment as moderators of punishment effectiveness.

  findings: The paper argues that mechanisms such as punishment and reward, studied extensively in human public goods games, are also relevant in non-human animal cooperation. It reviews how laboratory studies (often using public goods or n-player social dilemma games) have inspired comparative research, and how features like punishment, partner choice, and communication can affect cooperation rates. The authors emphasize the need for more ecologically valid experiments and for understanding how laboratory findings translate to real-world helping and cooperation. The review notes that while punishment and reward can increase cooperation in public goods games, most evidence is about behavioral outcomes, not efficiency or group payoff.

It is useful for understanding the mechanisms and moderators that may influence the effect of punishment on cooperation, but cannot be used to predict efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions or control efficiency. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of ecological validity, the role of communication, and the need to consider real-world payoff structures.

  findings: The paper finds that experimental economics is a valuable tool for understanding behavior in environmental and development contexts, especially regarding cooperation, public goods provision, and the effects of punishment and peer enforcement. It reviews evidence that peer punishment (monetary and non-monetary) can increase contributions in public goods games, and that communication can sometimes be more effective than regulation. The paper also notes that the effectiveness of punishment and enforcement depends on social context, group composition, and cultural factors. However, it does not provide new quantitative findings on efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in public goods games.

However, it does not provide direct quantitative evidence or efficiency outcomes that can be used for prediction. Its main value is in summarizing the mechanisms and contextual factors that may moderate the effect of punishment, such as group composition, communication, and cultural context. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that punishment is generally associated with higher cooperation, but does not quantify the efficiency impact or provide game design-specific results.

- source: Raihani, N. J., & Power, E. A. (2021). No good deed goes unpunished: the social costs of prosocial behaviour. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that prosocial acts can incur social costs, especially when observers infer self-interested motives or feel disadvantaged by others' generosity. It reviews evidence that in public goods games and similar environments, cooperators can be punished (antisocial punishment) or evicted, and that overtly generous individuals may be derogated or disliked. The review suggests that these social costs can limit the emergence and scale of cooperation, and that reputation management strategies (such as modesty or anonymity) are often used to avoid such costs. The paper does not provide quantitative or direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, but highlights the importance of context, observer motives, and cultural variation in shaping responses to prosocial behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides important theoretical context for understanding when punishment or sanctions may have counterproductive effects in public goods games and related environments, particularly through antisocial punishment of cooperators. However, it does not provide quantitative or direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in a specific game design. It does suggest that the presence of antisocial punishment and reputational risks may reduce the positive impact of punishment on cooperation and efficiency, especially in contexts where observers feel threatened or infer strategic motives. For prediction tasks, this review supports the idea that punishment does not always increase efficiency and that context and social perception are important moderators.

  findings: The paper argues that a large body of experimental evidence supports the existence of other-regarding preferences, such as altruistic punishment, in games like the public goods game with punishment. It reviews findings that low contributors are frequently punished, even in late rounds and in stranger treatments, indicating that punishment is used to enforce cooperation. The authors also discuss the robustness of these behaviors to changes in stakes and context, and the importance of social norms. However, the paper does not present new data or directly analyze efficiency or group payoff; instead, it focuses on the behavioral mechanisms and theoretical implications.

It is useful for understanding the mechanisms and robustness of punishment and cooperation, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that punishment is likely to increase cooperation, but does not allow for direct inference about efficiency or payoff changes.

- source: Pal, S., & Hilbe, C. (2022). Reputation effects drive the joint evolution of cooperation and social rewarding. *Nature Communications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that reputation effects are crucial for the evolution of cooperation and social rewarding in PGG-like environments. When individuals can observe or infer others' rewarding strategies (i.e., reputation is possible), social rewarding and cooperation co-evolve and are stable, leading to higher group payoffs and efficiency. Without reputation, cooperation and rewarding do not emerge, and antisocial rewarding may arise under certain conditions (e.g., high assortment or mutually beneficial rewards). In multiplayer (PGG) settings, similar results hold: reputation enables stable cooperation and social rewarding, while its absence leads to defection and low efficiency. The model provides explicit conditions under which cooperation and rewarding are payoff-maximizing, and shows that the presence of reputation mechanisms is a key moderator of efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling reputation effects (e.g., making rewarding behavior observable) in PGG-like games will increase efficiency when social rewarding is possible. The model directly links game design dimensions such as group size, marginal per-capita return, reward cost and effectiveness, and information structure (reputation) to the emergence of efficient cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, the paper suggests that the presence of observable rewarding (reputation), sufficiently high reward effectiveness relative to cost, and the ability to target rewards to cooperators are critical for efficiency gains from reward mechanisms. The absence of reputation or the presence of antisocial rewarding can undermine efficiency. While the paper does not model punishment directly, it provides indirect evidence that the structure and observability of incentives (including punishment, by analogy) are key moderators of efficiency effects.

- source: Ohtsuki, H. (2004). Reactive strategies in indirect reciprocity. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in indirect reciprocity settings with public-goods-game-type payoffs, discriminating strategies (which conditionally cooperate based on others' past behavior, akin to punishment of defectors) are not stable evolutionary outcomes. Instead, populations tend to evolve toward unconditional cooperation (All C) or defection (All D), depending on whether the expected benefit of cooperation (modulated by information transfer and continuation probability) exceeds the cost. Discriminators can promote cooperation transiently by increasing the average 'discriminatoriness' in the population, but are ultimately outcompeted by more generous strategies. The paper also shows that errors (in perception or implementation) further destabilize discriminating strategies, making unconditional cooperation or defection more likely. The main outcomes are expressed in terms of average payoff (efficiency), and the model provides explicit threshold conditions for the evolution of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in public-goods-game-like environments with indirect reciprocity, strategies that punish defectors (by refusing to cooperate with them) do not lead to stable increases in group efficiency or payoff. Instead, such strategies are only transient promoters of cooperation, and populations tend to evolve toward unconditional cooperation or defection depending on the benefit-to-cost ratio and information transfer. The model does not include explicit costly punishment as in standard PGG experiments, so its direct predictive value for the effect of enabling punishment in a PGG is limited. However, it suggests that punishment-like strategies may not robustly increase efficiency unless additional mechanisms (e.g., standing, justified punishment) are present. For prediction tasks, this paper implies that simply enabling punishment (in the form of conditional cooperation) may not reliably increase efficiency, especially in the presence of errors.

- source: Nyborg, K. (2018). Reciprocal climate negotiators. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that reciprocal preferences can, under certain conditions (strong and widespread reciprocity), stabilize the grand coalition in a public-goods-like coalition game, leading to high group welfare (efficiency). If only a minority of countries are reciprocal, or if reciprocity is weak, the only stable coalitions are small and provide little welfare improvement over the non-cooperative baseline. The model demonstrates that the presence of reciprocal preferences can shift the game from a pure Prisoner's Dilemma to a coordination game in utilities, but this does not guarantee high efficiency unless reciprocity is strong and common. The paper does not model explicit punishment or sanctioning institutions, but reciprocity can act as a form of endogenous social sanctioning. The welfare/efficiency effects of reciprocity are thus mixed: potentially large if conditions are right, but often minimal in realistic parameter ranges.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that social preferences (reciprocity) can, in principle, increase efficiency in public-goods-like coalition games, but only if they are strong and widespread. In the absence of explicit punishment mechanisms, reciprocity alone is not a reliable path to high efficiency unless the population is highly reciprocal. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling social preference mechanisms (like reciprocity) may only improve efficiency if the relevant parameters (strength and prevalence of reciprocity) are high. The model does not address explicit punishment, so its direct predictive value for punishment-enabled games is limited, but it does show that endogenous social preferences can sometimes substitute for formal sanctions in supporting cooperation and efficiency. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is implemented via social preferences rather than institutionalized mechanisms.

- source: Schonmann, R. H., & Boyd, R. (2016). A simple rule for the evolution of contingent cooperation in large groups. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that contingent cooperation in large groups can invade and persist if relatedness is above a threshold that depends sensitively on the threshold fraction of cooperators required for cooperation to be sustained. The required relatedness increases rapidly as the threshold for cooperation increases. The number of repeated interactions (iterations) required for cooperation to invade also increases rapidly with the threshold and decreases with relatedness. The model shows that, under realistic levels of relatedness, only strategies that require cooperation from a small fraction of the group can invade; strategies that require near-universal cooperation are unlikely to evolve. The paper argues that contingent punishment (not formally modeled here) is a more plausible mechanism for the evolution of widespread cooperation, because even a small fraction of punishers can induce high levels of cooperation. The results are supported by mathematical derivations and numerical simulations of evolutionary dynamics in group-structured populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how group size, relatedness, and the threshold for contingent cooperation affect the evolutionary success (and thus efficiency) of cooperation in public-goods-like environments. It does not directly model or analyze punishment, but its results suggest that punishment-based mechanisms are more likely to support high efficiency in large groups than pure contingent cooperation. For prediction tasks, the paper's formulas can be used to estimate the conditions under which cooperation (and thus high efficiency) can be sustained, given the group structure and behavioral rules. However, because the paper does not model explicit punishment or compare efficiency with and without punishment, it provides only indirect support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Heintzelman, M. D., Salant, S. W., & Schott, S. (2009). Putting free-riding to work: A Partnership Solution to the common-property problem. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals form partnerships that share revenue equally, the resulting free-riding reduces excessive effort in common-property problems, potentially achieving the social optimum in aggregate payoff. The number of partnerships is a key design parameter: too many partnerships (everyone solo) leads to excessive effort, while too few (everyone in one group) leads to under-provision. An intermediate, endogenously determined number of partnerships can induce the efficient outcome. The model shows that this solution is stable under certain conditions (e.g., when solo production is sufficiently disadvantageous). The Partnership Solution can outperform standard regulatory interventions in terms of participant welfare. The paper generalizes the result to various sharing rules and to cases with heterogeneous agents.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that institutional design—specifically, the structure of group formation and revenue sharing—can have large, predictable effects on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. While it does not study punishment per se, it shows that endogenous free-riding within partnerships can substitute for external sanctions in curbing over-extraction and maximizing welfare. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that efficiency can be increased by mechanisms that induce free-riding when the baseline is excessive effort, and that the number and size of groups (player_count, partnership structure) are critical moderators. However, the absence of explicit punishment means the results are most directly relevant to settings where group structure, not punishment, is the main intervention.

- source: Richter, A., & Grasman, J. (2013). The transmission of sustainable harvesting norms when agents are conditionally cooperative. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the presence of conditional cooperators in a common-pool resource setting can lead to complex and sometimes abrupt transitions between cooperative and non-cooperative equilibria. Efficiency, measured as resource stock and group profit relative to the maximum economic yield, is highly sensitive to social dynamics. When temptation to defect increases or when group size increases, the system can shift from high-efficiency (cooperative) to low-efficiency (defective) states, sometimes with hysteresis or limit cycles. The model highlights that social norm enforcement via moral persuasion can sustain cooperation, but is vulnerable to collapse if conditional cooperators lose motivation. The absence of explicit punishment means that norm enforcement is 'soft' and collapses are possible even without changes in external parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in public-goods-like common-pool resource games, the efficiency of group outcomes is highly sensitive to the social composition (intrinsic vs. conditional cooperators vs. defectors) and to the strength of norm enforcement mechanisms. While it does not model explicit punishment, it suggests that in the absence of strong enforcement (e.g., costly punishment), efficiency is fragile and can collapse due to endogenous social dynamics. For prediction tasks, this paper implies that enabling or strengthening norm enforcement (potentially via punishment) could stabilize high-efficiency outcomes, especially in larger groups or when temptation to defect is high. However, the model also warns that even with some enforcement, efficiency can be lost if social dynamics shift. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is absent or weak, and thus provide a baseline for understanding the potential value of adding punishment.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a closed community harvesting a renewable resource, cooperation can be sustained through social norm contagion, but is vulnerable to collapse if the temptation to defect becomes too strong (e.g., due to increased population or technology). The system can flip from a high-efficiency (cooperative) equilibrium to a low-efficiency (defective) equilibrium, with substantial welfare loss. Hysteresis is present: reversing the parameter change that caused collapse is not sufficient to restore cooperation. The model provides explicit formulas for welfare under cooperation and Nash equilibrium, showing large efficiency losses after collapse. The presence of social norm diffusion (but not explicit punishment) can sustain cooperation up to a tipping point.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding the efficiency dynamics of common-pool resource games with social norm diffusion, but it does not model explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its results are most relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where cooperation is maintained by social pressure, persuasion, or contagion, rather than by costly punishment. The model shows that efficiency can be high and robust up to a tipping point, after which collapse is sudden and efficiency is lost. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong evidence about the role of population size, technology, and social norm strength in determining efficiency, but does not provide direct evidence about the effect of enabling or disabling punishment. It is best used as a theoretical baseline for environments without explicit punishment.

- source: Brown, S. P., & Taylor, P. D. (2010). Joint evolution of multiple social traits: a kin selection analysis. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that when social traits such as public good production and exploitation co-evolve, their interactions can lead to complex evolutionary dynamics, including bistability between efficient (social) and inefficient (asocial) outcomes. The efficiency of the group (measured as total payoff or welfare) depends on the joint equilibrium of these traits, which is sensitive to relatedness, the presence of independent public good production, and the nature of trait interactions (synergy or interference). In some cases, higher relatedness increases both cooperation and exploitation, with ambiguous effects on efficiency. The model highlights that multidimensional social dilemmas can have multiple stable outcomes, and small changes in parameters or initial conditions can lead to large differences in group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how the design dimensions of a public goods game—especially those affecting trait interactions, relatedness, and the possibility of multiple social traits—can lead to non-linear and sometimes counterintuitive effects on group efficiency when punishment-like or enforcement traits are present. While the model does not directly analyze punishment in the standard PGG sense, it shows that enforcement or exploitation traits can interact with cooperation to produce bistability or runaway dynamics. For prediction, this suggests that enabling punishment or enforcement mechanisms may not always increase efficiency; the effect depends on the interaction structure, initial conditions, and whether the system is in a region of parameter space that supports social or asocial equilibria. The findings caution against simple extrapolation from one-dimensional models and highlight the importance of considering multidimensional trait interactions.

- source: Quan, J., Zheng, J. J., Wang, X. J., & Yang, X. K. (2019). Benefits of asynchronous exclusion for the evolution of cooperation in stochastic evolutionary optional public goods games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that asynchronous exclusion (where excluders act sequentially and avoid redundant costs) is more effective at promoting cooperation than synchronous exclusion (where all excluders act simultaneously and pay redundant costs). This effect is strongest when the probability of exclusion success is high and the public goods multiplier is low (well-mixed) or intermediate (structured). The main reported outcomes are the probability of the system reaching a cooperative state and the frequency of different strategies at equilibrium. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency effects of punishment-like mechanisms in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effectiveness of exclusion (a sanctioning mechanism) in promoting cooperation in optional public goods games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the increased probability of reaching cooperative states under asynchronous exclusion suggests that, under some parameter regimes, more effective or less costly sanctioning can increase cooperation rates. However, because efficiency is not measured, and because exclusion differs from standard punishment (it removes defectors from benefit sharing rather than imposing a direct cost), the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in standard PGGs. The results are most relevant for settings where exclusion is the main sanction and where the cost structure of sanctioning can be varied.

- source: Chen, Q., Chen, T., & Wang, Y. J. (2016). How the expanded crowd-funding mechanism of some southern rural areas in China affects cooperative behaviors in threshold public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that both publishing the list of cooperators and the presence of lobbyists can significantly increase the number of cooperators in a threshold public goods game, especially when the synergy factor is not too high. The effectiveness of these mechanisms depends on the population structure (proportion of unconditional cooperators vs. defectors), the synergy factor, and emotional influence parameters. When the synergy factor is high, publishing the list is always beneficial and the role of lobbyists becomes negligible. When the synergy factor is low, the decision to publish the list should consider the relative numbers of cooperators and defectors and the strength of emotional inhibition. The paper does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how social recognition (publishing the list) and persuasive agents (lobbyists) can increase cooperation rates in threshold public goods games, especially under certain parameter regimes. However, because it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment or similar mechanisms on efficiency. The findings may be useful for understanding the behavioral pathways by which social mechanisms affect cooperation, but should be used with caution for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Du, J. M., Wu, B., & Wang, L. (2014). Climate collective risk dilemma with feedback of real-time temperatures. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The model demonstrates that coupling real-time environmental feedback (temperature) to payoffs in a threshold public goods game can promote and stabilize cooperation. When the public good threshold is not met, temperature rises, which reduces all players' payoffs (environmental punishment). This feedback leads to cyclical dynamics where cooperation rebounds as payoffs fall, stabilizing temperature near a reference level. The model outperforms versions without feedback in both temperature control and cooperation rates. However, the model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome, and the 'punishment' is environmental rather than peer-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is only indirectly relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The 'punishment' mechanism is environmental (temperature rise reducing payoffs for all), not a peer or institutional punishment option. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but shows that environmental feedback can stabilize cooperation and temperature. The findings suggest that automatic, group-level sanctions (environmental feedback) can promote cooperation, but the results may not transfer directly to settings with explicit punishment options. Use this paper for contextual or mechanistic insight, not for direct quantitative prediction.

- source: Hurlstone, M. J., Wang, S., Price, A., Leviston, Z., & Walker, I. (2017). Cooperation studies of catastrophe avoidance: implications for climate negotiations. *Climatic Change*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation in collective-risk social dilemma games is strongly affected by perceived risk, inequality, uncertainty about thresholds, intergenerational discounting, and the possibility of reputation-based rewards or punishments. High perceived risk, communication (pledges), intermediate targets, and reputation mechanisms can increase cooperation (i.e., the proportion of groups that avert catastrophe). Inequality and uncertainty about the threshold for catastrophe reduce cooperation. The paper discusses the need for credible enforcement mechanisms (punishment) in real-world climate agreements but does not review experiments that directly implement or vary punishment in the game. The main findings are about behavioral cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence about the importance of enforcement (punishment) mechanisms for sustaining cooperation in public-goods-like threshold games, but does not provide direct experimental evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff. It is useful for understanding moderators of cooperation and the conceptual role of punishment, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in a public goods game. For the prediction task, this paper suggests that credible punishment mechanisms are likely to be important, especially under uncertainty, but does not provide data or effect sizes.

- source: Aktipis, A. (2016). Principles of cooperation across systems: from human sharing to multicellularity and cancer. *Evolutionary Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper identifies three general principles that facilitate cooperation: (1) the ability to 'Walk Away' from uncooperative partners, (2) need-based resource transfers, and (3) the necessity of increasingly sophisticated cheater suppression mechanisms as cooperation scales up. It reviews agent-based models (from prior work) showing that conditional movement (Walk Away) can stabilize cooperation in both dyadic and group contexts, including public goods game-like environments. The paper discusses how positive assortment (cooperators interacting more with each other) is key to the evolution of cooperation. It also highlights the challenges of cheater suppression in large-scale systems and draws analogies between human societies, multicellular organisms, and social insects. However, it does not provide new empirical or experimental results on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it directly analyze the effects of punishment mechanisms in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical background on mechanisms that support cooperation, such as partner choice (Walk Away) and cheater suppression, which are relevant to understanding the effects of punishment in public goods games. However, it does not provide direct empirical or quantitative evidence on how punishment affects efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their variants. The discussion of cheater suppression includes punishment as one possible mechanism but is not specific or detailed enough to inform quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The most relevant insight is that as cooperative systems scale up, more sophisticated and costly cheater suppression mechanisms (including punishment) are required, but the paper does not specify the efficiency consequences of these mechanisms in controlled game environments.

- source: Centola, D. (2013). A Simple Model of Stability in Critical Mass Dynamics. *Journal of Statistical Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that weakly self-reinforcing collective action systems (where incentives to participate peak before full participation, allowing for free riders) are more robust to perturbations and have greater long-term stability than strongly self-reinforcing systems (where incentives increase monotonically with participation and free riding is eliminated). Strongly self-reinforcing systems can achieve higher participation but are more vulnerable to collapse from stochastic shocks. The presence of free riders in weakly self-reinforcing systems provides a reserve of potential contributors that stabilizes participation when it drops. The analysis is theoretical and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into the trade-off between maximizing participation (and thus, potentially, efficiency) and ensuring long-term stability in collective action systems. While it does not directly address efficiency or payoff outcomes, it suggests that systems designed to eliminate free riding (e.g., via strong incentives or possibly punishment) may be less stable, and thus may not always achieve higher long-term group benefit. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that the relationship between punishment (or strong incentives) and efficiency may be moderated by stability concerns, but does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence for efficiency effects. The model is most relevant for understanding the dynamics of participation and the potential unintended consequences of interventions aimed at increasing cooperation.

- source: Hu, L. W., He, N. R., Weng, Q. F., Chen, X. J., & Perc, M. (2020). Rewarding endowments lead to a win-win in the evolution of public cooperation and the accumulation of common resources. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: Rewarding endowments in a collective-risk social dilemma (a PGG variant with environmental feedback) can create a win-win for cooperation and resource accumulation, but only in a specific parameter region: low basic endowment and intermediate reward intensity. High reward intensity or high basic endowment can lead to resource exhaustion, even if cooperation is high. The model does not include punishment or sanctions. The main outcomes are the amount of common resources (group welfare) and a composite index of cooperation and resource accumulation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games, as it studies only reward mechanisms and does not include punishment or sanctions. However, it provides evidence that reward mechanisms can increase both cooperation and group welfare, but only under certain parameter regimes. The findings suggest that the design of incentive mechanisms (reward intensity, basic endowment) can have non-monotonic effects on group efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding the effects of reward as a moderator or alternative.

- source: Brown, S. P., & Taddei, F. (2007). The Durability of Public Goods Changes the Dynamics and Nature of Social Dilemmas. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that when the public good is durable (i.e., changes slowly relative to the frequency of cooperators), the dynamics of cooperation and group payoff can be substantially altered compared to classic models. For example, durable public goods can lead to multi-generational oscillations in cooperation and payoff, shift the thresholds for invasion and stability of cooperation, and even allow for transient increases in cooperation in settings where defection would otherwise dominate. The explicit modeling of public good dynamics reveals that group payoff and efficiency are not solely determined by current cooperation rates, but also by the history and durability of the public good. However, the paper does not address punishment, sanctions, or any intervention mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for understanding how the dynamics and durability of the public good itself can affect efficiency and group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. It highlights that efficiency is not always a simple function of current cooperation rates, especially when the public good is durable or slow to change. However, because the paper does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is most relevant for understanding baseline dynamics and for considering how lagged or durable public goods might interact with other interventions in future models.

- source: Wang, X. J., Ding, R., Zhao, J. H., Chen, W. M., & Gu, C. L. (2022). Competition of punishment and reward among inequity-averse individuals in spatial public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that reputation-based indirect reciprocity can promote the spread of donation and cooperation behaviors in a donation game variant of the PGG, but this effect is limited by the costs of donation and cooperation, and by the presence of 'hypocrite' and 'realist' strategies. When only extreme altruists and egoists are present, cooperation rarely persists. The introduction of shunners (who conditionally cooperate based on reputation) allows for the spread of donation and cooperation under certain cost regimes. However, the addition of hypocrites (who donate for reputation but defect in daily interactions) and realists (who do not donate but cooperate in daily interactions) undermines the prevalence of donation, leading to more complex phase diagrams and sometimes the collapse of donation behavior. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of different strategies and the qualitative structure of phase transitions; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments, as it explicitly excludes punishment mechanisms and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it is informative about the role of indirect reciprocity (reputation) as an alternative to punishment in promoting cooperation, and about how the presence of certain strategy types and cost parameters can affect the prevalence of prosocial behaviors. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that mechanisms other than punishment (e.g., reputation) can support cooperation under some conditions, but also that the presence of exploitative or non-donating cooperators can undermine these effects. The findings are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Chen, X. J., & Perc, M. (2014). Excessive abundance of common resources deters social responsibility. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation in a collective-risk social dilemma is most sustainable when the initial amount of common resources and the effectiveness of cooperative contributions are at intermediate levels. Excessive abundance of resources or overly effective cooperation allows defectors to free-ride, leading to the eventual collapse of cooperation and resource depletion. Conversely, too little resource or ineffective cooperation also leads to collapse. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The main reported outcomes are the stationary fraction of cooperators and the sustainability of the common resource pool, not direct efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as defined in the prediction task. Its findings about resource abundance and cooperation may be contextually informative for understanding resource dynamics, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Hadzibeganovic, T., Lima, F. W. S., & Stauffer, D. (2012). Evolution of tag-mediated altruistic behavior in one-shot encounters on large-scale complex networks. *Computer Physics Communications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
