  findings: The study finds that tag-mediated cooperation can emerge and temporarily dominate in one-shot, multiplayer PD-like games on complex networks, especially when agents can recognize and preferentially help those with similar tags. The dominance of specific cooperative strategies (altruist vs. ethnocentric) depends on network topology (rewiring probability), but over long timescales, all strategies tend to equilibrate. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the prevalence of cooperation and strategy types.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It is relevant for understanding how tag-based recognition and network structure can support or suppress cooperation in the absence of punishment, but does not address payoff-based outcomes or the impact of sanctions. It may provide weak contextual information about baseline cooperation in one-shot, multiplayer PD-like environments without punishment, but is not informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Han, T. A. (2022). Institutional incentives for the evolution of committed cooperation: ensuring participation is as important as enhancing compliance. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional incentives (punishment or reward) can promote the evolution of cooperation and commitment compliance in a social dilemma with a prior commitment stage. Reward is generally more effective than punishment at promoting cooperation, especially when the cost of commitment is high or the benefit-to-cost ratio is large. Allocating part of the incentive budget to encourage participation in the commitment (not just compliance) further increases cooperation, particularly when participation is costly. The presence of noise in participation decisions can also stabilize cooperation. The results are robust across a range of parameter values and are based on explicit payoff calculations and evolutionary stability analysis.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in social dilemma games with a prior commitment stage, enabling punishment (or reward) increases group efficiency (cooperation frequency) compared to no incentives, provided the incentive budget is sufficient. Reward is more effective than punishment, especially as the cost of participation increases. Allocating part of the incentive budget to encourage participation (not just compliance) further increases efficiency, especially when participation is costly. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in games with a commitment or opt-in stage, and the model's explicit payoff calculations can be mapped to efficiency outcomes. However, the model is based on a two-player, one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma with a commitment stage, not a standard repeated public goods game, so predictions should be transferred with caution to multi-player, repeated settings.

- source: Haag, M., & Lagunoff, R. (2006). Social norms, local interaction, and neighborhood planning. *International Economic Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency of repeated cooperation enforced by punishment depends critically on the network structure and the distribution of individual patience (discount factors). In maximally connected cliques, punishment is most effective due to perfect monitoring, and full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency) is sustainable if all individuals are sufficiently patient. However, when some individuals are impatient (low discount factor), cliques can lead to cascades of defection and lower welfare. In such cases, less connected networks (e.g., grids) can isolate the effects of uncooperative types, allowing more tolerant individuals to absorb free riding and maintain higher overall welfare. The optimal network design thus balances the benefits of effective punishment (which requires connectivity and observability) against the costs of social conflict from connecting intolerant and uncooperative types. The paper provides explicit formulas for expected welfare under different network designs and type distributions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in repeated local public goods or Prisoner's Dilemma environments as a function of network structure, monitoring, and heterogeneity in patience. It shows that punishment is most effective (and efficiency is highest) in small, maximally connected groups with perfect monitoring and homogeneous patience. As heterogeneity increases, or as monitoring becomes more local/imperfect, the efficiency gains from punishment can be reduced or even reversed, with less connected networks sometimes yielding higher welfare. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by network connectivity, monitoring structure, and the distribution of patience among players. However, the results are theoretical and assume rational, forward-looking agents using trigger strategies; they do not provide empirical effect sizes.

- source: Han, T. A., Duong, M. H., & Perc, M. (2024). Evolutionary mechanisms that promote cooperation may not promote social welfare. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary models of the Prisoner's Dilemma with peer or institutional incentives, punishment mechanisms (both peer and institutional) are often detrimental to social welfare, even when they increase cooperation. Peer punishment increases cooperation more than peer reward, but peer reward leads to higher social welfare. For institutional incentives, reward achieves positive social welfare across a broader range of parameters than punishment, even when both achieve similar cooperation levels. There is often an optimal, moderate level of incentive impact for maximizing social welfare, rather than maximizing cooperation or punishment strength. These findings are robust across mutation rates, selection intensities, and game configurations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling punishment in public-goods-game-like environments (here, the Prisoner's Dilemma) is likely to reduce efficiency (social welfare) compared to reward mechanisms, even if punishment increases cooperation. The effect is robust across a range of model parameters. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment (especially with high impact-to-cost ratio) should be expected to have a less positive or even negative effect on efficiency relative to control, unless the punishment is extremely cost-effective and moderate in impact. The findings suggest that maximizing cooperation via punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency, and that reward mechanisms are generally preferable for social welfare. The results are based on theoretical models, not empirical data, but are directly relevant to the efficiency prediction task.

- source: Feng, T. Y., Tai, S. L., Sun, C. S., & Man, Q. P. (2017). Study on Cooperative Mechanism of Prefabricated Producers Based on Evolutionary Game Theory. *Mathematical Problems in Engineering*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper models cooperation between two prefabricated producers using evolutionary game theory. It finds that, in the absence of external mechanisms, both mutual cooperation and mutual defection are stable equilibria, and the outcome depends on initial conditions. Introducing a punishment mechanism (where defectors are fined and the fine is transferred to the cooperator) or a sufficiently large incentive (reward) for cooperation changes the game so that only mutual cooperation is stable, provided the punishment or reward exceeds a threshold related to the cost of cooperation. This leads to a win-win outcome and maximizes group efficiency. The analysis is theoretical and does not provide empirical data, but it directly addresses the effect of punishment on group payoff and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in a two-player repeated game with payoff structure similar to a stag hunt or coordination game, introducing a sufficiently strong punishment mechanism (where defectors are fined and the fine is transferred to the cooperator) ensures that mutual cooperation is the only stable outcome, maximizing group efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar settings, enabling punishment (with sufficient strength) will reliably increase efficiency from potentially low (if initial conditions favor defection) to maximal (full cooperation). However, the model is limited to two players, does not include stochasticity or empirical heterogeneity, and assumes rational updating. The findings are most applicable to small, symmetric, repeated games with clear punishment transfer.

- source: Han, T. A., Pereira, L. M., Lenaerts, T., & Santos, F. C. (2021). Mediating artificial intelligence developments through negative and positive incentives. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that both punishment (sanctions) and rewards can increase group welfare and promote safety compliance in a repeated innovation race game, but their effects depend on the underlying risk and incentive parameters. Punishment is generally more effective at suppressing unsafe (defective) behavior and increasing group welfare in the 'dilemma' region where individual and collective interests diverge, but if punishment is too strong or too efficient, it can lead to over-regulation and reduce innovation even when risk is low and unsafe behavior would be collectively optimal. Rewards can also promote safety and welfare, especially when highly cost-efficient, and do not suffer from the risk of over-regulation, but are generally less effective than punishment. The model provides explicit conditions for when each incentive is beneficial or harmful for group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like innovation races is highly context-dependent. If the risk of disaster from unsafe behavior is high, punishment increases efficiency by promoting safe behavior. If the risk is low, strong punishment can reduce efficiency by suppressing innovation. The cost-effectiveness of punishment (punishment cost and impact) is critical: punishment is most beneficial when its cost to the punisher is low relative to its impact on the punished. Reward mechanisms can also increase efficiency, especially when highly cost-effective, and are less likely to reduce efficiency through over-regulation. For prediction tasks, the model suggests that the effect of enabling punishment depends on the risk/benefit structure, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the baseline efficiency of the control game. The findings are most directly applicable to innovation race or winner-take-all settings, but the mechanisms are adjacent to public goods games.

- source: Murase, Y., & Baek, S. K. (2020). Five rules for friendly rivalry in direct reciprocity. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that strategies combining partner and rival features (friendly rivals) exist in the repeated prisoner's dilemma, with the CAPRI strategy as a prime example. CAPRI achieves mutual cooperation (maximal efficiency) when both players use it, and punishes defection to prevent exploitation. Evolutionary simulations show that CAPRI dominates other strategies in terms of abundance and average payoff across a wide range of benefit-to-cost ratios, error rates, and population sizes. The paper also shows that memory length (m >= 3) is crucial for such strategies. While the main results are for the two-player PD, the authors note that similar strategies exist for the three-person public goods game, suggesting potential generalizability.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that punishment strategies designed to be both efficient and defensible (like CAPRI) can substantially increase group efficiency and average payoff in repeated prisoner's dilemma settings, and by extension, in closely related public goods games. The findings suggest that, when punishment is implemented with sufficient memory and recovery mechanisms, it can robustly improve efficiency regardless of population size, error rate, or benefit-to-cost ratio. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the view that enabling well-designed punishment in repeated games will increase efficiency, especially when players can remember and respond to past actions. However, the results are most directly applicable to two-player repeated PDs, with only indirect evidence for multi-player PG games.

- source: Skatova, A., & Ferguson, E. (2011). What makes people cooperate? Individual differences in BAS/BIS predict strategic reciprocation in a public goods game. *Personality and Individual Differences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial prisoner's dilemma with super cooperators (who both punish defectors and reward cooperators), the ability of speculative defectors to identify and cooperate with super cooperators (i.e., higher information level) generally increases the prevalence of super cooperators and raises average social payoff (efficiency), provided the cost of punishment/reward is not too high. When the cost is high, increasing information can instead destroy super cooperators. The alliance between speculative defectors and super cooperators can insulate super cooperators from exploitation by unconditional cooperators, further supporting cooperation and efficiency. The model demonstrates that the introduction of punishment/reward mechanisms and information about others' strategies can substantially improve group efficiency compared to the baseline with only unconditional cooperators and defectors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in spatially structured prisoner's dilemma environments, enabling punishment (and reward) mechanisms—especially when paired with higher information about others' strategies—can increase group efficiency, as measured by average social payoff. The effect is strongest when the cost of punishment/reward is moderate or low. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in games with similar structure, though the model is not a standard public goods game but a spatial PD with three strategies. The results suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment (with moderate cost and sufficient information) should increase efficiency relative to the control. However, if the cost of punishment/reward is high, the effect can reverse. The model's focus on spatial structure, information, and cost of punishment/reward provides indirect but informative guidance for analogous PGG-like environments.

- source: Jordan, J. J., & Rand, D. G. (2017). Third-party punishment as a costly signal of high continuation probabilities in repeated games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This paper develops a formal model in which third-party punishment (TPP) acts as a costly signal of trustworthiness in repeated games. The model shows that when individuals differ in their probability of future interaction (continuation probability), only those with high continuation probability (trustworthy types) find it worthwhile to engage in TPP, as they are more likely to receive future reciprocal benefits. The signaling value of TPP can make punishment equilibrium behavior in parameter regions where, absent signaling, punishment would not occur. The model provides explicit payoff formulas and equilibrium conditions, and demonstrates via evolutionary simulations that the punishment signaling equilibrium is dynamically stable. The presence of TPP as a signal increases the set of parameter values for which cooperation and higher group payoffs are sustained, thus improving efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the availability of punishment as a costly signal can increase group efficiency in repeated games, especially when individuals differ in continuation probability and when signaling is possible. The model gives explicit conditions (in terms of cost/benefit ratios, continuation probabilities, and punishment costs) under which punishment increases efficiency. While the model is not a direct PGG or lab experiment, its structure is closely related to repeated social dilemma games and provides mechanistic insight into when and why punishment will improve efficiency. The findings suggest that, in environments where signaling is possible and individuals can benefit from future interactions, enabling punishment can lead to higher efficiency than disabling it, provided the cost of punishment is not too high.

- source: Bhaskar, V., & Thomas, C. (2019). Community Enforcement of Trust with Bounded Memory. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the ability to sustain efficient cooperation (trust/lending) in large societies with random matching and one-sided moral hazard depends critically on the information structure available to punishers (lenders). With perfect bounded memory, punishment unravels and efficiency collapses. With coarse information (binary flag for recent default), temporary exclusion can sustain high efficiency, with the exact level depending on the profitability of lending and the exclusion length. The model provides explicit formulas for borrower and lender payoffs under different regimes. When lending is very profitable, some inefficiency is unavoidable due to the need for randomization and endogenous adverse selection. The results generalize to other sequential-move games with one-sided moral hazard.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining efficiency depends on the information structure: coarse information (pooling recent defaulters) enables efficient punishment and high payoffs, while fine-grained information leads to unraveling and zero efficiency. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that punishment will only improve efficiency if the punishment mechanism is supported by an information structure that prevents cherry-picking of near-rehabilitated defectors. The model is not a PGG or direct PGG variant, but the logic applies to trust games and other one-sided moral hazard settings. The findings are most relevant for settings with random matching, temporary exclusion, and information constraints.

- source: Baker, S., & Choi, A. H. (2018). Reputation and Litigation: Why Costly Legal Sanctions Can Work Better than Reputational Sanctions. *Journal of Legal Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that legal sanctions (punishment) can be more efficient than reputational sanctions (boycotts) in deterring undesirable behavior, even when both are costly. This is because legal sanctions have an inframarginal effect: increasing damages not only deters more lawsuits at the margin but also increases the penalty for existing lawsuits, improving deterrence at lower cost. Reputational sanctions lack this inframarginal effect, so increasing their severity is less efficient. The efficiency advantage of legal sanctions is greatest when courts are accurate and when litigation outcomes are public. When court error is high or when consumers have independent, reliable information about quality, reputational sanctions become more important. The model predicts that the optimal regime combines both types of sanctions, but leans toward legal sanctions unless litigation is highly error-prone or costly. The analysis is theoretical and does not provide experimental or empirical estimates, but it offers clear comparative statics and policy implications for the efficiency of punishment mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment (legal sanctions) in repeated moral hazard environments can increase efficiency relative to relying solely on reputational sanctions, especially when punishment is not too costly or error-prone and when outcomes are public. The inframarginal effect of punishment means that its efficiency benefit can be greater than that of reputational sanctions, which only deter at the margin. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment should increase efficiency, particularly when the punishment mechanism is accurate and visible. However, if punishment is highly error-prone or costly, or if reputational information is highly reliable, the efficiency gain from punishment may be reduced or even reversed. The model is not a direct PGG or lab experiment, so quantitative predictions should be made with caution, but the qualitative comparative statics are clear and relevant.

- source: Quan, J., Pu, Z. J., & Wang, X. J. (2021). Comparison of social exclusion and punishment in promoting cooperation: Who should play the leading role?. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that both keeping and reversing social norms can promote cooperation and increase social welfare (average payoff) in indirect reciprocity games with punishment. The effect is moderated by the benefit-cost ratio: keeping rules are more effective at low benefit-cost ratios, while reversing rules are more effective at high benefit-cost ratios. Public reputation scenarios consistently support higher cooperation and welfare than private reputation scenarios. Punishment actions can help sustain cooperation, but their cost can reduce average welfare. The specific social norm in use (among 16 studied) determines whether the population reaches a cooperative or non-cooperative stable state, especially in private reputation settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the design of social norms and the structure of reputation (public vs. private) are critical moderators of the effect of punishment on efficiency (average payoff) in indirect reciprocity games. The results suggest that, in environments similar to pairwise donation games with indirect reciprocity, enabling punishment can increase cooperation and sometimes efficiency, but the net effect on efficiency depends on the cost of punishment and the social norm in use. Public reputation and appropriate social norms (keeping or reversing, depending on benefit-cost ratio) are important for maximizing efficiency gains from punishment. The findings are most directly applicable to pairwise donation games, not standard public goods games, but the mechanisms are adjacent and informative for prediction.

- source: Saak, A. E. (2012). Collective Reputation, Social Norms, and Participation. *American Journal of Agricultural Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that collective reputation mechanisms with local peer punishment can sustain higher efficiency (average payoffs, welfare) than individual reputation, as long as group size is not too small or too large and peer monitoring is present. Peer punishment deters shirking and allows for softer public punishment, increasing the duration of efficient cooperation. The optimal group size increases with the discount factor, the informativeness of public signals, and the quality premium, but decreases with the relative gain from shirking. Without peer monitoring, collective reputation is less efficient than individual reputation. Perfect global peer monitoring allows for arbitrarily large efficient groups. Mandatory traceability (which enables individual punishment) can reduce welfare by undermining group-based peer punishment. The model provides clear comparative statics and welfare implications for the design of collective brands and group-based sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling peer punishment (local or global) in repeated group settings with imperfect public monitoring increases efficiency and group payoffs, compared to settings without such punishment. The model is not a literal public goods game, but is adjacent: it is a repeated moral hazard game with group-level payoffs, endogenous peer punishment, and collective reputation. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment mechanisms (especially peer punishment) on efficiency, conditional on group size, monitoring structure, and information. The paper also provides comparative statics on how efficiency gains from punishment depend on group size, monitoring, and information, which can inform predictions about moderators. However, the model is theoretical and does not provide empirical effect sizes.

- source: Okamoto, K., & Matsumura, S. (2000). The evolution of punishment and apology: an iterated prisoner's dilemma model. *Evolutionary Ecology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that strategies incorporating both punishment and apology signals (especially the Pavlov-like pPAS strategy) can maintain high levels of cooperation and average payoffs in the iterated prisoner's dilemma, even in the presence of errors and deceptive strategies. The average payoff in a pPAS-dominated population is nearly as high as in a fully cooperative population, and much higher than in populations dominated by tit-for-tat (TFT) or defectors. The success of pPAS depends on the costs of punishment and apology: pPAS dominates when the cost of giving punishment is low, the cost of receiving punishment is high, or the cost of giving apology is high. The model also shows that pPAS can remain stable even when there is a dominance asymmetry in costs between players. The findings suggest that punishment and apology mechanisms can evolve to support efficient cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated social dilemmas with error, the availability of punishment (and apology) mechanisms can substantially increase group efficiency, provided the costs of punishment and apology are within certain bounds. The results are most directly applicable to iterated prisoner's dilemma games, which are adjacent but not identical to public goods games. The findings suggest that, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, one should expect a positive effect if the punishment cost is not too high, the punishment is effective, and the apology cost is not too low (to prevent deception). The paper also highlights the importance of considering the cost structure of punishment and apology when making such predictions. However, the results are based on theoretical models and evolutionary simulations, not empirical experiments.

- source: Han, T. A., Lenaerts, T. C., Santos, F. C., & Pereira, L. M. (2022). Voluntary safety commitments provide an escape from over-regulation in AI development. *Technology in Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated two-player (and generalizable to N-player) innovation race game, unconditional punishment of risk-taking (unsafe) behavior can reduce social welfare by over-regulating and suppressing beneficial innovation when risk is low. Introducing voluntary, binding commitments to safe behavior—where only those who commit and then defect are punished—enables the system to achieve high efficiency: safety compliance prevails when it is collectively optimal, and innovation is not stifled when risk-taking is preferred. Both peer and institutional punishment mechanisms are effective if they are targeted only at commitment violators. The model shows that the efficiency of punishment (cost to punisher vs. impact on punished) modulates these effects, but the voluntary commitment mechanism robustly improves outcomes across parameter regimes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the design of punishment mechanisms—specifically, whether punishment is unconditional or conditional on voluntary commitment—has a major impact on group efficiency in public-goods-like innovation dilemmas. For prediction tasks, the presence of voluntary, binding commitments with targeted punishment should be expected to increase efficiency relative to unconditional punishment, especially in environments where the collective optimum varies with risk. The model also highlights the importance of punishment cost and effectiveness, and shows that allowing players to opt out of commitment (and thus avoid punishment) can prevent over-regulation and welfare loss. These insights are directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in games with similar structure, even if the setting is not a classic PGG.

- source: Amor, D. R., & Fort, J. (2011). Effects of punishment in a mobile population playing the prisoner's dilemma game. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing social punishment (where cooperators punish defectors with a small probability) in a mobile PD population can transform a system that would otherwise end in universal defection into one of universal cooperation, thereby maximizing group payoff. Even a 10% probability of social punishment is sufficient in many parameter regimes. The effect is robust to some degree of nonsocial punishment, but as nonsocial punishment increases, cooperation and mean payoff decline. The paper provides explicit analytical predictions and simulation results for mean payoff in the final state, showing that social punishment increases efficiency (mean payoff), while nonsocial punishment reduces it. The effect of punishment is moderated by mobility and temptation to defect: higher values of these parameters require higher rates of social punishment to sustain cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in pairwise PD games with mobile agents, enabling social punishment (even at low rates) can dramatically increase group efficiency (mean payoff), often shifting the system from all-defection (zero payoff) to all-cooperation (maximum payoff). The effect is robust to moderate levels of nonsocial punishment, but high nonsocial punishment or harsh environments (high mobility, high temptation to defect) can negate the benefits. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs (pairwise PD, mobile or spatially structured populations, punishment with cost:impact ratio of 1:3), enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency, especially when the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low. However, the results are for pairwise PD, not n-person public goods games, so direct quantitative transfer to PGGs should be cautious, though qualitative patterns are likely similar.

- source: Perry, L., & Gavrilets, S. (2020). Foresight in a Game of Leadership. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a two-player leadership/inspection game, standard rational or learning protocols do not support the emergence of punishment or cooperation, leading to inefficient outcomes (no production, no inspection). However, if leaders use foresight (weighting future payoffs and anticipating subordinates' best responses), then punishment and production can become stable equilibria, resulting in higher payoffs for both leader and group (i.e., higher efficiency). Selective imitation can also support this outcome if subordinates use best response. The effect depends on the weight placed on future payoffs (the foresight parameter) and the cost-benefit structure of punishment and production.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated or multi-round games where leaders (punishers) value future payoffs and anticipate others' responses, punishment can increase group efficiency by enabling cooperation/production that would not occur otherwise. The effect is sensitive to the cost of punishment, the benefit of production, and the degree of foresight. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in games with forward-looking or strategic agents (or mechanisms that mimic foresight/selective imitation) can substantially increase efficiency, especially when the cost of punishment is not too high relative to the benefit. However, in the absence of such mechanisms, punishment may not emerge and efficiency remains low.

- source: Leimar, O. (1997). Repeated games: A state space approach. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated games like the IPD and dominance games, the ability to punish can support the evolutionary stability of cooperative strategies, leading to higher efficiency (i.e., higher expected payoffs, closer to full cooperation) when the probability of continuation is high and punishment is sufficiently severe and/or effective. However, the effect of punishment on efficiency is parameter-dependent: if the probability of continuation is too low or punishment is too costly/ineffective, cooperation cannot be sustained and efficiency is low. The analysis also shows that there are many possible stable strategies, some of which are more efficient than others, and that the structure of punishment (e.g., how many rounds of mutual defection follow a defection) affects the range of stability and efficiency. In dominance games, punishment by a dominant can enforce resource control and higher efficiency for the dominant, but alternative equilibria exist where the subdominant prevails. In alternating PD, reciprocal strategies can be stable and efficient if the benefit-to-cost ratio is high enough. The findings are theoretical and based on formal analysis of payoffs and stability conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated games depends on game design dimensions such as the probability of continuation (analogous to number of rounds), the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the structure of the game (simultaneous vs. alternating moves, role asymmetry). It shows that punishment can increase efficiency by supporting cooperation, but only when parameters are favorable (e.g., high continuation probability, effective punishment). The results suggest that, for prediction tasks, one should expect punishment to improve efficiency in repeated games when the game is long enough and punishment is not too costly or weak, but that the effect can be neutral or negative if these conditions are not met. The paper also highlights that many different punishment strategies can be stable, so the specific implementation of punishment matters. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency in repeated, two-player, payoff-based games with punishment.

- source: Okada, I. (2020). Two ways to overcome the three social dilemmas of indirect reciprocity. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in indirect reciprocity models with private reputation assessment, punishment can increase group efficiency and maintain stable cooperation if assessment norms are designed to (1) detect free riders, (2) justify punishment of bad actors, and (3) generously assess cooperation toward badly reputed recipients. Two main solutions are identified: one based on recipient-focused justified punishment (with generosity toward bad recipients), and another based on doer-focused justified punishment (with high cooperation efficiency required). The stability and efficiency of cooperation depend on the benefit-to-cost ratio (b/c), with more generous norms required at lower efficiency. The presence of punishment, when justified and properly assessed, leads to higher average payoffs and stable cooperative regimes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends critically on the assessment norm and the efficiency of cooperation (b/c). If the punishment system is designed so that justified punishment is recognized and not misinterpreted (i.e., the intention is correctly communicated), and if generosity is applied in assessing cooperation toward bad recipients, then enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to the control. The benefit-to-cost ratio (mpcr analog) is a key moderator: at higher efficiency, more norms can sustain cooperation. The findings suggest that, for prediction, the presence and design of punishment, the assessment norm, and the efficiency parameter are crucial for forecasting the efficiency gain from punishment.

- source: Isakov, A., & Rand, D. G. (2012). The Evolution of Coercive Institutional Punishment. *Dynamic Games and Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional punishment can evolve and be sustained when leaders (kings) can punish subjects for low tribute, especially when subjects are intelligent (accurate learners) and update their strategies quickly relative to kings. Strong punishment leads to high tribute payments but reduces subject payoffs and overall efficiency, except in the idealized case where punishment is never actually used. The model predicts that confusion or slow learning among subjects can collectively benefit them by undermining the effectiveness of coercive punishment. Increased global awareness (subjects learning from other kingdoms) destabilizes coercion and can collapse institutional punishment. The model is robust to changes in population size and punishment strength bounds.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that institutional punishment, when used coercively in asymmetric settings, can increase compliance (tribute payment) but generally reduces group efficiency and aggregate payoffs compared to non-punishment or noncoercive equilibria. For prediction tasks, the model suggests that enabling punishment in similar asymmetric games will likely decrease efficiency unless compliance is perfect and punishment is never actually used. The effects are moderated by learning dynamics (intelligence, update speed) and the degree of information subjects have about other groups. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is imposed by a central authority and the 'public good' is tribute to that authority, rather than a symmetric PGG.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2016). Evolution of cooperation by the introduction of the probabilistic peer-punishment based on the difference of payoff. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of a probabilistic peer-punishment mechanism, where punishment is more likely and more severe when the payoff difference between punisher and target is larger, leads to a substantial increase in both cooperation and average payoff in spatial prisoner's dilemma simulations. This effect is robust across regular, random, and scale-free network topologies, and for different average degrees of connectivity. The model demonstrates that, contrary to some previous findings with standard peer-punishment, this form of punishment can increase group welfare (average payoff) rather than decrease it.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in spatial prisoner's dilemma environments, a punishment mechanism that is probabilistic and based on payoff differences can increase average payoff, in contrast to standard fixed-cost peer punishment which often reduces group payoff. While the model is not a public goods game, the findings are relevant for predicting the effect of punishment in adjacent game structures, especially where punishment is adaptive or conditional on payoff differences. The results suggest that the design of the punishment mechanism (e.g., making it proportional to payoff differences and probabilistic) is a key moderator of its effect on efficiency. However, direct quantitative predictions for PGGs should be made with caution due to the difference in game structure.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2017). Characteristics of the evolution of cooperation by the probabilistic peer-punishment based on the difference of payoff. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a probabilistic peer-punishment mechanism—where the probability and severity of punishment are proportional to payoff differences—can increase both cooperation and the average payoff of all players in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. The effect is robust across different network topologies (regular, random, scale-free) and for varying average degrees of connectivity, though the parameter range for positive effects narrows as connectivity increases. The model avoids antisocial punishment by design. The results suggest that, with appropriate parameterization, punishment can enhance group efficiency, not just cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in environments structurally similar to spatial prisoner's dilemma games, a well-designed probabilistic peer-punishment mechanism can increase group efficiency (average payoff), especially when the punishment is neither too weak nor too strong. The effect is sensitive to the network structure: as the average number of connections increases, the range of punishment parameters that yield efficiency gains becomes narrower. While the model is not a public goods game, the payoff-based outcome and the punishment mechanism are closely related, making the findings informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like settings, especially where network structure and punishment calibration are relevant.

- source: Jaffe, K. (2004). Altruism, altruistic punishment and social investment. *Acta Biotheoretica*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper uses agent-based simulations to examine the effects of altruistic punishment on aggregate wealth (efficiency) in artificial societies. It finds that while punishment increases generosity and norm compliance, it reduces aggregate wealth compared to societies without punishment, unless the punished behaviors trigger synergistic benefits for society. The effect is observed in both structured (with division of labor) and unstructured societies, though structured societies are more susceptible to social control via punishment. The study concludes that punishment is a strong mechanism for norm enforcement but is not, by itself, efficiency-enhancing.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper provides evidence that enabling punishment in a public-goods-like environment (here, an agent-based resource-sharing simulation) increases norm compliance and generosity but reduces group efficiency (aggregate wealth), unless there are additional synergistic benefits from the enforced behaviors. The negative effect on efficiency is robust to variations in social structure and punishment intensity. Thus, when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper suggests that, in the absence of explicit synergistic returns, punishment is likely to reduce efficiency relative to the control. The findings are most directly relevant to settings where punishment is costly and does not directly generate group-level surplus.

- source: Gao, S. P., Wu, T., & Wang, L. (2018). Evolution of global cooperation and ethnocentrism in group-structured populations. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper develops an analytical model of a group-structured population where individuals can adopt one of four strategies (global cooperator, ethnocentrist, traitor, egoist) and interact via a simplified prisoner's dilemma. Without any promoting mechanism, global cooperation is never favored by selection, regardless of migration pattern or group number; ethnocentrism is promoted, especially by global migration. When leader punishment is introduced (leaders punish defectors), global cooperation can be favored and can become more abundant than ethnocentrism if the punishment fine is sufficiently high. Efficiency-oriented leader punishment is more effective than equality-oriented punishment. The number of groups, migration rate, and mutation rate all moderate these effects. The main outcomes are derived as conditions for strategy abundance, which are directly linked to group payoff and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in group-structured prisoner's dilemma-like environments, punishment mechanisms (especially leader/third-party punishment with sufficient fines) can transform a setting where global cooperation is not favored into one where it is. The effect is more pronounced for efficiency-oriented punishment. The model is not a standard public goods game but is closely related, and the payoff-based selection conditions are directly interpretable as efficiency outcomes. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence and type of punishment, the cost/fine of punishment, and the structure of the population (number of groups, migration/mutation rates) are critical moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment. In the absence of punishment, efficiency is low (global cooperation not favored); with strong leader punishment, efficiency can be high (global cooperation favored).

- source: Ubeda, F., & Duéñez-Guzmán, E. A. (2011). POWER AND CORRUPTION. *Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a Prisoner's Dilemma with costly punishment, allowing for corrupt punishers and power asymmetries enables the evolution of stable states with both cooperation and corruption. When the social cost of punishing a punisher (q+d) is less than the cost of punishing a civilian (p), and within a certain range, a mixed equilibrium emerges with both cooperating civilians and corrupt policers. In this equilibrium, the population mean payoff (efficiency) is higher than in the all-defect equilibrium, and can approach the fully cooperative payoff as power asymmetries increase. The model demonstrates that costly punishment can increase both cooperation and efficiency, especially when punishers are protected by power asymmetries. The likelihood of reaching the efficient equilibrium depends on the relative costs and effectiveness of punishment. The results challenge the view that costly punishment is always inefficient, showing that under the right conditions, it can benefit the group even when punishers are themselves corrupt.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency depends critically on the structure of punishment costs and power asymmetries. If punishment is costly but power asymmetries exist (punishers are less vulnerable to punishment or punishment is more effective against non-punishers), then enabling punishment can increase efficiency above the control (no-punishment) baseline. The model suggests that the efficiency gain is larger when the punishment of defectors is strong and the punishment of punishers is weak. The findings are most directly applicable to Prisoner's Dilemma settings, but the logic is relevant for public goods games with similar punishment structures. The paper does not address all game design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, communication), but provides direct, mechanistic insight into how punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and power asymmetries moderate the efficiency impact of punishment.

- source: Liu, M. M., Ma, Y. H., Liu, Z. Y., & You, X. M. (2017). An IUR evolutionary game model on the patent cooperate of Shandong China. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that higher punishment for negative cooperation (defection) increases both the rate of positive cooperation and the average payoff (\bar{U}) in the network, especially when punishment exceeds the development cost. When punishment is low, negative cooperation is more attractive and average payoffs are lower. Knowledge spillover reduces cooperation and can increase payoffs for defectors, but if punishment is high, spillover has less effect. The most efficient and cooperative outcome is achieved when profits are split equally between the two sides. The findings are based on simulation of the theoretical model, not on lab or field experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical/simulation evidence that, in a networked, repeated, two-population cooperation game with punishment, increasing the punishment for negative cooperation increases group efficiency (average payoff), especially when punishment exceeds the cost of cooperation. The model is not a standard public goods game but is closely related. The results suggest that, for similar networked cooperation games, enabling or increasing punishment should be predicted to increase efficiency, particularly when the punishment is substantial relative to the cost of cooperation. The findings are most directly applicable to settings with network structure, two-sided cooperation, and payoff sharing, but the qualitative direction is likely to generalize to PGG-like environments.

- source: Friedman, D., & Singh, N. (2004). Negative reciprocity - The coevolution of memes and genes. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that negative reciprocity (punishment) can transform a standard social dilemma (Prisoner's Dilemma) from an inefficient all-defect equilibrium to a coordination game with a Pareto efficient all-cooperate equilibrium, provided the punishment motive is strong enough. However, individual willingness to punish is undermined by evolutionary free-rider problems, as the costs are borne individually but the benefits are shared. The key finding is that group-level enforcement of punishment norms (memes), using low-cost sanctions such as shunning or status reduction, can sustain the optimal level of negative reciprocity and thus maximize group fitness. The model predicts that the efficiency of cooperation increases with the strength and enforcement of punishment norms, and that these norms will coevolve with individual capacities and environmental hostility. The comparative statics suggest that efficiency is higher when punishment is less costly, more effective, and more strongly enforced by group norms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment (especially when group-enforced) increases efficiency in social dilemmas, compared to control conditions without punishment. The model is based on a two-player Prisoner's Dilemma, but the authors argue that the logic extends to n-person games like public goods games. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence and strength of punishment, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the rigor of group enforcement are key moderators of the efficiency gain from punishment. The model also predicts that efficiency gains from punishment are larger when punishment is less costly and more effective, and when group norms for enforcement are strong. However, the model is theoretical and does not provide empirical effect sizes or direct evidence from public goods games.

- source: Wubs, M., Bshary, R., & Lehmann, L. (2016). Coevolution between positive reciprocity, punishment, and partner switching in repeated interactions. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games, the evolutionary success of punishment, positive reciprocity, and partner switching depends on the number of rounds, group size, and the costs/effectiveness of punishment and switching. Punishment is favored in small and moderately sized groups, especially when punishment is efficient and its behavioral effect is long-lasting. Partner switching dominates in large groups or when the number of rounds is high, unless switching is costly. Positive reciprocity is rarely the dominant mechanism, except when punishment is less effective or more costly. The model does not directly report group efficiency or total payoff, but evolutionary success is determined by aggregate payoff, so the results indirectly inform about conditions under which punishment or other mechanisms would lead to higher average payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma-like environments. It suggests that punishment is more likely to improve group outcomes (in terms of evolutionary success/payoff) in small or moderately sized groups, especially when punishment is efficient and its behavioral effect is long-lasting. In large groups or with many rounds, partner switching may be more effective. The findings are most relevant for adjacent settings to public goods games, especially repeated dyadic interactions, and should be interpreted with caution when generalizing to standard PGGs. The paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency, but the evolutionary dominance of strategies is determined by payoff, so the results can be used to infer the likely direction of punishment's effect on efficiency under different design dimensions.

- source: Han, T. A., & Lenaerts, T. (2016). A synergy of costly punishment and commitment in cooperation dilemmas. *Adaptive Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that a strategy combining prior commitment and costly punishment (CPP) can achieve higher frequencies of cooperation than either mechanism alone, across a wide range of parameter values. The improvement is most significant when the cost of arranging commitments is high and the punishment impact is above a threshold. The model shows that commitment reduces the effect-to-cost ratio required for punishment to be effective, and punishment helps deal with free-riders who exploit commitment. The results are robust across different cost and punishment parameters, but are derived for the two-player, one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma, not for public goods games or group interactions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that combining commitment and punishment mechanisms can outperform either alone in promoting cooperation, especially when commitment is costly and punishment is sufficiently effective. However, the model is based on the two-player Prisoner's Dilemma, not a public goods game or group setting. The outcomes are reported as frequencies of strategies and risk-dominance, not as group efficiency or total payoff. Thus, while the findings suggest that punishment (especially when combined with commitment) can improve cooperation and, by implication, group outcomes, the results cannot be directly mapped to efficiency predictions in public goods games. The paper is most useful for understanding the interaction of punishment and commitment mechanisms and their parameter dependencies, but less directly informative for quantitative efficiency prediction in PGG-like environments.

- source: Zhu, Y. Y., Zhang, J. L., Sun, Q. L., & Chen, Z. Q. (2017). Evolutionary dynamics of strategies for threshold snowdrift games on complex networks. *Knowledge-based Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment in threshold snowdrift games increases the evolutionary advantage of cooperation over defection, especially when punishment cost is low and the threshold is moderate. Larger thresholds generally promote cooperation, but too high or too low thresholds can be detrimental. Lower punishment cost (to the punisher) and higher punishment impact (on the defector) both facilitate cooperation. These effects are robust across well-mixed and networked populations, including single-layer and double-layer (interconnected) networks. However, the main reported outcomes are cooperation rates, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment can increase cooperation rates in threshold public-goods-like games, especially when punishment is effective and not too costly. However, it does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. Theoretical payoff difference (P_c - P_d) is analyzed, but simulation results focus on cooperation rates. The findings suggest that punishment is more effective when the cost to punishers is low and the penalty to defectors is high, and that network structure and threshold size moderate these effects. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that punishment can improve cooperation under certain parameter regimes, but does not provide direct quantitative evidence on efficiency.

- source: Cui, P. B., & Wu, Z. X. (2014). Selfish punishment with avoiding mechanism can alleviate both first-order and second-order social dilemma. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that introducing selfish punishment with an avoiding mechanism into a spatial prisoner's dilemma game can alleviate both first-order and second-order social dilemmas, depending on the punishment cost, fine, and identification probability. The presence of selfish punishers allows for the stable coexistence of cooperators and punishers, and under certain parameter regimes, reduces the prevalence of defectors and second-order free riders. Theoretical predictions using extended pair approximation and interaction web analysis match well with simulation results. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of each strategy type, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment (especially when including mechanisms for selfish punishment and avoidance) can promote cooperation and reduce free riding in spatially structured social dilemmas. However, because the model is a prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game) and the main outcomes are strategy frequencies rather than group efficiency or total payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that the effectiveness of punishment depends on the cost/fine ratio and the ability to identify defectors, but do not provide direct quantitative predictions for efficiency outcomes in public goods games. The paper is most useful for understanding mechanisms and qualitative moderators of punishment effects, rather than for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Roos, P., Gelfand, M., Nau, D., & Carr, R. (2014). High strength-of-ties and low mobility enable the evolution of third-party punishment. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that responsible third-party punishment (3PP) can evolve and sustain cooperation in structured populations when social-structural constraints are high (i.e., high strength-of-ties and low mobility). Under these conditions, clusters of responsible punishers can induce cooperation in their local neighborhoods, leading to higher local pay-offs and the spread of both cooperation and punishment. In contrast, in well-mixed or highly mobile populations, responsible 3PP does not evolve, and cooperation fails to emerge. The model shows that 3PP is pivotal for the emergence of cooperation and increased pay-off, as neither population structure alone nor direct punishment alone suffice. The findings are robust to variations in network structure and to the presence of antisocial punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation and increasing pay-off (and thus efficiency) depends critically on the social structure of the population. Specifically, punishment is more likely to increase efficiency in games with high strength-of-ties (low average degree, i.e., players interact repeatedly with a small set of neighbors) and low mobility (players do not frequently change partners). In well-mixed or highly mobile settings, punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that game designs with fixed, repeated local interactions and low partner switching will see greater efficiency gains from punishment mechanisms, while designs with random matching or high mobility will not. The model is adjacent to public goods games (it uses a two-player cooperation game with third-party punishment), so the transfer to standard PGGs is not exact but likely informative.

- source: Takesue, H. (2018). Evolutionary prisoner's dilemma games on the network with punishment and opportunistic partner switching. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that both punishment and partner switching (opportunistic rewiring of social ties) can support the evolution of cooperation in a networked prisoner's dilemma. When partner switching is infrequent, strong punishment is necessary to suppress defectors. When partner switching is frequent, cooperation dominates even with weak or no punishment. The mechanism is that defectors sever ties with punishers and preferentially connect to cooperators, which increases cooperators' opportunities to be imitated and thus proliferate. Average payoffs by strategy are reported, showing that cooperators and punishers can outperform defectors, but the main outcome is the frequency of each strategy. The results suggest that social fluidity (partner switching) can substitute for punishment in maintaining cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report group efficiency or total welfare directly, it shows that both punishment and partner switching can increase the prevalence of cooperation and reduce defection, and that average payoffs for cooperators and punishers can exceed those of defectors. The findings suggest that in networked games with opportunities for partner switching, the marginal benefit of adding punishment to increase efficiency may be reduced if partner switching is already frequent. The results also highlight the importance of the punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and the fluidity of social relationships (analogous to partner switching) as moderators of punishment's effect. However, because the main outcomes are strategy frequencies and not group efficiency, the evidence is most useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators rather than for direct quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Helbing, D., Yu, W. J., Opp, K. D., & Rauhut, H. (2014). Conditions for the Emergence of Shared Norms in Populations with Incompatible Preferences. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment (sanctioning) is a powerful mechanism for establishing shared norms in heterogeneous populations, even when preferences are incompatible. Behavior-based punishment is more effective than preference-based punishment in creating conformity. However, the resulting norm is not always efficient: sometimes a minority can establish an unpopular or suboptimal norm, and average payoff may not be maximized. Adaptive group pressure (where local majorities punish minorities) can lead to spontaneous norm emergence, but again, the content of the norm may be arbitrary and not welfare-maximizing. The model highlights the importance of path dependence, local interaction, and randomness in norm formation. Payoff (welfare) is explicitly tracked, and the model demonstrates that sanctioning can both increase and decrease average payoff depending on the scenario.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency (payoff) in public-goods-like environments is highly context-dependent. Punishment can increase conformity and create shared norms, but does not guarantee higher efficiency: the emergent norm may be suboptimal. The model suggests that the effectiveness of punishment depends on its cost, effectiveness, and whether it is behavior- or preference-based. Local interaction and adaptive group pressure can facilitate norm emergence, but do not ensure welfare maximization. For prediction, this paper suggests that enabling punishment will often increase conformity but may have mixed or even negative effects on efficiency, especially if the norm that emerges is not aligned with group welfare. The model's adjacent structure to PGGs means its findings are suggestive but not directly quantitative for standard PGG efficiency prediction.

- source: Boyd, R., & Mathew, S. (2015). Third-party monitoring and sanctions aid the evolution of language. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper develops a repeated signaling game (a variant of the Sir Philip Sidney game) to model the evolution of honest communication. It finds that when only direct reciprocity is possible and lies are hard to detect, honest signaling is not evolutionarily stable except in special cases. However, when third-party monitoring and punishment are introduced—so that multiple community members can observe, aggregate evidence, and sanction dishonesty—the range of parameters supporting honest signaling expands greatly. This is because third-party monitoring increases the probability of detecting lies and reduces false accusations, making it more likely that group-beneficial honest signaling (analogous to high efficiency) is maintained.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that adding third-party monitoring and punishment mechanisms to repeated social dilemmas (even when not strictly public goods games) can substantially increase the likelihood of group-beneficial outcomes, such as honest signaling or cooperation. For prediction tasks, the presence of third-party punishment and monitoring should be expected to have a strongly positive effect on efficiency or group payoff, especially when direct detection of defection is difficult. The model is not a standard PGG, but the logic is closely related and the results are likely to transfer to PGG-like environments where monitoring and punishment are possible.

- source: Larson, J. M. (2017). Networks and Interethnic Cooperation. *Journal of Politics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the ability of groups to enforce cooperation through peer punishment depends critically on the structure of their internal communication networks. Specifically, the 'reach' or 'integration' of the network—how widely and quickly information about defections can spread—determines how easily cooperation can be sustained. Denser networks are not necessarily better; networks with high integration (no highly peripheral or bottleneck nodes) are optimal for supporting cooperation. The model also shows that there is an upper bound on the proportion of interethnic (cross-group) interactions that can be kept cooperative, determined by the network's reach. Improvements in communication speed, longer punishment phases, and more integrated networks all make cooperation easier to sustain. The paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, but rather the theoretical conditions for sustaining cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on how network structure moderates the effectiveness of peer punishment in sustaining cooperation in repeated social dilemmas. For prediction tasks, it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment (and thus the likely efficiency gain from enabling punishment) depends not on network density but on integration/reach. However, the model is not a public goods game per se, and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly for quantitative prediction of efficiency. It is most useful for qualitative reasoning about the importance of network structure (especially communication reach) as a moderator of punishment's effect on cooperation.

- source: Morris, A., MacGlashan, J., Littman, M. L., & Cushman, F. (2017). Evolution of flexibility and rigidity in retaliatory punishment. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that when punishment is costly, flexible (learning-based) victims are less likely to learn to punish, making them more vulnerable in repeated interactions. This leads to the evolution of rigid punishment strategies (i.e., always punish theft), even when such punishment is costly and not always effective. The learning dynamics of reinforcement learning agents show that thieves are more likely to adapt flexibly, while victims tend to persist in punishment. The evolutionary simulations confirm that the cost of punishment biases selection toward rigid punishment. The findings are primarily about the evolution and persistence of punishment strategies, not about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence about the evolution of punishment strategies in repeated dyadic games, focusing on the interplay between learning dynamics and evolutionary selection. While it does not directly address efficiency or group payoff in public goods games, it is informative about when rigid punishment is likely to evolve and persist, especially when punishment is costly. For prediction tasks about efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper suggests that the cost of punishment and the learning dynamics of participants may influence the prevalence of rigid punishment, but it does not provide direct evidence about efficiency outcomes. Its relevance is mainly in understanding the conditions under which punishment is likely to be rigid or flexible, which may indirectly affect efficiency in related environments.
