While it reviews behavioral and neural evidence on costly punishment and other pro-social behaviors, the outcomes discussed are primarily neural activation and behavioral propensities (e.g., willingness to punish, cooperate, or trust), not efficiency or group payoff. The review may be useful for understanding the psychological and neural mechanisms underlying punishment, but it does not support quantitative or qualitative prediction of efficiency outcomes based on game design dimensions.

- source: Mulder, L. B. (2018). When sanctions convey moral norms. *European Journal of Law and Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists
  findings: The paper argues that sanctions can have an expressive function, conveying moral norms and motivating compliance for moral reasons rather than purely instrumental ones. It reviews evidence that (1) retributive sanctions are more likely to induce moral norms than compensatory ones, (2) punishments are more effective than rewards at conveying obligatory moral rules, and (3) severe (but not unreasonably severe) sanctions are more likely to induce moral disapproval and compliance, especially when implemented by trustworthy and fair authorities. However, the paper also notes that sanctions can backfire, especially among those already engaging in the undesired behavior, and that excessive severity or unfairness can undermine moral norms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and psychological context for how punishment may affect compliance and norm internalization in public-goods-like environments, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes. It suggests that the design and framing of punishment (retributive vs. compensatory, punishment vs. reward, severity, authority trustworthiness, and fairness) can moderate the effectiveness of sanctions in changing behavior. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative, as it does not report or synthesize efficiency or group payoff data. Its main value is in highlighting psychological mechanisms and moderators that could influence the effectiveness of punishment in experimental designs.

- source: Raihani, N. J., & McAuliffe, K. (2012). Does Inequity Aversion Motivate Punishment? Cleaner Fish as a Model System. *Social Justice Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that in cleaner fish, punishment of cheating partners is not motivated by inequity aversion, as these fish do not show behavioral sensitivity to unequal outcomes in controlled experiments. Instead, punishment appears to be triggered by loss aversion—when the punisher experiences a reduction in expected payoff (e.g., when a client leaves after a partner cheats). In humans, both inequity aversion and loss aversion may motivate punishment, but the evidence is not conclusive. The paper reviews but does not present new data on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It is useful for understanding the psychological or proximate mechanisms underlying punishment (loss aversion vs. inequity aversion), which may inform the design of future experiments or the interpretation of why punishment sometimes increases cooperation. However, it does not report or synthesize efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes in a way that can be directly mapped to the prediction task. Its relevance is primarily theoretical and mechanistic.

- source: Zhang, Y., & van der Schaar, M. (2013). Robust Reputation Protocol Design for Online Communities: A Stochastic Stability Analysis. *Ieee Journal of Selected Topics in Signal Processing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper shows that in a repeated random matching game (asymmetric gift-giving, not a standard PGG), a reputation protocol with probabilistic punishment (parameterized by punishment probability beta) can induce full cooperation and achieve maximal efficiency (social welfare = b-c) in the long run, provided the discount factor and punishment strength are within certain bounds. If punishment is too weak or too strong, efficiency is lower. Theoretical analysis is supported by numerical simulations showing that the optimal protocol achieves much higher average social welfare than direct reciprocity (tit-for-tat) or less robust reputation schemes. The protocol's design is robust to stochastic perturbations and ensures convergence to the efficient equilibrium.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated random matching environments with binary contribution (gift-giving), the introduction of a well-calibrated reputation-based punishment protocol can move the system from zero efficiency (no cooperation) to full efficiency (all cooperate, social welfare maximized). The effect is robust to noise and initial conditions, provided the punishment probability is neither too low nor too high. While the game is not a standard PGG, the adjacent structure and explicit efficiency analysis make the results highly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in similar environments. The findings suggest that, when punishment is enabled via a reputation protocol and properly tuned, efficiency can increase dramatically compared to the no-punishment baseline.

- source: Hooper, P. L., Kaplan, H. S., & Jaeggi, A. V. (2021). Gains to cooperation drive the evolution of egalitarianism. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The model demonstrates that punishment of dominance (hawkishness) by withholding cooperation (defection in the PD game) can be evolutionarily stable and lead to egalitarian outcomes when the future benefits of cooperation outweigh the short-term gains from dominance. The efficiency of the group increases as leveller strategies proliferate, reducing the frequency of costly contests and increasing resource sharing. Simulations show that an ecological shift increasing the gains to cooperation leads to a transition from dominance/defection equilibria to egalitarian/cooperative equilibria with higher payoffs. Empirical analysis of forager societies finds that greater reliance on cooperative hunting predicts a higher probability of egalitarianism, consistent with the model's predictions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment (here, the ability to withhold cooperation from dominant individuals) increases group efficiency and resource equality, especially when the marginal benefit of cooperation is high and the probability of future interaction is high. The model is adjacent to public goods games but not an exact match; however, the logic and payoff structure are highly relevant. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that in repeated, high-mpcr, high-future-interaction environments, enabling punishment (even in the form of exclusion or shunning) will increase efficiency relative to control. The effect is stronger when the benefits of cooperation are high relative to the benefits of dominance or defection.

- source: Li, J. M., & Jiang, S. S. (2023). How can governance strategies be developed for marine ecological environment pollution caused by sea-using enterprises?-A study based on evolutionary game theory. *Ocean & Coastal Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in the absence of strong governance mechanisms (punishment, compensation, or high ecological performance appraisal), all parties tend to adopt minimal effort strategies, resulting in low efficiency and poor environmental outcomes (prisoner's dilemma). Introducing punishment (penalties for non-compliance, ecological damage compensation), increasing the weight of ecological indicators in local government performance appraisal, and combining these tools can shift the system to a more efficient equilibrium, where local governments and enterprises fully implement and comply with environmental policies, even if central government supervision is weak. The effect of any single tool is limited, but their combination is necessary to achieve high efficiency in governance.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the presence and strength of punishment mechanisms (penalties, compensation for damage) and reward mechanisms (performance appraisal) are critical moderators of the efficiency gains from introducing punishment in public-goods-like governance settings. The results suggest that, in adjacent multi-agent settings, punishment alone may not be sufficient, but when combined with other governance tools, it can substantially increase efficiency. For prediction tasks, the presence and magnitude of punishment, reward, and their combination should be considered as key moderators of treatment efficiency, especially when the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low due to weak incentives.

- source: Yuan, Y., Wang, F. Y., & Zeng, D. (2016). Developing a cooperative bidding framework for sponsored search markets - An evolutionary perspective. *Information Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated sponsored search auctions, if advertisers are limited to unconditional strategies (always cooperate or always compete), competitive strategies dominate, resulting in high bids and low efficiency (advertiser revenue is about 0.64 times the UBNE outcome, which is suboptimal). When contingent 'nice and retaliatory' (NR) strategies are available—where advertisers cooperate unless a rival deviates, in which case they punish by bidding competitively—stable cooperation emerges. This leads to lower bids (at the GEAE, the lowest-price equilibrium) and advertiser revenues at the socially optimal VCG level. The NR strategies are neutrally evolutionarily stable: they cannot be strongly invaded by competitive strategies, and cooperation persists. The effect is robust to incomplete information, as long as advertisers can monitor rivals' bids and retaliate. The findings are supported by both theoretical analysis and coevolutionary simulations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment-like contingent strategies (NR) in repeated, multi-player, auction-like public goods environments can substantially increase efficiency (group payoff) relative to a control condition where only unconditional strategies are available. The effect is robust across a range of parameterizations (number of players, rounds, etc.), and the mechanism is directly analogous to punishment in public goods games: punishment of defectors stabilizes cooperation and increases group welfare. For prediction tasks, the presence of contingent punishment strategies should be expected to increase efficiency from a low baseline (competitive equilibrium) to near the social optimum, provided players can observe and respond to others' actions. The results are most directly applicable to repeated, multi-player, auction-like games with observable actions and the possibility of contingent retaliation.

The model provides explicit conditions under which full cooperation, partial cooperation, or defection equilibria are stable. However, the main reported outcomes are the equilibrium strategy fractions, not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings are useful for understanding how punishment/reward mechanisms can promote cooperation in multi-stakeholder environments similar to public goods games, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency outcomes.

While the model is not a standard public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it is informative for predicting that stronger, more effective punishment mechanisms are likely to increase cooperation rates. - source: Geng, Y. N., Shen, C., Hu, K. P., & Shi, L. (2018). Impact of punishment on the evolution of cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of a punishment mechanism—where players with below-average payoffs are punished by a reduction in their fitness coefficient—substantially increases the stationary frequency of cooperators in a spatial prisoner's dilemma model. Increasing the punishment rate and memory length both further promote cooperation, leading to full dominance of cooperation under many parameter settings. The mechanism's effect is attributed to increased heterogeneity in individual fitness, which supports network reciprocity. However, the paper does not report on group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial prisoner's dilemma environments, especially when punishment is more intense and lasts longer. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, and because the game is a prisoner's dilemma rather than a public goods game, the findings should be transferred to PGG-like efficiency prediction with caution. The results suggest that, in similar spatial or networked environments, enabling punishment may increase cooperation, which could plausibly increase efficiency, but this is not directly demonstrated. The specific punishment mechanism (punishing those with below-average payoffs) is also nonstandard compared to typical PGG punishment designs.

- source: Safarzynska, K. (2013). The coevolution of culture and environment. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that costly resource conservation (including punishment of defectors) can evolve in structured populations facing renewable resource constraints and environmental shocks. Groups with many defectors are more likely to overharvest and collapse, while groups with more cooperators and punishers are more resilient. Punishment reduces the within-group advantage of defection, but is itself costly. Environmental shocks (weather) accelerate the elimination of non-cooperative groups. Between-group interactions (resource-conflict and harvest-sharing) modulate the evolution of cooperation: resource-conflict increases the frequency of resource conservation by increasing collapse of defector-heavy groups, while harvest-sharing reduces group collapse and allows more defection. Larger group size has a non-monotonic effect: above a threshold, larger groups are more likely to collapse, which can favor the evolution of conservation. Policies that increase the cost of extraction or penalize defectors promote conservation. However, the model does not report group efficiency or total payoff as a primary outcome, focusing instead on behavioral strategy frequencies and group survival.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical support for the idea that punishment and resource conservation can evolve and persist in group-structured resource dilemmas, especially under environmental variability and when group collapse is possible. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff in public goods games or their close variants. The model's outcomes are mainly about the frequency of cooperative/punishing strategies and group survival, not efficiency as defined for the prediction task. Thus, while the mechanisms discussed (e.g., punishment reducing defection, group collapse selecting for cooperation) are relevant for understanding when punishment might improve group outcomes, the paper does not provide data or results that can be directly mapped to efficiency predictions for specific game design dimensions.

- source: Jin, Q., Wang, Z., Wang, Z., & Wang, Y. L. (2012). Strategy changing penalty promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of a strategy changing penalty (a form of endogenous punishment for switching strategies) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game increases the fraction of cooperators. Both higher penalty rates and longer memory (the period after which the penalty is lifted if the player does not change strategy again) monotonically promote cooperation, with the effect saturating at high memory. The mechanism works by inducing heterogeneity in fitness coefficients, which helps cooperator clusters survive and expand. The paper does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, so the findings are about cooperation rates, not efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that endogenous penalty mechanisms can increase cooperation in spatial social dilemmas, but it does not report efficiency or group payoff. The game is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and the punishment is not peer-to-peer but is an automatic penalty for changing strategy. Thus, while the findings suggest that certain forms of punishment can promote cooperation, they do not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The main value is as adjacent, mechanistic support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms can increase cooperation, especially when combined with memory or forgiveness.

- source: Bi, Y., & Yang, H. (2023). Based on reputation consistent strategy times promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner?s dilemma game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The introduction of a reputation-based warning/punishment mechanism—where players are less likely to imitate neighbors who have persistently defected—substantially increases the fraction of cooperators in a spatial PDG simulation. Stronger punishment (higher discount factor) and more timely punishment (lower threshold for triggering the discount) both further increase cooperation rates. The effect is robust across a range of temptation-to-defect parameters. However, the paper does not report on group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, so the findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment-like mechanisms targeting persistent defectors can increase cooperation rates in spatial PDG environments. However, because it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings may be useful for understanding mechanisms that increase cooperation, but should be used with caution for efficiency prediction, as higher cooperation does not always translate to higher group payoff depending on the cost structure of punishment and the payoff matrix.

- source: Vukov, J., Santos, F. C., & Pacheco, J. M. (2012). Cognitive strategies take advantage of the cooperative potential of heterogeneous networks. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that allowing players to use reactive strategies (punishing defectors and rewarding cooperators based on memory of past actions) leads to high and robust levels of cooperation in scale-free networks, even when participation costs are present. The effect is much stronger than for unconditional strategies. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperative acts, not group efficiency or total payoff. The model is based on the prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, but the mechanisms of punishment/retaliation and reward are closely related. The findings suggest that cognitive strategies can overcome the negative effects of participation costs and defector invasion on cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment/retaliation mechanisms (as part of cognitive/reactive strategies) can sustain high cooperation rates in networked social dilemmas, even with participation costs and frequent defector invasion. However, because the model is a prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game) and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited. The main signal is that punishment/retaliation can be highly effective in maintaining cooperation under adverse conditions, especially when players can condition their behavior on others' past actions. The findings are most relevant for settings with network structure, memory, and the possibility of both punishment and reward.

- source: Helbing, D., & Johansson, A. (2010). Cooperation, Norms, and Revolutions: A Unified Game-Theoretical Approach. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the dynamics and outcomes of cooperation and norm establishment in multi-population games depend critically on the payoff structure (game type), the relative power of populations, and initial conditions. In multi-population prisoner's dilemma games, defection dominates regardless of power. In stag hunt games, shared norms can emerge, often set by the more powerful or initially larger group, but sometimes by the weaker group depending on initial conditions. In snowdrift games, polarization and abrupt 'revolutionary' transitions can occur. Costly punishment can transform a prisoner's dilemma into a stag hunt, supporting norm establishment. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral equilibria and norm dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how punishment, payoff structure, and group power shape the emergence of cooperation and norms in environments related to, but not identical with, public goods games. It suggests that punishment can promote norm establishment by changing the effective game structure, but does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it supports the idea that punishment can shift equilibria toward more cooperative outcomes in some settings, but does not quantify efficiency gains or losses. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of game structure and group asymmetries as moderators of punishment effects.

- source: Li, Q., Iqbal, A., Perc, M., Chen, M. Y., & Abbott, D. (2013). Coevolution of Quantum and Classical Strategies on Evolving Random Networks. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when agents can punish by reducing link weights, cooperation can dominate for a wider range of temptation values, especially when the network structure is updated frequently. Stronger punishment (lower minimum link weight) increases the range where cooperation dominates. As the temptation to defect increases, quantum strategies eventually dominate. The number of neighbors (network degree) has little effect if all agents have equal degree. The network evolves to have higher clustering and small-world properties. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the findings are about strategy prevalence, not direct payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (reducing link weights) on the prevalence of cooperation in an evolving network Prisoner's Dilemma. It suggests that stronger punishment and more frequent network updating support cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only adjacent: it supports the idea that punishment can sustain cooperation under some conditions, but does not quantify efficiency gains or losses. The game is not a public goods game, but a networked PD with quantum strategies, so transfer to PGG-like settings should be cautious.

- source: Mameli, M. (2013). Meat made us moral: a hypothesis on the nature and evolution of moral judgment. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper argues that the evolution of moral judgment is rooted in emotional dispositions that support the enforcement of group-beneficial norms through punishment and meta-punishment. It draws on ethnographic and evolutionary accounts of hunter-gatherer societies, where collective punishment of cheaters and bullies (those who disrupt cooperation and resource sharing) is common and is enforced through both direct and reputational means (e.g., gossip, ostracism, disapproval). The author suggests that these mechanisms enabled stable cooperation in small groups, but the analysis is conceptual and evolutionary rather than empirical or quantitative. The paper does not provide direct evidence or predictions about efficiency or payoff changes due to punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment and meta-punishment mechanisms can stabilize cooperation in small, egalitarian groups, especially in contexts analogous to public goods dilemmas (e.g., meat sharing after cooperative hunting). However, it does not provide empirical estimates or direct predictions about efficiency or group payoff outcomes with or without punishment. Its relevance is mainly in supporting the plausibility of punishment as a stabilizer of cooperation, especially when punishment is collective, reputational, and embedded in group norms. For prediction tasks, this paper may inform the expected direction of punishment effects in small, highly interactive, reputation-sensitive groups, but it does not provide quantitative or design-specific guidance.

  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
