  findings: The paper finds that (1) lower average degree and higher clustering coefficient in scale-free networks promote higher cooperation frequency in the iterated prisoner's dilemma; (2) introducing a punishment mechanism, where unsatisfied cooperators can sever and rewire links to defectors with some probability, further increases the frequency and persistence of cooperation. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and all results are based on simulation of strategy frequencies and network structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation frequency in networked iterated prisoner's dilemma environments, especially when combined with certain network structures. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings may be useful for understanding the behavioral mechanisms by which punishment can promote cooperation, but should be used with caution for payoff-based prediction tasks.

- source: Romano, A., Saral, A. S., & Wu, J. H. (2022). Direct and indirect reciprocity among individuals and groups. *Current Opinion in Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that group membership, gossip, and third-party punishment promote prosocial behavior through psychological mechanisms related to direct and indirect reciprocity. Third-party punishment is framed as a tool for upregulating reputation and deterring mistreatment, which in turn increases prosocial behavior among others. The review highlights that reputational concern and anticipation of future interaction are key drivers of both prosocial and punitive behaviors. However, the review does not report or synthesize results on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and focuses on behavioral and psychological outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and conceptual support for the idea that punishment (especially third-party punishment) can increase prosocial behavior, but it does not provide quantitative or direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their variants. It is useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators (e.g., reputation, observability, group membership), but does not inform direct prediction of efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is best used as background on psychological mechanisms and as a pointer to empirical work, rather than as a source of quantitative or design-specific evidence.

- source: Giardini, F. (2012). Deterrence and transmission as mechanisms ensuring reliability of gossip. *Cognitive Processing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that deterrence (punishment for unreliable gossip) and transmission (the ability to hide the source of information) jointly ensure the reliability and evolutionary stability of gossip. It suggests that the threat of punishment alone would make gossip too risky, but the ability to transmit information as 'reputation' (with an unknown source) allows individuals to avoid direct responsibility and punishment. The paper references agent-based simulations showing that when agents can only use direct experience, average payoffs are lower than when communication (gossip/reputation) is allowed. It also cites experimental economics studies where reputation mechanisms increase cooperation and prevent overuse of public resources. However, these findings are not original to this paper and are used to support the theoretical argument.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how punishment and reputation mechanisms may affect cooperation and, indirectly, group efficiency in social dilemmas. It does not provide direct empirical evidence or parameterized results that can be used for quantitative prediction of efficiency in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it supports the general idea that punishment (deterrence) can sustain cooperation, but only when paired with mechanisms (like transmission/reputation) that allow information to spread without excessive risk to informers. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment and reputation mechanisms can increase cooperation and possibly efficiency, but the effect depends on the balance between deterrence and the ability to avoid punishment via transmission. The lack of direct empirical or payoff data limits its utility for precise prediction.

- source: Glover, J., & Kim, E. (2021). Optimal Team Composition: Diversity to Foster Implicit Team Incentives. *Management Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated team production, the efficiency of implicit team incentives (mutual monitoring and punishment for free-riding) depends critically on team composition and the expected duration of the relationship (discount factor). Diverse teams (with productive complementarity) always have a monotonic relationship between tenure and incentive efficiency: as tenure increases, the cost of providing incentives decreases, and collusion is not a concern. Specialized teams (with productive substitutability) can initially benefit from longer tenure (lower incentive costs), but as tenure increases further, collusion becomes a problem, raising the cost of incentives and reducing efficiency. The model provides explicit formulas and parameter thresholds for when each team type is optimal. The main efficiency outcome is the principal's expected payoff, which is maximized by choosing the team composition that best balances productive efficiency and incentive efficiency, given the risk of collusion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms (mutual monitoring, implicit threats) in repeated team production environments, especially as a function of team composition (diversity vs. specialization), the discount factor (tenure), and the production technology (complementarity vs. substitutability). While not a public goods game per se, the model is adjacent and highly relevant for settings where group payoff depends on repeated cooperation and the threat of future punishment. The results show that the efficiency effect of punishment depends on whether collusion is possible and on the structure of the team. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling punishment (mutual monitoring) generally increases efficiency in diverse teams and in specialized teams with short tenure, but may reduce efficiency in specialized teams with long tenure due to collusion. The explicit formulas and parameter thresholds can inform quantitative predictions if the relevant parameters are known.

- source: Abdulkadiroglu, A., & Bagwell, K. (2013). Trust, Reciprocity, and Favors in Cooperative Relationships. *American Economic Journal-microeconomics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated trust games with private information, efficiency (total group payoff) can be increased above the autarky (Nash) level through favor-exchange relationships (dynamic reciprocity) or through symmetric punishment (negative reciprocity). The optimal equilibrium structure depends on the parameters: when information asymmetries are low, symmetric punishment can support higher efficiency than favor-exchange. Hybrid equilibria (honeymoon period followed by favor-exchange or punishment) can further improve efficiency in some cases. The paper provides explicit formulas for the efficiency achieved under each regime. Immediate reciprocity (punishment for non-reciprocation) is not always necessary; dynamic reciprocity (future favor) is often sufficient. The model is not a PGG but is closely related in its focus on cooperation, trust, and efficiency under repeated interaction and private information.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical predictions for how efficiency in repeated cooperation games is affected by the availability and structure of punishment and favor-exchange mechanisms. While not a PGG, the model is adjacent and the efficiency formulas and comparative statics are informative for predicting efficiency in environments where punishment (symmetric, infrequent) is possible versus when only favor-exchange is available. The results suggest that, in some parameter regions, introducing symmetric punishment can increase efficiency above favor-exchange alone, especially when information asymmetries are low. However, the model is limited to two players and does not include standard PGG features like group size or marginal per-capita return. The findings are most useful for understanding the theoretical potential of punishment to enhance efficiency in repeated cooperation with private information, but direct quantitative prediction for PGGs would require caution.

- source: Frean, M. R., & Abraham, E. R. (2004). Adaptation and enslavement in endosymbiont-host associations - art. no. 051913. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The main finding is that in a two-species repeated cooperation game with differential adaptation rates, the slower-adapting species (host) evolves to provide just enough cooperation to keep the faster-adapting species (symbiont) maximally cooperative. This 'miser-slave' dynamic leads to highly asymmetric payoffs: hosts receive nearly all the benefit, while symbionts receive very little, even though they are highly cooperative. The total group payoff (efficiency) is much less than the maximum possible (full mutual cooperation), because the host withholds cooperation. The model provides a theoretical explanation for observed asymmetries in natural symbioses, where hosts benefit much more than symbionts. The findings are robust across a range of cost-benefit ratios and are supported by both analytic and simulation results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a theoretical mechanism showing that, in repeated cooperation games with contingent strategies and differential adaptation rates, the introduction of a contingent response (analogous to punishment or reward) by one party can lead to highly asymmetric efficiency outcomes. Specifically, if the 'punishing' party adapts more slowly, it can extract maximal cooperation from the other party while contributing minimally itself, resulting in low overall efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the structure of contingent responses and adaptation rates can strongly moderate the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms. However, the model is not a standard public goods game, and the 'punishment' is implemented as a contingent reward/withholding rather than explicit costly punishment. Thus, while the findings are informative about the potential for punishment/contingency to reduce efficiency via asymmetry, direct quantitative predictions for PGGs with explicit punishment should be made with caution.

- source: Ho, T. H. (1996). Finite automata play repeated prisoner's dilemma with information processing costs. *Journal of Economic Dynamics & Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The main findings are: (1) In a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with boundedly rational agents (finite automata), cooperation and high efficiency can evolve even from hostile initial conditions, as long as there are no costs for strategy complexity. (2) Imposing a cost on the size of the automaton (i.e., the 'storage' or monitoring cost) destroys cooperation and reduces efficiency, with populations converging to simple, defecting strategies. (3) Imposing a cost on the frequency of switching states (i.e., 'retrieval' or attention cost) does not harm, and may even promote, cooperation and efficiency, especially if the cost is quadratic. (4) The results are robust to a range of simulation parameters. (5) The findings suggest that the form of cognitive or implementation cost is a critical moderator of whether punishment-like strategies (contingent defection) can sustain cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated two-player games structurally similar to public goods games (but not group PGGs), the ability to implement contingent punishment (e.g., Tit-for-Tat or more sophisticated automata) is crucial for sustaining high efficiency. However, if the game design imposes costs on monitoring or strategy complexity (analogous to high punishment cost or high cognitive burden), efficiency collapses and simple, non-cooperative strategies dominate. In contrast, costs on the frequency of switching (analogous to costs for changing behavior or attention) do not undermine efficiency. For prediction, this suggests that the presence and form of punishment, and especially the cost structure for implementing contingent strategies, are key moderators of the efficiency effect. The results are most directly relevant to two-player, long-horizon, repeated games with deterministic feedback and no communication.

- source: Kraines, D. P., & Kraines, V. Y. (2000). Natural selection of memory-one strategies for the iterated prisoner's dilemma. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that, in the iterated Prisoner's Dilemma with memory-one strategies and evolutionary dynamics, the population tends to evolve toward a small set of stable strategies. The Pavlov (Win-Stay Lose-Switch) strategy is particularly robust, achieving high average payoffs and being resistant to invasion by other strategies, especially in the presence of noise. The analysis partitions the space of strategies into basins of attraction: cooperative (high payoff), defecting (low payoff), alternating (zero payoff), and a small equilibrium plane. The size of these basins depends on the initial conditions and the payoff matrix. The paper also shows that strategies that combine trust with readiness to retaliate (but not excessive forgiveness) are most successful. While explicit punishment mechanisms are not modeled, the evolutionary success of strategies that retaliate after being exploited is highlighted.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. While it does not study public goods games or explicit punishment institutions, it analyzes the efficiency (average payoff) of strategies in a closely related repeated social dilemma (the IPD) under evolutionary dynamics. The findings suggest that strategies incorporating contingent retaliation (analogous to punishment) can sustain high efficiency, but only if they avoid excessive forgiveness or spite. The mapping of strategy space to efficiency outcomes can inform predictions about the likely efficiency of repeated games with different behavioral rules, especially regarding the role of contingent sanctions. However, the lack of explicit group structure, multi-player interactions, and institutional punishment limits direct transfer to public goods games.

- source: Shibayama, S. (2015). Academic commercialization and changing nature of academic cooperation. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that increasing the prevalence of commercial actors (who do not cooperate gratis) in an academic community undermines the reputation mechanism that sustains indirect reciprocity. This leads to a decline in unconditional cooperation, a rise in demand for direct rewards (e.g., coauthorship, barter), and an increase in abstention from cooperation. The overall effect is a reduction in group efficiency and mean payoff, as the system moves away from the collectively optimal state. Centralized rewarding can partially restore cooperation and efficiency, but only if the reward is carefully calibrated; excessive rewards can backfire by incentivizing defectors. The model provides formal predictions and phase diagrams showing how the basin of attraction for efficient cooperation shrinks as commercialization increases.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the presence and design of punishment, reward, and reputation mechanisms are critical moderators of efficiency in public-goods-like environments. While not a direct PGG or lab experiment, the model is closely related and analyzes efficiency outcomes as a function of game design parameters (e.g., cost/benefit of cooperation, reward size, reputation availability, prevalence of defectors). For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling effective punishment or reward (especially centralized, carefully sized rewards) can increase efficiency, but that the presence of many defectors or commercial actors will reduce the effectiveness of reputation-based cooperation and lower efficiency. The model also highlights the importance of the matching rate for rewards and the availability of reputation information. However, the absence of empirical or experimental data means that quantitative predictions should be made with caution.

- source: Amegashie, J. A., & Runkel, M. (2012). The Paradox of Revenge in Conflicts. *Journal of Conflict Resolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that, contrary to conventional wisdom, the desire for revenge does not always escalate conflict. In a two-period contest model, the anticipation of revenge (self-deterrence effect) can lead factions to reduce their initial effort, resulting in lower total conflict costs (higher efficiency) than in a setting without revenge motives. The paradox is most likely when the benefit of revenge is highly elastic to past destruction or when there are asymmetries in the value of revenge or resource valuation between factions. The model provides explicit comparative statics and closed-form solutions for aggregate effort with and without revenge, showing conditions under which efficiency is improved by the possibility of revenge.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the introduction of a punishment/revenge mechanism can, under certain conditions, increase efficiency (reduce total cost) in conflict-like games by inducing self-deterrence. While the setting is a two-player, two-period conflict game (not a standard public goods game), the mechanism is closely related to punishment in repeated social dilemmas. The findings suggest that, when the threat of punishment is credible and especially when the benefit of punishment is highly sensitive to prior harm, the net effect on efficiency can be positive. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the possibility that enabling punishment can increase efficiency, especially in asymmetric or highly elastic settings, but the result is context-dependent and may not generalize to all PGG-like environments.

- source: Al-Dhanhani, A., Mizouni, R., Otrok, H., & Al-Rubaie, A. (2014). A game theoretical model for collaborative groups in social applications. *Expert Systems with Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of reciprocity and punishment strategies in repeated social dilemmas (modeled as asynchronous iterated prisoner's dilemma) has a strong effect on group efficiency and the distribution of gains between cooperative and selfish (free riding) users. Classical Tit for Tat leads to deadlock and declining payoffs. Generous Tit for Tat avoids deadlock and increases group gain, but allows free riders to benefit disproportionately. Reputation-based Tit for Tat reduces free rider gain but can also reduce cooperative user gain, especially if the reputation threshold is set high or the initialization phase is short. Group reputation-based Tit for Tat (indirect reciprocity) achieves the highest cooperative user gain and lowest free rider gain, thus maximizing group efficiency and survivability. The presence of free riders always reduces group efficiency, but the right punishment/reciprocity mechanism can mitigate this loss.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the design of punishment/reciprocity mechanisms (especially those based on group reputation and indirect reciprocity) can significantly improve group efficiency in repeated social dilemma environments, even in the presence of free riders. While the model is not a standard public goods game, it is closely related and the efficiency outcomes are directly comparable. The findings suggest that enabling punishment or exclusion based on group-level reputation (rather than only direct reciprocity) is likely to increase efficiency relative to no punishment or to simple direct reciprocity. The results are most relevant for settings with repeated interactions, observable histories, and the ability to track and act on group-level reputation. The paper does not provide empirical estimates or direct mappings to all design dimensions, but the qualitative direction is clear: more sophisticated, group-based punishment/reciprocity mechanisms are more effective at sustaining efficiency.

- source: Bravo, G. (2011). Agents' beliefs and the evolution of institutions for common-pool resource management. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The key finding is that enabling agents to build management institutions in a CPR environment leads to much higher efficiency and group payoffs compared to scenarios where agents act only on individual beliefs. In the base (no-institution) model, payoffs are 12–35% of the optimum; with institution building, payoffs rise to 72–91% of the optimum. The effect is robust to changes in cost, resource abundance, and agent tolerance. The models suggest that the feedback loop between beliefs and institutions is critical: institutions prevent the selection of purely self-interested, over-exploiting agents, allowing cooperation and sustainable resource use to persist. The results are consistent with empirical findings from CPR experiments and field studies, though the models do not include explicit punishment or sanctioning.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper provides strong evidence that the introduction of effective, collectively agreed-upon management institutions in CPR/public-goods-like environments can transform group efficiency from low (tragedy of the commons) to high (near-optimal). While the models do not include explicit punishment, the institution acts as a binding rule, functionally similar to enforced cooperation. The results suggest that, in designs where institution-building is possible and effective, one should predict a large positive jump in efficiency relative to the control. However, the absence of explicit punishment or sanctioning means the findings are most directly relevant to institution-building or rule-setting treatments, not to punishment per se.

- source: Sasaki, T., Okada, I., & Nakai, Y. (2016). Indirect reciprocity can overcome free-rider problems on costly moral assessment. *Biology Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in indirect reciprocity models with costly assessment, cooperation is not stable unless there is a mechanism to sanction or exclude those who evade the cost of assessment. By introducing a pre-assessment stage that labels non-payers as 'nasty' and excludes them from receiving help, the model shows that a cooperative equilibrium (where everyone pays for assessment and helps others) can be locally stable if the benefit of helping exceeds the cost of assessment. This mechanism is analogous to pool punishment in public goods games and is effective under leading social norms. The findings are theoretical but directly address efficiency and the conditions for stable cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that institutional mechanisms to sanction or exclude cost-evaders (such as pre-assessment or pool punishment) can significantly improve efficiency in social dilemma games with costly monitoring or punishment. For prediction tasks, the presence of a pre-assessment or institutionalized punishment mechanism is likely to shift efficiency from low (collapse of cooperation) to high (stable cooperation), provided the net benefit of cooperation is positive. The results are robust across different social norms and suggest that the design of the punishment/sanctioning mechanism is a key moderator of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Zhang, J. L., Zhu, Y. Y., & Chen, Z. Q. (2020). Evolutionary Game Dynamics of Multiagent Systems on Multiple Community Networks. *Ieee Transactions on Systems Man Cybernetics-systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of the interaction network (community structure and interaction probabilities) and the payoff matrix jointly determine which strategies dominate and what equilibrium payoffs are achieved. In dominating games (like the prisoner's dilemma), the system converges to full defection or cooperation, mirroring well-mixed results. In coexistence games (like the snowdrift game), the system can reach an interior equilibrium with mixed strategies, provided certain conditions on the interaction matrix are met. In coordination games (like the stag-hunt), multiple stable equilibria are possible, and the final state depends on initial conditions and network structure. The paper provides sufficient conditions for the stability of equilibria and shows that higher payoff strategies tend to dominate. However, it does not analyze the effect of punishment, reward, or other institutional interventions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how network structure and payoff matrices affect equilibrium efficiency and welfare in multiagent evolutionary games. However, it does not address the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it model public goods games directly. Its results are most useful for understanding baseline efficiency outcomes in structured populations without institutional interventions. It does not inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from punishment or reward mechanisms, but it does clarify how efficiency depends on game type and network structure in the absence of such mechanisms.

- source: Golubski, A. J., & Klausmeier, C. A. (2010). Control in mutualisms Combined implications of partner choice and bargaining roles. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the ability to appropriate surplus (analogous to control over punishment/reward) and the ability to choose partners (partner choice) interact to determine the evolution of mutualism and the distribution of payoffs in mutualistic associations. When the guild with partner choice also receives the surplus, mutualistic strategies are favored and average payoffs are high. However, the presence of empty slots or high frequencies of exploiters can lead to cyclical dynamics where exploitative strategies temporarily increase, reducing average payoffs. The model predicts that partner choice is most effective at selecting against moderately exploitative partners, but highly exploitative partners can persist under some conditions. The overall effect on efficiency is mixed: partner choice and surplus control can increase efficiency, but not always, especially if exploiters are present or if association opportunities are limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that mechanisms analogous to punishment (partner choice, surplus appropriation) can increase efficiency (group payoff) in mutualism-like bargaining games, especially when combined. However, the effect is not universally positive: the presence of exploiters and the structure of association opportunities (e.g., empty slots) can lead to reduced efficiency or cyclical dynamics. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling partner choice and surplus control generally increases efficiency, but the effect may be moderated or reversed if exploiters can persist or if association opportunities are limited. The model is adjacent to public goods games, so its predictions should be transferred with caution, but it provides informative indirect evidence about the conditions under which punishment-like mechanisms improve or fail to improve efficiency.

- source: Wang, R. W., & Shi, L. (2010). The evolution of cooperation in asymmetric systems. *Science China-life Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in asymmetric cooperative systems, the net benefit (efficiency) to the recipient depends critically on the intrinsic growth rates of benefit and cost, the strength of density-dependent interference competition among cooperative actors, and the availability of common resources. Stronger density-dependent interference competition among cooperative actors and higher initial relatedness (intrinsic contribution ratio) increase the probability of maintaining cooperation (positive efficiency). However, changes in these parameters or resource saturation can lead to a transition from cooperation to conflict, with net benefit becoming negative. The model also shows that recipient-enforced discrimination (punishing cheaters, rewarding cooperators) can increase initial relatedness and favor cooperation, but does not guarantee global stability. The system can exhibit oscillatory or chaotic dynamics between cooperation and conflict.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how efficiency in public-goods-like asymmetric systems is affected by the number of actors, resource saturation, and density-dependent competition. It suggests that efficiency gains from cooperation are not guaranteed by punishment or sanctions alone, but depend on ecological and structural parameters such as the strength of interference competition and resource availability. For prediction tasks, the model implies that enabling punishment (in the form of recipient-enforced discrimination) can increase efficiency only when it effectively increases initial relatedness and when density-dependent competition among actors is strong enough to prevent resource overexploitation. However, efficiency can decrease or oscillate if these conditions are not met. The findings are most relevant for systems with asymmetric roles and may be less directly transferable to standard symmetric PGG lab games.

- source: Szabó, G., & Bunth, G. (2018). Social dilemmas in multistrategy evolutionary potential games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that in symmetric potential games, efficiency (measured as average payoff or contentment) is maximized when the symmetric part of the payoff matrix dominates, aligning individual and collective interests. The introduction of antisymmetric (deluding) components can create social dilemmas, leading to inefficient Nash equilibria and lower average payoffs. The likelihood of inefficiency increases with the number of strategies. However, if the antisymmetric component supports the same equilibrium as the symmetric part, it can help maintain efficiency, especially under noise. The paper provides explicit mappings of parameter regions where efficiency is high or low, but does not study punishment or public goods games directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how the structure of payoff matrices in multi-strategy evolutionary games affects efficiency, especially the role of antisymmetric (deluding) components in creating or resolving social dilemmas. While it does not study public goods games or punishment directly, it offers a framework for predicting when efficiency will be high or low based on the alignment of individual and collective incentives. For the downstream prediction task, the results suggest that efficiency is sensitive to the presence of payoff components that create conflicts between individual and group interests, and that the probability of inefficiency increases with the number of available strategies. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment mechanisms or specific public goods game design dimensions.

- source: Martinez-Vaquero, L. A., Han, T. A., Pereira, L. M., & Lenaerts, T. (2017). When agreement-accepting free-riders are a necessary evil for the evolution of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in one-shot games, the coexistence of proposers and acceptors can be necessary to keep defectors out of the population, especially when the cost of commitment must be paid before knowing if the other will accept (prior commitments). In repeated games, proposers alone can sustain cooperation, and the presence of acceptors can actually reduce cooperation by making it easier for defectors to invade. The introduction of an apology-forgiveness mechanism (where apologies after defection can restore cooperation) can be exploited by acceptors if the apology cost is too low, leading to lower cooperation. The results are framed in terms of evolutionary stability and the stationary distribution of strategies, with average payoff as the fitness criterion, but do not directly report group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, but it does offer adjacent theoretical insights about how commitment mechanisms (which can be seen as a form of pre-emptive contract or compensation for defection) affect the prevalence of cooperation and the exclusion of defectors. The findings suggest that the design of commitment mechanisms (timing of costs, ability to recognize intentions, presence of apology-forgiveness) can strongly moderate the success of cooperation-promoting strategies and thus the average payoff in the population. However, because the model is based on the prisoner's dilemma and not a public goods game, and does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning as in PGGs, its direct predictive value for the downstream task is limited. The results are most relevant for understanding how alternative mechanisms to punishment (commitments, compensation, apology) might affect cooperation and payoff in related social dilemmas.

- source: Han, T. A., & Tran-Thanh, L. (2018). Cost-effective external interference for promoting the evolution of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: This work provides analytical and numerical results showing that an external decision-maker can promote high levels of cooperation in a population of self-interested agents by using a cost-efficient, state-dependent rewarding scheme. Rather than always rewarding cooperators, it is more cost-effective to reward only when the number of cooperators is below a threshold. The optimal threshold depends on the per-individual reward cost, the intensity of selection, and the desired cooperation level. For a wide range of parameters, this targeted approach achieves the same or higher cooperation at a lower total cost than always-on institutional incentives. The model does not directly analyze group payoff or efficiency in the public goods sense, but the results are closely related to maximizing cooperation frequency while minimizing resource expenditure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is relevant for predicting the effect of reward-based interventions (not punishment) on cooperation and cost-efficiency in Prisoner's Dilemma-like settings. It suggests that targeted, conditional incentives can be more cost-effective than unconditional ones, especially under weak selection. However, the model is not a public goods game and does not analyze punishment, so direct quantitative predictions for PGG efficiency with punishment are not possible. The findings support the idea that the design of incentive schemes (e.g., when and whom to reward) strongly moderates the cost-effectiveness of cooperation promotion, and that always-on incentives may be suboptimal. For downstream prediction, the results are most informative for settings with external, state-dependent rewards, and less so for punishment or standard PGGs.

- source: Fulker, Z., Forber, P., Smead, R., & Riedl, C. (2021). Spite is contagious in dynamic networks. *Nature Communications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in dynamic networks where agents can choose interaction partners and update their strategies by imitating higher-payoff individuals, spiteful (costly harmful) behavior can reliably invade and fixate in the population. This occurs even when the overall network does not exhibit strong anti-correlation, due to local and transient anti-correlated interactions. The spread of spite is facilitated by reinforcement learning and imitation based on average payoff per interaction. When spiteful behavior dominates, average payoffs are lower than in populations of only 'Social' agents, indicating a reduction in group efficiency. The findings challenge classic conditions for the evolution of costly harm and suggest that dynamic partner choice is a powerful mechanism for the emergence of antisocial behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is primarily theoretical and does not directly study public goods games or experimental punishment interventions. However, it provides indirect evidence that dynamic partner choice and imitation based on payoffs can enable the spread of costly harmful behaviors, which reduce group efficiency. In environments where agents can choose partners and update strategies based on payoffs, the introduction of punishment-like costly harm may decrease efficiency, especially if such behaviors can spread by imitation. The findings suggest caution in predicting positive efficiency effects from punishment in dynamic or endogenous network settings, as costly harm can become self-reinforcing and reduce welfare. The model is adjacent to PGGs and does not include a cooperative contribution stage, so direct quantitative prediction is limited, but the qualitative mechanism is relevant for understanding the risks of antisocial punishment or spite in flexible social environments.

- source: Angourakis, A., Santos, J. I., Galán, J. M., & Balbo, A. L. (2015). Food for all: An agent-based model to explore the emergence and implications of cooperation for food storage. *Environmental Archaeology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The model demonstrates that cooperation in food storage is most likely to emerge and persist when (1) cooperative storage is at least as efficient as household storage, (2) there is moderate to high dependency on storable food, and (3) social norms regulating access to the cooperative stock are neither too strict nor too lax. Too strict enforcement (high reciprocity requirement) can undermine cooperation by excluding agents who fail to contribute due to bad luck, while too lax enforcement allows free-riding to undermine the common stock. The model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff in the public goods sense, but shows that cooperation increases the probability of avoiding shortages and, under certain conditions, can lead to surplus. The findings suggest that moderate sanctioning (via access control) is optimal for sustaining cooperation in common-pool resource settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff in a public goods game with explicit punishment, it models a closely related common-pool resource dilemma where access to the common resource is conditional on past cooperation (a form of sanction). The results suggest that intermediate levels of sanctioning (not too strict, not too lax) maximize cooperation and system performance (in terms of shortage avoidance and potential surplus). For prediction tasks, this implies that punishment or exclusion mechanisms that are too harsh may reduce efficiency by discouraging recovery from bad luck, while too little enforcement allows free-riding. The model also highlights the importance of storage efficiency and dependency on the resource, which may map onto MPCR and baseline efficiency in public goods games. However, direct quantitative predictions of efficiency with and without punishment are not available from this model.

- source: Cordes, C., Richerson, P. J., & Schwesinger, G. (2010). How corporate cultures coevolve with the business environment: The case of firm growth crises and industry evolution. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation within firms is sustainable up to a critical group size, after which opportunistic behavior spreads and performance declines. In innovative, uncertain environments, cooperative cultures provide a competitive advantage, but as firms grow, the influence of informal leadership wanes and cooperation collapses. In mature, stable environments, large firms can succeed by implementing monitoring regimes and realizing economies of scale, even with more opportunistic employees. The model links these dynamics to firm growth crises, industry shakeouts, and the evolution of market structure. While the model is not a direct PGG or punishment experiment, it provides theoretical insight into how group size, monitoring, and social learning affect cooperation and firm performance.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical support for the idea that group size and the presence or absence of monitoring (analogous to punishment) are key moderators of cooperation and efficiency in group settings. It suggests that in small groups, informal cooperation can sustain high performance, but as group size increases, cooperation collapses unless formal monitoring (akin to punishment) is introduced. The model implies that enabling monitoring or sanctions in large groups can restore or maintain efficiency, especially in stable environments. However, the findings are not directly transferable to PGG lab experiments, as the context is organizational and the outcomes are modeled rather than measured. The paper is most useful for understanding the qualitative effects of group size and monitoring on efficiency, rather than for quantitative prediction.

- source: Wu, J. H., Számadó, S., Barclay, P., Beersma, B., Cruz, T. D. D., Lo Iacono, S., Nieper, A. S., Peters, K., Przepiorka, W., Tiokhin, L., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2021). Honesty and dishonesty in gossip strategies: a fitness interdependence analysis. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the honesty of gossip is determined by the gossiper's fitness interdependence with the receiver and target, and by the marginal cost/benefit structure of the underlying game between receiver and target. A simple matching rule is proposed: gossipers are honest when their fitness interdependence aligns with the effect of honest gossip, and dishonest when it mismatches. The model is applied to four game types, including a punishment game, but the focus is on the strategic choice of gossip honesty rather than on direct efficiency or payoff outcomes. The paper suggests that honest gossip can support cooperation, but that dishonest gossip can undermine reputation-based cooperation, especially when fitness interdependence is negative or mixed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how information transmission (gossip) can act as a low-cost reputational punishment mechanism in social dilemmas, including punishment games. However, it does not provide empirical or simulated data on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it manipulate or analyze the standard public goods game dimensions. Its main contribution is to clarify when gossip will be honest or dishonest, which can indirectly affect cooperation and efficiency, but the paper does not quantify these effects. For prediction tasks about punishment effects on efficiency in public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by highlighting the importance of information accuracy and fitness interdependence in the effectiveness of reputational sanctions.

- source: Manapat, M. L., Rand, D. G., Pawlowitsch, C., & Nowak, M. A. (2012). Stochastic evolutionary dynamics resolve the Traveler's Dilemma. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in the Traveler's Dilemma, evolutionary dynamics favor higher (more cooperative) claims when the reward/penalty R is small relative to the maximum claim M, and lower (less cooperative) claims when R is large. The model reproduces the empirical finding that average claims decrease as R increases. The analysis is focused on the distribution of claims, not on group efficiency or total payoff, and the Traveler's Dilemma is structurally different from public goods games: it is a two-player, competitive undercutting game with a specific bonus/penalty mechanism, not a multi-player contribution game. The findings are relevant for understanding how incentive structures (like R) shape cooperative behavior, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency or punishment effects in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, adjacent evidence for the prediction task. It shows that in a two-player social dilemma with a reward/penalty for undercutting (Traveler's Dilemma), increasing the size of the penalty/reward (R) leads to less cooperative behavior (lower claims). However, the game structure is not a public goods game, and the outcomes are not group efficiency or total payoff, but rather the average claim. There is no direct manipulation or analysis of punishment in a PGG or direct efficiency outcomes. Thus, the paper may inform general intuitions about how incentive structures affect cooperation, but should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Guo, H., Chu, C., Shen, C., & Shi, L. (2018). Reputation-based coevolution of link weights promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that reputation-based coevolution of link weights in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game promotes cooperation. When link weights are allowed to vary (parameterized by delta), higher heterogeneity in link weights leads to higher levels of cooperation. Punishment is implemented as a reduction in link weight to the lowest-reputation neighbor, and reward as an increase to the highest-reputation neighbor. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff. The effect of punishment and reward is to facilitate the formation of cooperative clusters, which resist invasion by defectors. The effect is stronger for larger delta (greater possible link weight variation).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation-based punishment and reward mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial social dilemma settings. However, because the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game), and the outcome is cooperation rate rather than efficiency or group payoff, its direct applicability to predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games is limited. The findings may be useful for understanding the role of network structure, reputation, and coevolving relationships in promoting cooperation, but do not provide quantitative guidance for efficiency prediction in PGG-like environments.

- source: Madeo, D., & Mocenni, C. (2020). Self-regulation versus social influence for promoting cooperation on networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that strong self-regulation (relative to social influence from neighbors) can lead to full cooperation in networked prisoner's dilemma games, while weak self-regulation leads to defection. For intermediate self-regulation, complex behaviors such as partial cooperation, reciprocity, and anti-reciprocity emerge. Network topology matters: scale-free networks foster higher average cooperation than Erdös-Rényi or regular networks, especially when self-regulation is moderate. The model suggests that social diversity and heterogeneity in network structure can promote cooperation, even when the 'cost' of self-regulation is not high. However, the study does not analyze efficiency or group payoff directly, nor does it include explicit punishment or reward treatments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It models a continuous-strategy prisoner's dilemma with self-regulation (an internal, not externally imposed, mechanism) and analyzes cooperation levels, not efficiency or group payoff. There is no manipulation of punishment, punishment cost, or related design dimensions. The findings may be contextually informative for understanding how intrinsic motivation or network structure can affect cooperation, but do not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment-enabled efficiency.

- source: Wu, Y. E., Zhang, S. H., & Zhang, Z. P. (2018). Environment-based preference selection promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Scientific Reports*.

- source: Wardil, L., & da Silva, J. K. L. (2010). Distinguishing the opponents promotes cooperation in well-mixed populations. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that allowing players to distinguish between opponents and update their strategies in the interaction that gave the worst payoff leads to the evolutionary stability of cooperation in well-mixed populations. Under synchronous update, cooperation eventually dominates, while under asynchronous update, the fraction of cooperation remains at the lower bound set by the initial mutual cooperation. The mechanism of 'implicit punishment'—where exploitative (C,D) interactions are replaced by mutual defection (D,D)—prevents defectors from exploiting cooperators indefinitely, but does not involve explicit costly punishment. The model does not include explicit payoff-based punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is of limited direct use for predicting the effect of explicit punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants, as it studies a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with a novel update rule and no explicit costly punishment or reward. The 'implicit punishment' mechanism is a structural feature of the strategy update process, not a player choice to pay a cost to reduce another's payoff. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff. The findings may be relevant for understanding how mechanisms that neutralize exploitation without explicit costs can stabilize cooperation, but do not provide quantitative evidence on the efficiency effects of punishment in standard PGG-like environments.

  findings: The paper develops a theoretical model of the asymmetric Hawk-Dove game, showing that the frequency of cooperation increases with greater asymmetry between players (i.e., when one player is much stronger or more dominant than the other). The model also shows that a lower cost-to-benefit ratio of cooperation increases cooperation frequency. The discussion links these findings to real-world systems where dominant individuals can punish non-cooperators, suggesting that credible punishment and asymmetry can stabilize cooperation. However, the paper does not provide direct predictions or data on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the probability of cooperation and conflict.

Thus, while the paper suggests that punishment and asymmetry can facilitate cooperation, it does not provide direct evidence for the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Santos, F. P., Pacheco, J. M., & Santos, F. C. (2021). The complexity of human cooperation under indirect reciprocity. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper reviews the literature on indirect reciprocity, emphasizing how the complexity of social norms and strategies affects the evolution and maintenance of cooperation. It synthesizes theoretical and computational results showing that: (1) more complex social norms can, in principle, sustain higher cooperation, but are harder for individuals to implement; (2) the costliness of reputation spreading, the observability of interactions, and empathy are key moderators of cooperation under indirect reciprocity; (3) when complexity costs are introduced, simpler norms may outperform more complex ones in sustaining cooperation. The paper provides a proof-of-concept simulation showing that cooperation rates under different social norms decline as the cost of implementing complex strategies increases. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or direct welfare outcomes, and does not manipulate or analyze punishment in a public goods context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not report or synthesize results on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, nor does it manipulate punishment or sanctions. Its main relevance is in highlighting that the complexity of social norms and the cognitive costs of implementing strategies can moderate cooperation rates in reputation-based systems. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff outcomes in PGG-like games with punishment, this paper provides only indirect, contextual background, not quantitative or directly transferable evidence.

  findings: The paper argues that human cooperation evolved in two major transitions: first, among Pleistocene foragers, where cooperation was individually advantageous and stabilized by mutualism, reciprocation, and punishment of freeriders (both shirkers and bullies); and second, in the Holocene, where larger, more hierarchical societies required new mechanisms (including group selection and intergroup conflict) to maintain cooperation. Punishment is discussed as a key mechanism for controlling freeriding and maintaining cooperation, but the analysis is conceptual and evolutionary rather than experimental or quantitative. The paper references experimental public goods games and their findings (e.g., punishment stabilizes cooperation), but only as background for theoretical arguments.

However, it supports the idea that punishment is more likely to stabilize cooperation (and thus efficiency) in small, transparent, repeated-interaction groups, and that the effectiveness of punishment may depend on social structure, group size, and transparency. For prediction tasks, this paper is best used as background for mechanism hypotheses rather than as a source of direct predictive evidence.

- source: Isoni, A., & Sugden, R. (2019). Reciprocity and the Paradox of Trust in psychological game theory. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The main finding is that models of reciprocal kindness, as formalized in psychological game theory (e.g., Rabin 1993, Charness & Rabin 2002, Falk & Fischbacher 2006), cannot explain mutually beneficial trust and trustworthiness in Trust Games—this is termed the 'Paradox of Trust.' The paper argues that trust and trustworthiness are better explained by a different form of reciprocity, namely reciprocal cooperation, where individuals are motivated to play their part in mutually beneficial practices conditional on others doing the same. The paper sketches how this approach can be applied to public goods games, but does not provide empirical results or efficiency data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative results that can be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. However, it is conceptually relevant for understanding the psychological underpinnings of cooperation and the limitations of certain reciprocity models. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that models focusing solely on rewarding/punishing intentions (reciprocal kindness) may not capture the mechanisms that lead to efficient cooperation in trust or public goods games. Instead, models of reciprocal cooperation may be more appropriate for predicting when mutually beneficial outcomes (i.e., high efficiency) can arise. The paper does not address specific game design dimensions or provide data on how punishment affects efficiency.

- source: Morris, A., & Cushman, F. (2018). A COMMON FRAMEWORK FOR THEORIES OF NORM COMPLIANCE. *Social Philosophy & Policy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper synthesizes and formalizes four major theories of norm compliance—planning (folk theory), habit, internalization, and choice set construction—within a reinforcement learning framework. It argues that norm compliance can arise from explicit planning over expected rewards (including punishment/reward), from habits formed by past reinforcement, from internalized intrinsic rewards for norm compliance, or from the exclusion of norm-violating actions from consideration. The paper reviews evidence from economic games (including public goods and ultimatum games) to illustrate the persistence of norm compliance even in the absence of external enforcement, but does not report new data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. The framework highlights that the mechanisms by which punishment or sanctions affect behavior may differ (e.g., via planning, habit, or internalization), and that these mechanisms have different implications for the persistence and malleability of norm compliance.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. However, it is useful for understanding the possible cognitive mechanisms by which punishment, reward, and norm salience may influence cooperation and compliance. The framework suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on whether compliance is driven by planning (sensitive to incentives), habit (sensitive to past reinforcement), or internalization (less sensitive to external incentives). For prediction tasks, this implies that the design dimensions that affect the salience, frequency, and perceived legitimacy of punishment or reward may interact with these underlying mechanisms, but the paper does not provide quantitative or direct evidence for such effects.

It reviews evidence and theory suggesting that, in both small- and large-scale societies, humans are generally sensitive to material payoffs in social dilemmas, and that institutions such as monitoring, sanctioning, and reputation systems evolve to support cooperation. The paper highlights the importance of distinguishing between cooperative (payoff-increasing for all) and altruistic (payoff-decreasing for the actor) prosocial behaviors, and suggests that in large-scale societies, cooperation among non-relatives is typically cooperative, not altruistic, due to institutional design.

- source: André, J. B., & Morin, O. (2011). Questioning the cultural evolution of altruism. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.

  findings: The paper finds that models of cultural evolution can, in principle, allow for the spread of biologically altruistic behaviors (including costly punishment) among nonkin, but only because of maladaptive features of social learning such as conformist or payoff-biased imitation. These mechanisms do not change the fundamental selective pressures on altruism; rather, they introduce constraints or errors that allow maladaptive behaviors to persist. The paper critiques the literature for sometimes conflating mutualistic cooperation (which can be adaptive) with genuine altruism (which is maladaptive by definition), and for overstating the explanatory power of cultural group selection. The authors argue that the prevalence of genuine altruism in human societies is likely limited and always reflects some form of maladaptation, whether cognitive or cultural.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence relevant to predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games or their variants. It does, however, offer theoretical caution: the presence of punishment or helping behaviors in cultural evolutionary models does not guarantee group-level efficiency or welfare gains, as these behaviors may be maladaptive and persist only due to constraints in learning or imitation. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the mere presence of punishment mechanisms (especially when costly) does not ensure improved efficiency, and that claims about the benefits of punishment should be scrutinized for underlying assumptions about adaptation and learning. However, the paper does not analyze or report efficiency, payoff, or welfare outcomes, so its direct value for quantitative prediction is limited.

- source: Bravo, G., & Tamburino, L. (2008). The evolution of trust in non-simultaneous exchange situations. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The simulations show that in the Trust Game, average efficiency (measured as average payoff per interaction) is low when agents lack information about others' past behavior or when reputation effects are weak. Introducing strong reputation effects or increasing the spread of information about past behavior leads to a sharp increase in efficiency, with the system moving from a low-efficiency equilibrium (average payoff near 1) to a high-efficiency equilibrium (average payoff above 2, up to ~2.6 out of a maximum of 3). Direct reciprocity (repeated interaction with the same partner) can also increase efficiency, especially when information is incomplete. The transition between low and high efficiency is abrupt, with thin thresholds in the relevant parameters. The model does not include explicit costly punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; instead, 'punishment' is implemented as the refusal to trust low-reputation agents, which is individually rational and costless in this setting.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in Trust Game-like environments, the availability and reliability of information about others' past behavior (i.e., reputation mechanisms) are critical moderators of the effect of indirect reciprocity on efficiency. When reputation effects are strong and information is widely available, efficiency increases sharply. Direct reciprocity (repeated partners) can also boost efficiency, especially when information is incomplete. However, the model does not include explicit costly punishment or sanctioning mechanisms as in public goods games with punishment. Therefore, while the findings are highly informative about the role of information, reputation, and reciprocity in driving efficiency, they are only weakly informative about the effects of costly punishment per se. For prediction tasks focused on punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled efficiency, this paper is most useful as a source of indirect evidence about the importance of information and reputation, rather than direct evidence about punishment.

  findings: The paper finds that costly signaling of cooperative intent can evolve as an honest signal when the long-term benefits of repeated cooperation outweigh the costs of signaling. In such environments, agents who signal are more likely to be chosen as partners, and those who are chosen are more likely to cooperate, leading to higher payoffs for both signalers and choosers. The model predicts that efficiency (total payoff) is maximized when signaling costs are moderate and the number of repeated interactions is high. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning, and is based on dyadic rather than group interactions, but the findings are relevant for understanding how reputation mechanisms can support efficiency in social dilemmas.

While not a public goods game per se, the model is closely related and suggests that, in environments where reputation-building is possible and partner choice is allowed, efficiency will be higher than in environments without these mechanisms. The absence of punishment in the model means it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does highlight the importance of costly signaling and partner choice as alternative or complementary mechanisms. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling reputation-building and partner choice (analogous to some forms of communication or signaling in PGGs) can increase efficiency, especially when the cost of signaling is not prohibitive and the benefits of cooperation are high.

  findings: The study finds that allowing for fraternal (other-regarding) player types in spatial social dilemmas leads to the coexistence of egoist and fraternal strategies, often in spatially ordered patterns. The presence of fraternal players reduces the parameter region where only defectors survive, thus supporting higher levels of cooperation and higher average payoffs. The model demonstrates that spatial structure and personality diversity can promote cooperation and mitigate the tragedy of the commons, even without explicit punishment or reward mechanisms.

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial voluntary prisoner's dilemma game with emotional profile imitation, the average payoff (efficiency) is high when the temptation to defect (T) is low (T=1.0, average payoff 0.92, cooperation rate ~95%), and low when T is high (T=1.25, average payoff 0.3, cooperation rate ~1%). The system exhibits order-chaos-order transitions as T increases, with bifurcations in strategy and emotional profile distributions. The presence of loners and the structure of emotional imitation modulate the system's efficiency, but no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. The findings are relevant for understanding efficiency in voluntary participation social dilemmas, but not for predicting the effect of punishment.

- source: Kurokawa, S. (2016). Imperfect information facilitates the evolution of reciprocity. *Mathematical Biosciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of imperfect information on the evolution of reciprocity (and thus on group efficiency/payoff) depends on the structure of payoffs. When payoffs are linear, imperfect information always makes reciprocity (cooperation) less likely to evolve, reducing group efficiency. However, when payoffs are nonlinear (synergetic effects), imperfect information can sometimes make reciprocity more likely to evolve, increasing group efficiency. The analysis is theoretical, based on evolutionary stability conditions for tit-for-tat (TFT) versus always-defect (ALLD) strategies in the iterated prisoner's dilemma with mistakes and imperfect information. The results do not generalize directly to public goods games or to settings with punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how information structure and payoff nonlinearity can affect the evolution of cooperation and group efficiency in repeated dyadic games. However, it does not address public goods games, punishment, or sanctions, and thus is only indirectly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The main lesson is that the effect of information and error structure on efficiency can be non-monotonic and depend on payoff nonlinearity, but this does not directly inform the effect of punishment in multi-player public goods games.

- source: Hadzibeganovic, T., Cui, P. B., & Wu, Z. X. (2019). Nonconformity of cooperators promotes the emergence of pure altruism in tag-based multi-agent networked systems. *Knowledge-based Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in tag-based multi-agent systems, the combination of spatial structure (small-world networks) and the presence of nonconforming (free) altruists, together with conformity-biased transmission of other strategies, is necessary for the stable dominance of pure altruism (unconditional cooperation). Conformity alone, or spatial structure alone, is insufficient to produce this outcome. The model shows that even a small fraction (>1%) of nonconforming altruists can drive the system toward near-complete altruism, resulting in higher mean fitness and group payoff. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are robust across a range of network parameters and conversion sensitivities. The results are supported by both agent-based simulations and mathematical analysis.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it is highly informative about the role of conformity, spatial structure, and unconditional cooperation in evolutionary games with tag-based interactions. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly applicable, but it does provide a strong theoretical and computational baseline for understanding how other mechanisms (conformity, spatial structure, nonconformity) can promote efficiency and cooperation in the absence of punishment. If a model or prediction task involves tag-based cooperation, conformity, or spatial structure, the findings here suggest that these factors can be powerful moderators of efficiency, but the effect of punishment would need to be assessed from other sources.

- source: Molleman, L., Pen, I., & Weissing, F. J. (2013). Effects of Conformism on the Cultural Evolution of Social Behaviour. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of conformism on the evolution of cooperation depends on the game structure. In social dilemmas, conformism can make cooperation a stable equilibrium if it is already common, but in group-structured populations, conformism hinders the spread of cooperation because it prevents rare cooperative strategies from invading. In evasion games, conformism erodes polymorphic equilibria and leads to fixation of a single strategy. The main outcomes are fixation probabilities and average payoffs at equilibrium, which are related to efficiency. The paper does not study punishment, sanctions, or reward mechanisms, and does not directly model public goods games, but the results are relevant for understanding how social learning biases affect group-level cooperation and welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or reward mechanisms. However, it does provide theoretical insight into how social learning rules (payoff-based vs. conformist) affect the spread and stability of cooperation and group payoffs in two-strategy games, including social dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only indirectly relevant: it suggests that mechanisms which stabilize cooperation (such as conformism) may not always increase group efficiency, especially in group-structured populations. However, since punishment is not modeled, and the game structure is not a PGG or its direct variant, this paper should be considered only for background or mechanistic context, not for quantitative prediction.

  findings: The study finds that introducing aspiration-based updating into a population of imitators generally reduces cooperation, especially when mutation is present.
